The Poincaré Problem for a foliated surface Thanks: This work is supported by Shanghai Pilot Program for Basic Research, National Natural Science Foundation of China, Fundamental Research Funds for central Universities, and Science and Technology Commission of Shanghai Municipality (No. 22DZ2229014)
Xin Lü, Sheng-Li Tan

Abstract
. Let ℱ \mathcal{F} be a foliation on a smooth projective surface S S over the complex number ℂ \mathbb{C} . We introduce three birational non-negative invariants c 1 2 ​ ( ℱ ) c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}) , c 2 ​ ( ℱ ) c_{2}(\mathcal{F}) and χ ⁡ ( ℱ ) \chi(\mathcal{F}) , called the Chern numbers. If the foliation ℱ \mathcal{F} is not of general type, the first Chern number c 1 2 ​ ( ℱ ) = 0 c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=0 , and c 2 ​ ( ℱ ) = χ ⁡ ( ℱ ) = 0 c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=\chi(\mathcal{F})=0 except when ℱ \mathcal{F} is induced by a non-isotrivial fibration of genus g = 1 g=1 . If ℱ \mathcal{F} is of general type, we obtain a slope inequality when ℱ \mathcal{F} is algebraically integral. As a corollary, ℱ \mathcal{F} is always transcendental if the slope is less than 2 2 . On the other hand, we also prove three sharp Noether type inequalities if ℱ \mathcal{F} is of general type. As applications, we obtain a criterion for foliations to be transcendental using Noether type inequalities, and we also give a partial positive answer to the question on the lower bound on the volume of a foliation of general type.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14J29; 14D06; 32S65

1.
Introduction

The main purpose of this paper tries to solve the following problem of Poincaré
on the algebraic integrability of a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on a smooth projective surface
S
over the complex number
\mathbb{C}
.

Poincaré Problem
.

Is it possible to decide whether a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on a smooth projective surface
S
is algebraically integral or not?

Historically, Darboux
[
Dar78a
,
Dar78b
]
, Poincaré
[
Poi85
,
Poi91
]
and Painlevé
[
Pai92
]
etc. studied the foliation given by a differential equation
of first order on the affine plane
\mathbb{A}^{2}
,

\dfrac{dy}{dx}=\dfrac{p(x,y)}{q(x,y)},\qquad\text{or equivalently,}\quad\omega:=p(x,y)dx-q(x,y)dy=0.

where
p
and
q
are relatively coprime polynomials. Hilbert
listed this kind of study as
Hilbert’s 16th Problem
in

[
Hil02
]
. One of the central problems is about the above Poincaré problem on the algebraic integrability
of the above differential equation, namely to find criterions so that the above differential
equation has a rational first integral.
If
f(x,y)
is a rational first integral of the above differential equation, then the integral curves defined by

f(x,y)=\text{constant},

give a pencil of algebraic curves.
Because of this, the differential equation is
algebraically integral
if it admits a rational first integral; otherwise we call it
non-algebraic
or
transcendental
.
If
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral, then it gives a pencil of algebraic curves of the genus
g
, in which case we will call
g
the genus of
\mathcal{F}
for convenience.

Painlevé Problem
.

Is it possible to recognize the genus
g
of an algebraically integral foliation
\mathcal{F}
?

Poincaré suggested to study the above two problems on the projective plane
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, or more generally on a smooth projective surface
S
.
By a
foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
we mean a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on a smooth projective surface
S
.
It can be defined by a collection of one-forms

\omega_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{S}(U_{i})
with at most isolated zeros
and there exists
f_{ij}\in\mathcal{O}^{*}_{S}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})
whenever
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
such that

\omega_{i}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}=f_{ij}\omega_{j}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}.

Equivalently, we can say that
\mathcal{F}
is defined by a twisted one form

\omega\in H^{0}\big(S,\Omega_{S}^{1}(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{F}})\big)
,
where
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{F}}
is the normal bundle of
\mathcal{F}
, see
Section 2
for details.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, a theorem of Darboux-Jouanolou-Ghys
[
Dar78a
,
Jou78
,
Ghy00
]
says roughly that if
\mathcal{F}
admits sufficiently many invariant irreducible algebraic curves, then the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral.
This gives a solution to the Poincaré problem in some sense.
As already noticed by Poincaré
[
Poi91
]
, it is difficult to obtain the algorithm of that invariant curves, even for foliations on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
In
[
Per02
]
, Pereira obtained a nice solution to Poincaré’s and Painlevé’s problems by showing that the degree of a first integral for a plane foliation
(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{F})
is bounded from above by the degree and pluri-genera of
\mathcal{F}
.
On the other hand, Lins Neto
[
LN02
]
constructed families of foliations on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, where the analytic type of each singularity is constant but one cannot decide the algebraic integrability.
Furthermore, the genera of algebraically integral foliations in the families are either not
bounded.
This shows in particular that there might be some obstruction to Poincaré’s and Painlevé’s problems, and hence one has to find more invariants
(should be global) in order to solve these two problems.

In the last several decades, the foliation theory has attracted more and more attention in algebraic geometry, especially in birational geometry.
One can even consider foliations on higher dimensional variety, not only on surfaces.
For a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on a normal variety
X
, it is possible to define its canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
.
The canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
of a foliation plays a similar role in many aspects as the canonical divisor
K_{X}
of a variety.
For instance, the criterion on the uniruledness of foliated varieties with non-pseudo-effective canonical divisors by Miyaoka and Campana-Păun,
[
Miy87
,
CP19
]
,
the minimal model theory on foliated surfaces due to Brunella and McQuillan etc.
[
Bru99
,
McQ08
]
,
and the deformation invariance of the pluri-genera by Cascini-Floris
[
CF18
]
and Liu
[
Liu17
]
.
There have been also many works on the adjoint canonical divisors recently;
see for example
[
PS19
,
SS23
,
CHL+24
]
.
In particular, Pereira-Svaldi
[
PS19
]
proved a cohomological characterization of foliations with non-pseudo-effective divisors.
Recently, the minimal model program for foliated varieties was introduced in the beautiful paper
[
Cas21
]
.
It stimulates a new project in birational geometry to generalize everything to foliated varieties.

The algebraic integrability of a foliated surface is clearly a birational property.
Hence it is reasonable to study the Poincaré problem
using the birational invariants of foliated surfaces.
In fact, the interaction of the birational geometry with surface foliations have also made great success in
the Poincaré problem.
The following criterion of Miyaoka
[
Miy87
]
(see also
[
BM16
,
CP19
]
)
solves the Poincaré problem in the case when
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is not pseudo-effective.

Theorem 1.1

(Miyaoka)
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface with canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Suppose that
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is not pseudo-effective. Then
\mathcal{F}
is induced by a family of rational curves. In particular, it is always algebraically integral.

Motivated by the formulas for the modular invariants of families of
algebraic curves (cf.
[
Tan96
,
Tan10
]
),
we will first introduce three new birational invariants
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}),c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
and
\chi(\mathcal{F})
for a foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
.
The three invariants are all non-negative rational numbers satisfying the following properties.

Theorem 1.2

(=
Definition 5.2
+
Theorem 5.3
+
Corollary 5.7
+
Proposition 5.10
+
Theorem 5.14
)
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface.
We can define its Chern numbers
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}),c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
and
\chi(\mathcal{F})
,
which are non-negative rational numbers.

(
i
)

The three Chern numbers are birational invariants.

(
ii
)

The three Chern numbers satisfy the Noether equality:

12\chi(\mathcal{F})=c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})+c_{2}(\mathcal{F}).

(1.1)

(
iii
)

The first Chern number
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})
, where
\vol(\mathcal{F})
is the volume of the foliation
\mathcal{F}
, whose definition is given in
Definition 2.11
.

(
iv
)

Suppose that the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
is induced by a family
f:\,S\to B
of curves. Then

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\kappa(f),\qquad c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=\delta(f),\qquad\chi(\mathcal{F})=\lambda(f),

(1.2)

where
\kappa(f),\,\delta(f)
and
\lambda(f)
are the modular invariants of
f
,
whose definitions will be recalled in
Section 4.1
.

Foliations of non-general type are classified, cf.
[
Bru03
,
Bru04
,
McQ08
]
.
We determine the Chern numbers of foliations of non-general type as follows.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface.
Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is not of general type, i.e., its Kodaira dimension
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})\in\{-\infty,0,1\}
.

(i)

The first Chern number
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

(ii)

The other two Chern numbers
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=\chi(\mathcal{F})=0
except when
\mathcal{F}
is induced by a non-isotrivial fibration of genus
g=1
.

As a direct consequence, we obtain the following criterion on the algebraic integrability of a foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
of non-general type.

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface.
Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is not of general type, and that
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})>0
or
\chi(\mathcal{F})>0
.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral.

Lins Neto’s
[
LN02
]
famous counterexamples with
d=2,3
or
4

are foliations of non-general type with
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=\chi(\mathcal{F})=0
by
[
LLT23
]
.
In particular, we can not determine the algebraic integrability of foliated surfaces with
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=\chi(\mathcal{F})=0
using the Chern numbers.
Combined with the above corollary, the Poincaré problem is completely solved for foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
of non-general type.

We next turn to the case when
\mathcal{F}
is of general type.
In this case,
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})>0
, and hence
\chi(\mathcal{F})>0
due to the Noether equality (
1.1
) and the non-negativity of
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
.
We may thus define the
slope
of
\mathcal{F}
as

\lambda(\mathcal{F}):=\frac{c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})}{\chi(\mathcal{F})}=\frac{\vol(\mathcal{F})}{\chi(\mathcal{F})}.

According to the non-negativity of the Chern numbers and the Noether equality (
1.1
),
it holds that
0<\lambda(\mathcal{F})\leq 12
.
We are interested in a positive lower bound on the slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})
.
Cornalba-Harris-Xiao’s slope inequality
[
CH88
,
Xia87
]
on fibred surface together with (
1.2
) gives a positive lower bound on the slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})
when
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface.
Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is of general type and algebraically integral, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
is induced by a non-isotrivial family
f:\,S\to B
of curves of genus
g\geq 2
. Then

\lambda(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{4(g-1)}{g},\qquad\text{or equivalently,}\qquad c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{4(g-1)}{g}\chi(\mathcal{F}).

(1.3)

As an application, we obtain a partial solution to both Poincaré’s and Painlevé’s problems for foliations of general type with lower slope.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, and suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is of general type.

(
i
)

Suppose that
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<4
and that
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
is induced by a non-isotrivial family
f:\,S\to B
of curves of genus
g\geq 2
. Then

g\leq\frac{4}{4-\lambda(\mathcal{F})}.

(1.4)

(
ii
)

If
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<2
, then
\mathcal{F}
is transcendental.

It is well-known that the bound (
1.4
) is sharp, and the equality can be achieved by semi-stable fibrations for every genus
g\geq 2
.
Moreover, it is not difficult to construct algebraically integral foliations with
\lambda(\mathcal{F})=4
but the genus
g
can be arbitrarily large, cf.
[
Xia87
]
.
On the other hand, we will construct a transcendental foliation of general type (cf.
Example 7.1
) with
\lambda(\mathcal{F})=\frac{12}{7}<2
.
However, the sharp lower bound on the slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})
is still mysterious to us for a transcendental foliation
\mathcal{F}
of general type.
We tend to guess
\lambda(\mathcal{F})\geq 1
, i.e.,
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})\geq\chi(\mathcal{F})
.

Corollary 1.6
shows in particular that the Poincaré problem is solved when the slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<2
for foliations of general type.
Moreover, if
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<4
and
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral, one obtains an upper bound on the genus
g
, i.e., Painlevé’s problem can be also solved.
In fact, by
[
LZ18
]
, any algebraically integral foliation with
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<4
must be induced by a hyperelliptic fibration if
g\geq 16
.
Hence such a foliation has a very special structure: it is a double cover of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.
It is reasonable to wonder whether the Poincaré problem can be solved for foliations with
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<4
?
Namely, besides the slope, can one construct more invariants, so that the algebraic integrability of
\mathcal{F}
can be completely determined if
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<4
?
For instance, if
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<\frac{8}{3}
, then
\mathcal{F}
is either transcendental or algebraically integral of genus
g=2
.
The rich geometry of genus-
2
fibrations (cf.
[
Xia85
]
) might help us to characterize the algebraic integrability of foliations with
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<\frac{8}{3}
.
On the other hand, Lins Neto’s famous counterexamples
[
LN02
]
show that there indeed exist obstructions to determine the algebraic integrability in general.
In fact, it has been shown in
[
LLT23
]
that the foliations of general type constructed by Lins Neto have slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})\geq 6
.
It is natural to ask whether there exist counterexamples to Poincaré’s problem (as well as Painlevé’s problem) with
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<6
.
More precisely, we would like to propose the following question.

Question 1.7
.

What is the critical slope
\lambda_{0}
, such that the algebraic integrability can be determined if
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<\lambda_{0}
, and that there exist counterexamples if
\lambda(\mathcal{F})\geq\lambda_{0}
?

Another approach to Poincaré’s problem for foliations of general type is the Noether type inequalities.
The classical Noether inequality
[
Noe70
]
asserts that

\vol(S)\geq 2p_{g}(S)-4,

(1.5)

for every complex smooth projective surface
S
of general type.
Here we recall that the volume
\vol(S)
and the geometric genus
p_{g}(S)
of
S
are defined as follows.
Let
K_{S}
be the canonical divisor of
S
.
Then

\displaystyle p_{g}(S)

\displaystyle=\dim H^{0}(S,K_{S});

\displaystyle\vol(S)

\displaystyle=\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\dim H^{0}(S,nK_{S})}{n^{2}/2}.

These are two important birational invariants of
S
.
If
S
is minimal, then the volume
\vol(S)
is equal to the intersection number
K_{S}^{2}
,
which is also the first Chern number of
S
.
The Noether inequality (
1.5
) is one of the fundamental inequality in the surface theory.
Minimal surfaces of general type with the equality are usually said on the Noether line, which has been systematically studied by Horikawa
[
Hor76
]
.

In a series of nice works
[
Kob92
,
Che04
,
CCZ06
,
Che07
,
CC15
,
CH17
,
CCJ20b
,
CCJ20a
]
,
the following sharp Noether inequality has been established for every minimal
3
-fold
X
of general type
with
p_{g}(X)\leq 4
or
p_{g}(X)\geq 11
:

\vol(X)\geq\frac{4}{3}\,p_{g}(X)-\frac{10}{3}.

It is proved in
[
CJ17
]
that the Noether type inequality holds also in higher dimension:
there exist positive numbers
a_{d}
and
b_{d}
, depending only on the dimension
d
of the variety
X
,
such that

\vol(X)\geq a_{d}\,p_{g}(X)-b_{d}.

Inspired by the Noether type inequalities for algebraic varieties,
it is natural to ask the following question.

Question 1.8
.

Do there exist Noether type inequalities for a foliation of general type?

We will settle the above question in dimension two.

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, and suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is of general type.
Let
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
be its geometric genus, and
\varphi
be the canonical map as in
Section 2.3
.

(i)

The following Noether inequality holds

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2.

(1.6)

Moreover, if the equality holds in (
1.6
), then the canonical map
\varphi
defines a birational map whose image is a surface of minimal degree
(
equal to
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2)
in
\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\neq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2
with
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})>0
. Then

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2+\frac{1}{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})}.

(1.7)

Moreover, if the equality holds in (
1.7
), then the image
\Sigma=\varphi(S)
is of one-dimensional.

(iii)

Suppose moreover that the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral. Then

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{3}{2\big(2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1\big)}.

(1.8)

Moreover, if the equality holds in (
1.8
), then the image
\Sigma=\varphi(S)
is of one-dimensional.

Applying the third Noether type inequality (
1.8
) to the Poincaré problem, we get

Corollary 1.10
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, and suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is of general type.
If

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})<p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{3}{2\big(2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1\big)},

then
\mathcal{F}
is transcendental.

Based on the first two Noether type inequalities (
1.6
) and (
1.7
),
we can also give a partial answer to the question
on a positive lower bound on the volume of a foliation of general type,
cf.
[
Cas21
, § 3]
and
[
HL21
, Question 4]
.

Corollary 1.11
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface.
Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is of general type with
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})\geq 2
. Then

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Remark 1.12
.

(i)  We will construct examples in
Section 9

reaching the equalities in (
1.6
), (
1.7
) and (
1.8
),
which show that all the three Noether type inequalities in
Theorem 1.9
are sharp.
In particular, taking
n=2
in
Example 9.2
, one obtains
an example reaching the equality in
Corollary 1.11
.

(ii)  The inequality (
1.7
) will be referred as the second Noether inequality for foliations of general type.
This is slightly different from the second Noether inequality for
algebraic varieties
X
of general type.
When
X
is of dimension two, the volume is an integer, and hence

\vol(X)\geq 2p_{g}(X)-3=(2p_{g}(X)-4)+1,\qquad\text{if $\vol(X)\neq 2p_{g}(X)-4$.}

When
X
is a threefold, Hu-Zhang
[
HZ22
]
proved that if
p_{g}(X)\not\in[5,10]
and
\vol(X)\neq\frac{4}{3}p_{g}(X)-\frac{10}{3}
, then

\vol(X)\geq\frac{4}{3}p_{g}(X)-\frac{19}{6}=\Big(\frac{4}{3}p_{g}(X)-\frac{10}{3}\Big)+\frac{1}{6}.

Such a phenomenon is different for foliations of general type.
Indeed, we will construct in
Example 9.2
a sequence of foliated surfaces
(S_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
reaching the equality in (
1.7
); namely

\vol(\mathcal{F}_{n})>p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})-2
, but the difference
\vol(\mathcal{F}_{n})-\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})-2\big)
can be arbitrarily close to zero.

Both the slope inequalities and Noether type inequalities can give lower bounds on the volume
\vol(\mathcal{F})
in terms of the Chern number
\chi(\mathcal{F})
and the geometric genus
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
respectively.
It is natural to ask the relation between the two birational invariants
\chi(\mathcal{F})
and
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
.
Combining the Noether inequality (
1.6
) together with (
1.1
) and the non-negativity of
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
, one obtains immediately the following.

Corollary 1.13
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, and suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is of general type.
Then

p_{g}(\mathcal{F})\leq 12\chi(\mathcal{F})+2.

(1.9)

Conversely, the Chern number
\chi(\mathcal{F})
(as well as the other two Chern numbers
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})
and
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
) can not be bounded from above by the geometric genus
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
in general; see
Example 9.5
.

The paper is organized as follows.
In
Section 2
, we review some basic facts about foliated surfaces.
In
Section 3
, we recall the Zariski decomposition of the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
for a relatively minimal foliation, and prove in
Proposition 3.6
an upper bound on the coefficients
a_{C}
’s of the negative part
N=\sum a_{C}C
.
In
Section 4
, we restrict ourselves to the algebraically integral foliations, namely foliations with rational first integrals.
In particular, we will present the resolution of non-reduced singularities on an algebraically integral foliation, which will be helpful in obtaining the Noether inequalities for an algebraically integral foliation.
In
Section 5
, we will introduce the Chern numbers of foliations with pseudo-effective canonical divisors, and prove some basic properties of these Chern numbers.
In particular,
Theorem 1.2
will be proved in this section.
In
Section 6
, we compute the Chern numbers of foliations which are not of general type, and prove
Theorem 1.3
and
Corollary 1.4
.
In
Section 7
, we consider the slope of foliations of general type.
In particular, we will prove
Theorem 1.5
and
Corollary 1.6
.
We also construct an example of transcendental foliation of general type with slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})=\frac{12}{7}<2
in this section.
In
Section 8
, we prove the three Noether type inequalities in
Theorem 1.9
; while both
Corollary 1.10
and
Corollary 1.11

follow directly from the Noether type inequalities.
Finally, we will construct in
Section 9
several examples reaching the equalities in the three Noether type inequalities in
Theorem 1.9
,
which illustrate the sharpness of those inequalities.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall some basic facts about the foliations on smooth surfaces. For more details we refer to
[
Bru04
,
McQ08
]
.

2.1.
Basic definitions

We work over the complex number
\mathbb{C}
.
By a
foliated surface

(S,\mathcal{F})
we mean a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on the smooth projective surface
S
over the complex number
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A foliation
\mathcal{F}
on a smooth projective surface
S
over the complex number
\mathbb{C}
is defined by a saturated invertible subsheaf
T_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq T_{S}
in the tangent sheaf
T_{S}
,
i.e.,
T_{S}/T_{\mathcal{F}}
is torsion free.

Equivalently, a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
S
can be given by an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow T_{\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow T_{S}\longrightarrow I_{\Delta}(N_{\mathcal{F}})\longrightarrow 0,

where
T_{\mathcal{F}}
and
N_{\mathcal{F}}
are respectively called the
tangent bundle
and
normal bundle
of
\mathcal{F}
,
and
I_{\Delta}
is an ideal sheaf.
In other words,
a foliation on
S
is given by the data
\{(U_{i},v_{i})\}_{i\in I}
,
where
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is an open covering of
S
,

v_{i}
is a holomorphic vector field on
U_{i}
with at most isolated zeros,
and there exists
g_{ij}\in\mathcal{O}^{*}_{S}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})
whenever
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
such that

v_{i}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}=g_{ij}v_{j}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}.

(2.1)

The cocycle
\{g_{ij}\}
defines a line bundle which is nothing but the dual of the tangent bundle
T_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}
.
The bundle
T_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}
will be called the
canonical divisor
of
\mathcal{F}
, and we denote it by
K_{\mathcal{F}}:=T_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}
.

Alternatively, one can also define
\mathcal{F}
using one-forms instead of vector fields, as studied by Poincaré and Painlevé etc. over one hundred years ago.
A foliation on
S
is given by a collection of one-forms

\omega_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{S}(U_{i})
with at most isolated zeros
and there exists
f_{ij}\in\mathcal{O}^{*}_{S}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})
whenever
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
such that

\omega_{i}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}=f_{ij}\omega_{j}|_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}.

(2.2)

The cocycle
\{f_{ij}\}
defines a line bundle which is the conormal bundle
N_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}
.
One can also translate this into an exact sequence:

0\longrightarrow N_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}\longrightarrow\Omega_{S}^{1}\longrightarrow I_{\Delta}(K_{\mathcal{F}})\longrightarrow 0.

We remark that a foliation is classically defined by a
nonzero global meromorphic 1-form
\alpha
on
S
.
The one-dimensional zeros and poles of
\alpha
define a divisor

\textrm{div}(\alpha)=(\alpha)_{0}-(\alpha)_{\infty}\,.

Let
s
be the canonical nonzero meromorphic section of
\mathcal{O}_{S}(\textrm{div}(\alpha))
such that

\textrm{div}(s)=\textrm{div}(\alpha)
, and let
\alpha=s\cdot\omega
. Then
\omega
is a twisted holomorphic 1-form
\omega
in

\Omega^{1}_{S}(-\textrm{div}(\alpha))
with isolated zeros,
which coincides with the above definitions satisfying
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}=\mathcal{O}_{S}\big(\text{\rm{div\,}}(\alpha)\big)
.
For any surjective morphism
\Pi:\,\widetilde{S}\to S
from another smooth projective surface
\widetilde{S}
,
there is an induced foliation
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\Pi^{*}(\mathcal{F})
defined by

\Pi^{*}(\alpha)
if
\alpha
is a nonzero global meromorphic
1
-form defining
\mathcal{F}
.

An irreducible curve
C\subseteq S
is said to be
\mathcal{F}
-invariant
if the inclusion

T_{\mathcal{F}}|_{C}\hookrightarrow T_{X}|_{C}
factors through
T_{C}
.
By a curve
C\subseteq S
we mean a reduced and compact algebraic curve.
So it might be singular and reducible.
Suppose that
C
is not
\mathcal{F}
-invariant, or more precisely every irreducible component of
C
is not
\mathcal{F}
-invariant.
Then one defines the tangency of
\mathcal{F}
to
C
as follows.
Let
p\in C
be any point.
Around
p
, let
\{f=0\}
be a local equation of
C
,
and
v
be a local holomorphic vector field defining
\mathcal{F}
around
p
.
Then the tangency of
\mathcal{F}
to
C
at
p
is defined to be

\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F},C,p)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathcal{O}_{S,p}}{\langle f,v(f)\rangle}.

As
C
is not
\mathcal{F}
-invariant,
\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F},C,p)<+\infty
and
\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F},C,p)=0
except for finitely many points.
Hence one defines the tangency of
\mathcal{F}
to
C
.

\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F},C)=\sum_{p\in C}\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F},C,p).

Proposition 2.2

(
[
Bru04
, Proposition 2.2]
)
.

Let
C
be a curve on
S
which is not
\mathcal{F}
-invariant. Then

\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F},C)=K_{\mathcal{F}}C+C^{2}.

In particular,

K_{\mathcal{F}}C+C^{2}\geq 0.

We now suppose that
C
is
\mathcal{F}
-invariant, or more precisely every irreducible component of
C
is
\mathcal{F}
-invariant.
Given any point
p\in C
, let
\{f=0\}
be a local equation of
C
,
and
\omega
be a local holomorphic one-form defining
\mathcal{F}
around
p
.
Because
C
is
\mathcal{F}
-invariant, we may write

g\omega=hdf+f\eta,

for some holomorphic one-form
\eta
and holomorphic functions
g,h
around
p
,
such that
h
and
f
are coprime.
We define

\displaystyle Z(\mathcal{F},C,p)

\displaystyle=\text{vanishing order of $~\frac{h}{g}\Big|_{C}$ at $p$},

\displaystyle\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C,p)

\displaystyle=\text{residue of $~-\frac{\eta}{h}\Big|_{C}$ at $p$}.

By definition, both
Z(\mathcal{F},C,p)
and
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C,p)
are zero if
p
is not a singular point of
\mathcal{F}
.
If
\mathcal{F}
is reduced, then
Z(\mathcal{F},C,p)\geq 0
for any
p\in C

[
Bru97
]
.
Let

\displaystyle Z(\mathcal{F},C)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in C}Z(\mathcal{F},C,p)=\sum_{p\in C\,\cap\,\text{Sing}(\mathcal{F})}Z(\mathcal{F},C,p),

\displaystyle\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in C}\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C,p)=\sum_{p\in C\,\cap\,\text{Sing}(\mathcal{F})}\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C,p).

Proposition 2.3

(
[
Bru04
, Proposition 2.2 and Theorem 3.2]
)
.

Let
C
be a curve on
S
which is
\mathcal{F}
-invariant. Then

\displaystyle Z(\mathcal{F},C)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{\mathcal{F}}C+\chi(C),\quad\text{where~}\chi(C)=-K_{S}C-C^{2};

\displaystyle\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C^{2}.

(2.3)

The second equality above is called the Camacho-Sad formula.
The first equality implies in particular that if
\mathcal{F}
is reduced and
C
is an
\mathcal{F}
-invariant curve, then

0\leq K_{\mathcal{F}}C+\chi(C)=K_{\mathcal{F}}C-K_{S}C-C^{2}=N_{\mathcal{F}}C-C^{2}.

2.2.
The singularities of a foliation

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface.
The singular locus of
\mathcal{F}
is the set of points
p\in S

where the quotient sheaf
T_{S}/T_{\mathcal{F}}

fails to be locally free at
p
.
The torsion-freeness of
T_{S}/T_{\mathcal{F}}
implies that the codimension of the singular locus of
\mathcal{F}
in
S
is at least two,
i.e., it consists of finitely many points.
Let
p
be a singular point of
\mathcal{F}
,
and
v=A\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+B\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
is a local vector field defining
\mathcal{F}
.
One defines the
multiplicity
of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
as

m_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathcal{O}_{p}}{\langle A,B\rangle}.

The total number of singular points of
\mathcal{F}
is (cf.
[
Bru04
, Proposition 2.1]
):

m(\mathcal{F}):=\sum_{p}m_{p}(\mathcal{F})=c_{2}(\Omega_{S}(N_{\mathcal{F}}))=c_{2}(S)+N_{\mathcal{F}}K_{\mathcal{F}}.

(2.4)

The two eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of the linear part
(Dv)(p)

at a singular point of
\mathcal{F}

are well-defined up to multiplication by a non-zero constant.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
p\in S
be a singularity of
\mathcal{F}
and
v
be a local vector field defining
\mathcal{F}
.

(i)

The singularity
p
is called non-degenerate if both of the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}
of
(Dv)(p)
are non-zero;
it is called a saddle-node if exactly one the two eigenvalues

\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}
is non-zero.

(ii)

The singularity
p
is called a
reduced singularity
if at least one of the two eigenvalues (say,
\lambda_{2}
) is not zero and the quotient
\lambda=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}
is not a positive rational number. In particular, a reduced singularity is either non-degenerate or a saddle-node.

(iii)

The foliation
\mathcal{F}
is said to be reduced if any singularity of
\mathcal{F}
is reduced.

If
\lambda_{2}\neq 0
, then the quotient
\lambda=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}
is unchanged by multiplication of
v
by a non-vanishing holomorphic function.
Of course, if
\lambda_{1}\neq 0
, we could also consider the quotient

\lambda^{-1}=\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}
instead of
\lambda
,
but then
\lambda\not\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
iff
\lambda^{-1}\not\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
.
The complex number
\lambda=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}
,
with an inessential abuse due to the exchange
\lambda\leftrightarrow\lambda^{-1}
,
is called the
eigenvalue
of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
following
[
Bru04
]
.
For convenience, we always assume the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}
at a saddle-node is
0
.
Given any foliation, one can obtain a reduced one by a sequence of blowing-ups, cf.
[
Sei68
]
or
[
Bru04
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem 2.5

(Seidenberg)
.

Given any foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
, there exists a sequence of blowing-ups
\pi:\,\widetilde{S}\to S
,
such that the induced foliation
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
on
\widetilde{S}
is reduced.

We want to introduce two new invariants for a singularity of
\mathcal{F}
.
First, for a complex number
u\in\mathbb{C}
, we define

\left\{\begin{aligned} \beta(u)&\,=\begin{cases}\frac{\gcd(a,b)^{2}}{ab},&\quad\textrm{~if~}u=\dfrac{a}{b}\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\},\\
0,&\quad\textrm{~otherwise}.\end{cases}\\[2.0pt]
\chi(u)&\,=\dfrac{1}{12}\left(u+\dfrac{1}{u}+\beta(u)-3\right),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{~if~}u\neq 0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.5)

We can check easily that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\beta(1)=1,\qquad\beta(-1)=-1;\\
&\beta(u^{-1})=\beta(u),\quad\forall\,u\neq 0.\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\qquad\left\{\begin{aligned} &\chi(1)=0,\qquad\chi(-1)=-1/2;\\
&\chi(u^{-1})=\chi(u),\quad\forall\,u\neq 0.\end{aligned}\right.

Moreover, if
u\neq 0,1
, then

\beta(u)=\beta(u+1)+\beta(u^{-1}+1),\qquad\chi(u)=\chi(u+1)+\chi(u^{-1}+1).

(2.6)

Definition 2.6
.

Let
p\in S
be either a non-degenerate singularity, or a saddle node of
\mathcal{F}
.
Let
\lambda_{p}
be the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
.
Define

\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\beta(-\lambda_{p}),\qquad\qquad\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F})=-\frac{1}{12}\big(BB_{p}(\mathcal{F})+m_{p}(\mathcal{F})-\beta(-\lambda_{p})\big),

(2.7)

where
BB_{p}(\mathcal{F})
is the Baum-Bott index of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
,
and
m_{p}(\mathcal{F})
is the multiplicity of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
.

With the help of the equalities above,
one checks easily that these two invariants are well-defined
(invariant under the exchange
\lambda\leftrightarrow\lambda^{-1}
).
Moreover, if
p
is a non-degenerate singularity with eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}
,
then
m_{p}(\mathcal{F})=1
and

BB_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\lambda_{p}+\lambda_{p}^{-1}+2
, cf.
[
Bru04
, § 3]
. Thus we have

\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\chi(-\lambda_{p}),\qquad\text{if $p$ is a non-degenerate singularity}.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\sigma:\,S^{\prime}\to S
be a blowing-up centered at some point
p\in S
with exceptional curve
E
,
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{F})
the induced foliation on
S^{\prime}
.

(i)

If
p
is a regular point, then
E
is
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant and there is a unique singularity
q_{1}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
E
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{q_{1}}=-1
.
In particular,

\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=1,\qquad\chi_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=0.

(ii)

If
p
is a non-degenerate singularity with eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}\neq 1
,
then
E
is
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant, and there are two non-degenerate singularities
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
E
.
Moreover the eigenvalues of these two singularities are
\{\lambda_{q_{1}},\lambda_{q_{2}}\}=\{\lambda_{p}-1,\lambda_{p}^{-1}-1\}
.
In particular,

\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+\beta_{q_{2}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),\qquad\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\chi_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+\chi_{q_{2}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}).

(2.8)

(iii)

If
p
is a non-degenerate singularity with eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}=1
(in particular,
p
is a non-reduced singularity),
then
E
can be
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant or not:

(iii
-
1)

if
E
is
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant, then there is a unique singularity
q_{1}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
E
which is a saddle-node of multiplicity
m_{q_{1}}=2
and

\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})-1,\qquad\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\chi_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})-\frac{1}{12};

(2.9)

(iii
-
2)

if
E
is not
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant, then there is no singularity of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
E
.

(iv)

If
p
is a saddle-node of multiplicity
m_{p}(\mathcal{F})
, then
E
is
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant, and there are two singularities
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
E
.
Moreover, one of these two singularities, say
q_{1}
, is a saddle-node of multiplicity
m_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=m_{p}(\mathcal{F})
,
and the other one is a non-degenerate singularity with eigenvalue
\lambda_{q_{2}}=-1
. In particular,

\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+\beta_{q_{2}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})-1,\qquad\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\chi_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+\chi_{q_{2}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})-\frac{1}{12}.

(2.10)

Proof.

The first statement is clear.
We consider next
p
is a non-degenerate singularity with eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}\neq 1
.
Then the local one-form
\omega
defining
\mathcal{F}
has the form

\omega=x\big(1+o(1)\big)dy-\lambda_{p}y\big(1+o(1)\big)dx.

Using local equations defining the blowing-up
\sigma
:

(x,t)\mapsto(x,y)=(x,xt),\qquad\text{and}\qquad(s,y)\mapsto(x,y)=(sy,y),

one shows directly that the exceptional curve
E
is
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant and
there are two non-degenerate singularities
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
E

with eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{q_{1}},\lambda_{q_{2}}\}=\{\lambda_{p}-1,\lambda_{p}^{-1}-1\}
.
The equalities in (
2.8
) follows from (
2.6
).

If
p
is a non-degenerate singularity with eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}=1
,
then by Poincaré-Dulac Normal Form Theorem (cf. the end of Chapter 1 in
[
Bru04
]
),
\mathcal{F}
is locally defined by a one-form

\omega=(\epsilon x+y)dx-xdy,\qquad\text{where~}\epsilon\in\{0,1\}.

If
\epsilon=0
(resp.
\epsilon=1
), then again by a direct computation one shows that

E
is not
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant and that there is no singularity of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
E

(resp. that
E
is
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-invariant and that there is a unique singularity
q_{1}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
E
which is a saddle-node of multiplicity
m_{q_{1}}=2
).
Moreover,
\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F})=-1
,
\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=0
,

BB_{p}(\mathcal{F})=4
, and
BB_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=BB_{p}(\mathcal{F})-1=3
according to the Baum-Bott formula (cf.
[
Bru04
, Theorem 3.1]
).
Hence

\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F})=-\frac{1}{2},\qquad\chi_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=-\frac{5}{12}.

This proves (
2.9
).

Finally, we consider the case when

p
is a saddle-node of multiplicity
m_{p}(\mathcal{F})
.
In this case, the blowing-up process is exhibited in
[
Bru04
, § 1.2]
,
and the conclusions follow from a direct computation as before.
We just remark
the formula
BB_{p}(\mathcal{F})=BB_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+BB_{q_{2}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+1
,
which can deduced from Baum-Bott formula (cf.
[
Bru04
, Theorem 3.1]
).
This completes the proof.
∎

In the proof of the above lemma, we have seen that the blowing-up of a non-degenerate singularity produces non-degenerate singularities or saddle-nodes.
By a sequence of blowing-ups, one can resolve a non-degenerate non-reduced singularity
p
.
Such a process can be found at the end of Chapter 1 in
[
Bru04
]
.
It depends on the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}
, which is a positive rational number by assumption.
For convenience, we briefly recall it in the following.

(i). If
\lambda_{p}\not\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\cup\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}^{+}}
,
then
\mathcal{F}
can be generated by a local vector field

v=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\lambda_{p}y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
,
where
\lambda_{p}=\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
with
\gcd(m_{1},m_{2})=1
.
Equivalently,
\mathcal{F}
is given by the levels of the meromorphic function
\frac{x^{m_{1}}}{y^{m_{2}}}
.
In particular, the local separatrices passing through
p
are defined by

\frac{x^{m_{1}}}{y^{m_{2}}}=c
or
x^{m_{1}}-cy^{m_{2}}=0
, where
c\in\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.
There are exactly two separatrices
\{C_{1},C_{1}^{\prime}\}
smooth at
p
,
defined respectively by
x=0
and
y=0
.
Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S
be the blowing-up centered at
p
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
.
Then
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F})
-invariant, and there are exactly two singularities on
E_{1}
, which are
q_{1}=\overline{C}_{1}\cap E_{1}
and
q_{1}^{\prime}=\overline{C}_{1}^{\prime}\cap E_{1}
, where
\overline{C}_{1}
(resp.
\overline{C}_{1}^{\prime}
) is the strict transform of
C_{1}
(resp.
C_{1}
).
The eigenvalue of
\{\lambda_{q_{1}},\lambda_{q_{1}^{\prime}}\}=\{\lambda_{p}-1,\lambda_{p}^{-1}-1\}
.
It follows that one singularity, say
q_{1}
, is reduced; and the other singularity
q_{1}^{\prime}
is still non-reduced with positive rational eigenvalue.
Let
\sigma_{2}:\,S_{2}\to S_{1}
be the blowing-up centered at
q_{1}^{\prime}
and continue the above process. Finally, one obtains a birational map

\tilde{\pi}:\,\widetilde{S}\to S
, such that there is only reduced singularities
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\tilde{\pi}^{*}(\mathcal{F})
on
\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(p)
. Moreover, all the exceptional curves except the last one
E_{k}
are
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
-invariant,
there is no singularity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
on the last exceptional curve
E_{n}
, and

\displaystyle K_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}})-E_{n},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(p)}\beta_{q}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})+1.

(2.12)

The second equality which follows from
Lemma 2.7
.
Indeed, by (
2.8
), one proves inductively that

\displaystyle\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})

\displaystyle=\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})+\beta_{q_{1}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})

\displaystyle=\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})+\beta_{q_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})+\beta_{q_{2}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})=\cdots

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\beta_{q_{i}}(\mathcal{F}_{n-1})+\beta_{q_{n-1}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{n-1})=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\beta_{q_{i}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})-1,

where we view
q_{i}
also as a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{j}
for
j>i
with the same
\beta
-invariants.
These points
\{q_{1},\cdots,q_{n-1}\}
are all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
lying on
\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(p)
.

(ii). If
\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\cup\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}^{+}}
,
then
\mathcal{F}
can be generated by a local vector field

v=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+(my+\epsilon x^{m})\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
,
where
m=\lambda_{p}
or
\lambda_{p}^{-1}
belongs to
\mathbb{N}^{+}
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
.
If
\epsilon=0
, then the situation is almost the same as the previous case
(the only difference is that there might be infinitely many separatrices smooth at
p
, and we donot know exactly the position of the two singularities on
E_{1}
), and we are done by a similar argument.
All the exceptional curves except the last one
E_{k}
are
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
-invariant,
and (
2.11
) still holds in this case.
If
\epsilon=1
, then as exhibited at the end of Chapter 1 in
[
Bru04
]
,
the resolution
\tilde{\pi}:\,\widetilde{S}\to S
of the non-reduced singularity
p
would create a saddle-node of multiplicity two at the last step.
In this case, all the exceptional curves are
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
-invariant, and it holds

K_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\tilde{\pi}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}}).

(2.13)

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface,
C_{1},C_{2}
be two
\mathcal{F}
-invariant curves, and
p\in C_{1}\cap C_{2}
be a non-degenerate singularity of
\mathcal{F}

with eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}
.
Suppose that both
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are smooth at
p
and intersect transversely at
p
.
Then module the exchange
\lambda_{p}\leftrightarrow\lambda_{p}^{-1}
,
it holds that

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C_{1},p)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{p}},\qquad\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C_{2},p)=\lambda_{p}.

(2.14)

Proof.

If
p
is reduced singularity, it is actually proved in
[
Bru04
, § 3.2]
.
We briefly recall the proof.
The foliation
\mathcal{F}
can be locally defined by a one-form

\omega=x(1+o(1))dy-\lambda_{p}y(1+o(1))dx,\qquad\text{with~}\lambda_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}.

The invariant
\lambda_{p}
is nothing but the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
.
There are exactly two separatrices through
p
, defined respectively by
\{x=0\}
and
\{y=0\}
, which are nothing but the two
\mathcal{F}
-invariant curves

C_{1}
and
C_{2}
by our assumption.
Recall that a separatrix of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
is a local holomorphic irreducible curve
C
(possibly singular) on a neighborhood of
p
which is
\mathcal{F}
-invariant and passes through
p
.
By a direct computation, one proves (
2.14
).

Suppose now that
p
is a non-reduced singularity, i.e., the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
.
If
\lambda_{p}\not\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\cup\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}^{+}}
,
then as recalled above,
\mathcal{F}
can be generated by a local vector field

v=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\lambda_{p}y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
,
where
\lambda_{p}=\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
with
\gcd(m_{1},m_{2})=1
.
It follows that the local separatrices passing through
p
are defined by

\frac{x^{m_{1}}}{y^{m_{2}}}=c
or
x^{m_{1}}-cy^{m_{2}}=0
, where
c\in\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.
There are exactly two separatrices smooth at
p
,
defined respectively by
x=0
and
y=0
,
which are nothing but the two
\mathcal{F}
-invariant curves

C_{1}
and
C_{2}
by our assumption.
Again (
2.14
) follows by a direct computation.
If
\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\cup\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}^{+}}
,
then
\mathcal{F}
can be generated by a local vector field

v=x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+(my+\epsilon x^{m})\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
,
where
m=\lambda_{p}
or
\lambda_{p}^{-1}
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
.
If
\epsilon=0
, then the situation is almost the same as the previous case
(the only difference is that there might be infinitely many separatrices smooth at
p
, and hence we can not decide the local defining equations of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, but this would not infect the CS-indices),
and we are done by a similar argument.
If
\epsilon=1
, then except the strict transform of
\{x=0\}
, all the other
separatrices through a point on
\pi^{-1}(p)
is actually contained in
\{x=0\}
.
It follows that there is only one separatrix of
\mathcal{F}
through
p
,
since the strict transform of any separatrix of
\mathcal{F}
through
p
gives a separatrix through a point on
\pi^{-1}(p)
and not contained in
\{x=0\}
.
This is a contradiction, since
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are already two different separatrices of
\mathcal{F}
through
p
.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

If
p
is a saddle node, one can also compute the
\mathrm{CS}
-indices on the local separatrices through
p
; see
[
Bru04
, § 3.2]
.

2.3.
Birational invariants of a foliated surface

In this subsection, we recall the birational invariants for a foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
; see
[
Bru99
,
Bru04
,
Men00
]
for more details.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, not necessarily reduced.
Let
\sigma:\,S^{\prime}\to S
be a blowing-up centered at some point
p\in S
, and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{F})
the induced foliation on
S^{\prime}
.
Let
q_{1},\cdots,q_{r}
be the singularities of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on the exceptional curve
E
.
Let
a_{p}
be the
vanishing order
of
\omega
at
p
, where
\omega
is a local one-form defining
\mathcal{F}
.
The number
a_{p}
is independent on the choice of
\omega
, and it is also the vanishing order of a local vector field
v
defining
\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 2.10
.

The above number
a_{p}
is called the
vanishing order
of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
,
and will be denoted by
a_{p}(\mathcal{F})
, or simply by
a_{p}
if there is no confusion.

For a singularity
p
, the vanishing order
a_{p}(\mathcal{F})
is different from the multiplicity
m_{p}(\mathcal{F})
in
Section 2.2
.
However, one checks easily that
m_{p}(\mathcal{F})\geq a_{p}(\mathcal{F})^{2}
.
According to
[
Bru04
, § 2.3, Example (1)]
, it holds that

K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=\sigma^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}}-(\ell_{p}-1)E,\quad\text{~where~}\ell_{p}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &a_{p},&~&\text{if $E$ is $\mathcal{F}$-invariant};\\
&a_{p}+1,&~&\text{if $E$ is not $\mathcal{F}$-invariant}.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.15)

Conversely, suppose
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is any foliation on
S^{\prime}
. Then the birational morphism
\sigma
induces a foliation

\mathcal{F}:=\sigma_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
on
S
by extending
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}|_{S^{\prime}\setminus E}
to
S
, where we view
S^{\prime}\setminus E\cong S\setminus\{p\}\subseteq S
.
Moreover,
K_{\mathcal{F}}=\sigma_{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{F}
is reduced, then
\ell_{p}=0
or
1
, and the exceptional curve
E
is always
\mathcal{F}
-invariant.
Hence for any birational morphism
\pi:\,S^{\prime}\to S
,

K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}}+\mathcal{E},

where
\mathcal{E}\geq 0
is supported on the exceptional curves of
\pi
.
In particular,

\dim H^{0}(S,nK_{\mathcal{F}})=\dim H^{0}(S^{\prime},nK_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}).

This stimulates the following definitions.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, and let
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
be any reduced model, i.e.,
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
is birational to
(S,\mathcal{F})
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is reduced.

(i)

For any integer
n\geq 1
, the
n
-th pluri-genus
p_{n}(\mathcal{F})
of
(S,\mathcal{F})
is defined to be

p_{n}(\mathcal{F}):=\dim H^{0}(S^{\prime},nK_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}).

When
n=1
, it is the geometric genus, and also denoted by
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})=p_{1}(\mathcal{F})
.

(ii)

The volume
\vol(\mathcal{F})
and the Kodaira dimension
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})
are defined as follows.

\left\{\begin{aligned} \vol({\mathcal{F}})&\,=\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\,p_{n}(\mathcal{F})}{n^{2}/2}=\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\,\dim H^{0}(S^{\prime},nK_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})}{n^{2}/2};\\[3.0pt]
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})&\,=\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\infty,&\quad&\text{if~$p_{n}(\mathcal{F})=0$ for any $n\geq 1$},\\
&\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\log p_{n}(\mathcal{F})}{\log n},&&\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.\end{aligned}\right.

By Seidenberg’s
Theorem 2.5
,
every foliated surface admits a reduced model by a sequence of blowing-ups.
The above definitions are independent on the choice of the reduced model,
and they are all birational invariants for a foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
.
Indeed, suppose that there are two reduced models
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
(S^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
.
Since
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
is birational to
(S^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
, there is a foliated surface
(\widetilde{S},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
with two birational morphisms
\rho^{\prime}:\,\widetilde{S}\to S^{\prime}

and
\rho^{\prime\prime}:\,\widetilde{S}\to S^{\prime\prime}
such that

(i)

the foliated surface
(\widetilde{S},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
is reduced;

(ii)

we have the isomorphism
(\rho^{\prime})^{*}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\cong(\rho^{\prime\prime})^{*}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime\prime}}

In particular,

\dim H^{0}(S^{\prime},nK_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})=\dim H^{0}(\widetilde{S},nK_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}})=\dim H^{0}(S^{\prime\prime},nK_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}).

Remark 2.12
.

(i) Historically, one defined the pluri-genera (as well as the volume and Kodaira dimension) using the divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
on
S
directly in some literatures, i.e., defined them to be
\dim H^{0}(S,nK_{\mathcal{F}})
.
However, it turns out that such definitions behave not well in birational geometry;
they might change under the birational morphisms between foliated surfaces.
This is one of the reasons for us to define them over a reduced model.

(ii) In the study of the minimal model program,
people prefer to consider foliated varieties with canonical singularities,
especially for foliations over high dimensional varieties, cf.
[
McQ08
,
Cas21
]
.
For foliated surfaces, it makes no essential difference between those with reduced singularities and with canonical singularities
because of Seidenberg’s
Theorem 2.5
.
Moreover, it would be more convenient to work with a reduced model to
introduce the new birational invariants: the Chern numbers; see
Section 5
for more details.

Suppose that the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is pseudo-effective for some reduced model
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
.
Let
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=P^{\prime}+N^{\prime}
be the Zariski decomposition, where
P^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
are respectively the nef and negative parts. By the Riemann-Roch theorem,

\vol(\mathcal{F})=(P^{\prime})^{2},

(2.16)

and
\vol(\mathcal{F})>0
if and only if
\mathcal{F}
is of general type, i.e., the Kodaira dimension
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})=2
.
In this case, we will also call the foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
is of general type,
in which case, the surface
S
might be of non-general type. Foliated surfaces of non-general type are classified up to birational equivalence, cf.
[
Bru03
,
Bru04
,
McQ08
]
.

\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})

Algebraic foliations (Fibrations of genus
g
)

Transcendental foliations

-\infty

g=0:

K_{\mathcal{F}}
is not pseudo-effective

Hilbert modular foliations

0

Isotrivial fibrations of genus
g=1

Quotients of Foliations generated by

global vector fields with isolated zeros

1

Non-isotrivial fibrations of genus
g=1

Riccati foliations or

or isotrivial fibrations of genus
g\geq 2

Turbulent foliations

We remark that in the above tabular,
the transcendental foliations of Kodaira dimension one
must be Riccati foliations or Turbulent foliations.
But the converse is not true.
Namely, there exist Riccati foliations or Turbulent foliations of Kodaira dimension zero.

3.
The Zariski decomposition of the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}

In this section, we are concerned about the Zariski decomposition of the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
.
We will first briefly recall the Zariski decomposition of the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
for a relatively minimal foliation
\mathcal{F}

with
K_{\mathcal{F}}
being pseudo-effective and refer to
[
McQ08
]
and
[
Bru04
, Chapter 8]
for more details.
Then we give a careful analysis on the negative part, and prove a technical result about the coefficients appearing in the negative part, which will be helpful in proving the Noether inequalities.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be reduced foliation on a smooth projective
S
.
An irreducible curve
C\subseteq S
is
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional if

(i).

C
is an exceptional curve of first kind on
S
, i.e., it is a smooth rational curve with
C^{2}=-1
;

(ii).

the contraction of
C
to a point produces a new foliation
(S_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
which is still reduced.

Definition 3.2
.

A foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
is called relatively minimal if

(i).

the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is reduced;

(ii).

there is no
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional curve on
S
.

It is proved that any foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
has a relatively minimal model, cf.
[
Bru04
, Proposition 5.1]
.
The relatively minimal model is not necessarily
unique, the foliations with at least two relatively minimal models
have been classified
[
Bru04
, Theorem 5.1]
:
fibrations by rational curves, Riccati foliations
and
very
special foliations.

We assume in the following that
\mathcal{F}
is a relatively minimal foliation on a smooth projective surface
S

such that
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is pseudo-effective.
In fact, the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is pseudo-effective if and only if
\mathcal{F}
is not induced by a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, cf.
[
Miy87
]
.
Denote the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
by

K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N,

(3.1)

where
P
is the nef part and
N
is the negative one.
In the case when
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal with pseudo-effective canonical divisor,
McQuillan proved that the support of the negative part
N
is a disjoint union of maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chains.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a relatively minimal foliation on a smooth projective surface
S
.
We say a curve
C\subseteq S
is an
\mathcal{F}
-chain if

(i).

the curve
C
is a Hirzebruch-Jung string, i.e.,
C=\cup_{j=1}^{r}C_{j}
, each
C_{j}
is a smooth rational curve with
C_{j}^{2}\leq-2
,
C_{j}\cdot C_{i}=1
if
|i-j|=1
and
0
if
|i-j|\geq 2
;

(ii).

each irreducible component
C_{j}
is
\mathcal{F}
-invariant;

(iii).

\mathrm{Sing}(\mathcal{F})\cap C
are all reduced and non-degenerate;

(iv).

Z(\mathcal{F},C_{1})=1
, and
Z(\mathcal{F},C_{j})=2
for any
2\leq j\leq r
.

Since each irreducible component
C_{j}
is a smooth rational curve, by
Proposition 2.3
the last condition (iv) is also equivalent to

(iv)
′
.

K_{\mathcal{F}}C_{1}=-1
, and
K_{\mathcal{F}}C_{j}=0
for any
2\leq j\leq r
.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
Bru04
, Theorem 8.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a relatively minimal foliation on a smooth projective surface
S
.
Suppose that
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is pseudo-effective with the Zariski decomposition as in (
3.1
).
Then the support
\mathrm{Supp}(N)
is a disjoint union of maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chains,
and
\lfloor N\rfloor=0
.

The above theorem shows that all the coefficients in
N
are less than
1
.
In fact, since the support
\mathrm{Supp}(N)
is a disjoint union of maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chains, which can be contracted to singularities of Hirzebruch-Jung type,
these coefficients can be explicitly computed out using continued fractions
[
BHPV04
, § III.5]
.
By contracting the support
\mathrm{Supp}(N)
, one obtains a surface
S_{0}
with finitely many singularities.
Then the negative part
N
can be decomposed into

N=\sum_{Q}N_{Q},

where the sum runs over all singularities
Q
’s on
S_{0}
, and
\mathrm{Supp}(N_{Q})
is supported on the inverse image of
Q
in
S
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose that
Q
is a singularity of type
A_{n,q}
,
and let
N_{Q}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}b_{j}C_{j}
with
C=\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{r}C_{j}
being a maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chain as above.
Then

b_{j}=\frac{\xi_{j}}{n},\qquad\forall\,1\leq j\leq r,

(3.2)

where
\xi_{r+1}=0
,
\xi_{r}=1
, and the rest
\xi_{j}
’s are given by the following recursion formula:

\xi_{j-1}-e_{j}\xi_{j}+\xi_{j+1}=0,\qquad\text{~$e_{j}=-C_{j}^{2}\geq 2$.}

(3.3)

In particular,

N_{Q}^{2}=-\frac{q}{n}.

(3.4)

Proof.

Define
\xi_{r+1}=0
,
\xi_{r}=1
, and the rest
\xi_{j}
’s by the following recursion formula (
3.3
) above.
Then it has been shown in
[
BHPV04
, § III.5]
that
\xi_{1}=q
,

\xi_{0}=n
, and that

\large\frac{n}{q}=e_{1}-\frac{1}{e_{2}-\frac{1}{\cdots\,-\,\frac{1}{e_{r}}}}.

Moreover, according to the property of the Zariski decomposition

N_{Q}C_{1}=K_{\mathcal{F}}C_{1}=-1,\qquad N_{Q}C_{j}=K_{\mathcal{F}}C_{j}=0,\quad\forall\,2\leq j\leq r.

On the other hand, define
N^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\xi_{j}}{n}C_{j}
.
Based on (
3.3
), one checks directly that

N^{\prime}C_{1}=-1=N_{Q}C_{1},\qquad N^{\prime}C_{j}=0=N_{Q}C_{j},\quad\forall\,2\leq j\leq r.

Since the intersection matrix
\big(C_{i}C_{j}\big)
is negatively definite,
it follows that
N_{Q}=N^{\prime}
, i.e., the equality (
3.2
) holds.
Moreover,

N_{Q}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}b_{j}N_{Q}\cdot C_{j}=-b_{1}=-\frac{q}{n}.

This proves (
3.4
). ∎

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
N_{Q}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{r}b_{j}C_{j}
with
C=\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{r}C_{j}
being a maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chain as above.
Then

b_{j}<\left\{\begin{aligned} &\,\frac{1}{e_{1}-1},&~&\text{~if~}j=1;\\[5.69054pt]
&\,\frac{1}{2e_{j}-3},&&\text{~if~}j\geq 2,\end{aligned}\right.

(3.5)

where
e_{j}=-C_{j}^{2}\geq 2
.

Proof.

Let
\xi_{r+1}=0
, and
b_{j}=\frac{\xi_{j}}{n}
for
1\leq j\leq r
.
By the above arguments,
\xi_{r}=1
, and the rest
\xi_{j}
’s can be computed by (
3.3
).
As proved in
[
BHPV04
, § III.5]
,
\xi_{1}=q
and
\xi_{0}=n
.
Note that each
e_{j}\geq 2
.
With the help of (
3.3
), one proves inductively that

\xi_{j-1}>\xi_{j},\qquad\forall\,1\leq j\leq r+1.

(3.6)

Hence

\xi_{j-1}=e_{j}\xi_{j}-\xi_{j+1}>e_{j}\xi_{j}-\xi_{j}=(e_{j}-1)\xi_{j}.

In particular,
n=\xi_{0}>(e_{1}-1)\xi_{1}
.
Equivalently,
b_{1}=\frac{\xi_{1}}{n}<\frac{1}{e_{1}-1}
as required.

Suppose now that
j\geq 2
. Then

n=\xi_{0}\geq\xi_{j-2}=e_{j-1}\xi_{j-1}-\xi_{j}\geq 2\xi_{j-1}-\xi_{j}>\big(2(e_{j}-1)-1\big)\xi_{j}=(2e_{j}-3)\xi_{j}.

It follows that
b_{j}=\frac{\xi_{j}}{n}<\frac{1}{2e_{j}-3}
if
j\geq 2
.
This completes the proof of (
3.5
).
∎

Remark 3.7
.

The above bounds on the coefficients of the negative part
N_{Q}
will be key to prove the Noether type inequalities for the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
.
One can similarly define the volume
\vol(L)
for any big divisor
L
on a smooth projective surface
S
by

\vol(L)=\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\dim H^{0}(X,nL)}{n^{2}/2}.

The naive Noether type inequalities do NOT hold for
L
.
For instance, let
e>0
and
S=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e)\big)
be the Hirzebruch surface admitting a unique section
C_{0}
with
C_{0}^{2}=-e<0
.
Let
f:\,S\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the geometrical ruling on
S
,
F
be a general fiber of
f
, and
L=mF+C_{0}
.
Suppose that
0<m<e
. Then one checks easily that the Zariski decomposition of
L
is

L=\Big(mF+\frac{m}{e}C_{0}\Big)+\frac{e-m}{e}C_{0},

and hence

\vol(L)=\frac{m^{2}}{e},\qquad h^{0}(L)=m+1.

Fixing
m\gg 0
and letting
e\to+\infty
, one deduces that there can not exist positive constants
a,b
such that

\vol(L)\geq ah^{0}(L)-b
.
Of course, in this concrete example, one shows that

L=K_{\mathcal{F}}
for some foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
S
can happen only when
m=e-1
based on (
3.3
) and (
3.2
).
If it is indeed the case, then

\vol(\mathcal{F})=\frac{m^{2}}{e}=\frac{(e-1)^{2}}{e}=e-2+\frac{1}{e}>e-2=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2.

We refer to
Example 9.2
for a construction of such a foliation.

4.
Algebraically integral foliations

In this section, we will first recall in
Section 4.1
the algebraic integrability of a foliation as well as the modular invariants of a fibration.

Section 4.2
is devoted to the resolution of non-reduced singularities of an algebraically integral foliation, which will be key to prove the Noether inequalities for algebraically integral foliations in
Section 8
.

4.1.
The algebraic integrability of a foliation and modular invariants

A foliation on a smooth projective surface
is called
algebraically integral
if it admits a rational first integral;
otherwise it is called
non-algebraic
or
transcendent
.
Equivalently, a foliation is algebraically integral if its integral curves form a pencil of algebraic curves.
It is clear that the algebraic integrability is a birational property:
if
(S,\mathcal{F})
and
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
are two foliated surface birational to each other,
then
(S,\mathcal{F})
is algebraic integral if and only if
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
is algebraic integral.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be an algebraically integral foliation on a smooth projective surface
S_{0}
,
and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
be the corresponding pencil.
By
[
Bru04
, Proposition 1.1]
, one proves easily that for any
p\in S_{0}
,

\displaystyle\text{the point $p$ is a dicritical (i.e., $\exists$ infinitely many separatrices of $\mathcal{F}$ through $p$),}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow~\text{the point $p$ is a base point of the pencil $\Lambda_{\mathcal{F}}$.}

By a sequence of possible blowing-ups
\sigma:\,S\to S_{0}
,
one obtains a surface fibration
f:\,S\to B
,
i.e.,
f
is proper surjective with connected fibers.
It defines a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
S
by taking the saturation of
\ker(df:\,T_{S}\to f^{*}T_{B})
in
T_{S}
.
By construction,
\mathcal{F}=\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
and it admits no dicritical points.
The canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is simple:

K_{\mathcal{F}}=K_{S/B}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}\Big(\sum(1-a_{i})C_{i}\Big),

(4.2)

where
K_{S/B}=K_{S}-f^{*}(K_{B})
is the relative canonical divisor,
the sum is taken over all components
C_{i}
’s in fibers of
f
,
and
a_{i}
is the multiplicity of
C_{i}
in fibers of
f
.

The foliation
\mathcal{F}
is reduced if and only if every possible singular fiber of
f
is normal crossing.
For any given foliation
\mathcal{F}
corresponding to a fibration
f:\,S\to B
above,
one can obtain a reduced one by a sequence of possible blowing-ups.
Such a foliation is relatively minimal if there is no redundant exceptional curves,
i.e., any possible exceptional curve in fibers of
f
intersects other components at at least three points.
In particular, if the fibration is semi-stable,
i.e., any possible singular fiber of
f
is a reduced node curve,
and any possible smooth rational component in such a singular fiber intersects other components at least two points,
then
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal and
K_{\mathcal{F}}=K_{S/B}
by (
4.2
).

There are several well-studied invariants for a surface fibration.
Let
f:\,S\to B
be a surface fibration (simply fibration).
Suppose that
f
is relatively minimal, i.e., there is no exceptional curve contained in fibers of
f
. Remark that this is different from the relative minimality of the foliation
\mathcal{F}
associated to
f
, as mentioned above.
Consider the following invariants.

\left\{\begin{aligned} K_{f}^{2}:&=K_{S/B}^{2}=K_{S}^{2}-8(g-1)(g(B)-1),\\
e_{f}:&=\sum_{F}\big(\chi_{\rm top}(F)+(2g-2)\big)=\chi_{\rm top}(S)-4(g-1)(g(B)-1),\\
\chi_{f}:&=\deg f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{S}(K_{S/B})=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{S})-(g-)(g(B)-1),\end{aligned}\right.

where
g
and
g(B)
are respectively the genus of a general fiber of
f
and the base
B
,
and
\chi_{\rm top}(F)
is the Euler topological characteristic of the reduced curve
F_{\rm red}
.
These are three non-negative integers and satisfy the Noether equality:

K_{f}^{2}+e_{f}=12\chi_{f}.

By the stable reduction theorem (cf.
[
DM69
]
),
there exists a base change
\phi:\widetilde{B}\to B
of finite degree, possibly ramified,
such that the pull-back fibration
\tilde{f}:\widetilde{S}\to\widetilde{B}
is semi-stable.
Here the pull-back fibration
\tilde{f}:\,\widetilde{S}\to\widetilde{B}
is constructed as follows.
Let
S_{1}
be the resolution of singularities of the fiber-product
S\times_{B}\widetilde{B}
.
Then
\tilde{f}:\,\widetilde{S}\to\widetilde{B}
is just the relatively minimal model of
S_{1}
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}}
\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{S\times_{B}\widetilde{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{0}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{B}

The modular invariants of
f
is then defined to be

\kappa(f):=\frac{K_{\tilde{f}}^{2}}{\deg\phi},\qquad\delta(f):=\frac{e_{\tilde{f}}}{\deg\phi},\qquad\chi(f):=\frac{\chi_{\tilde{f}}}{\deg\phi}.

The three invariants defined above are independent on the choices of the semi-stable reductions.
Moreover, these are three non-negative rational numbers satisfying the Noether equality

\kappa(f)+\delta(f)=12\chi(f).

(4.3)

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a (not necessarily reduced) foliation induced by a fibration
f:\,S\to B
.
Suppose that
E
is an exceptional curve and
\mathcal{F}
-invariant.
Let
\sigma:\,S\to S_{0}
be the blowing-down by contracting
E
.
Then there is an induced fibration
f_{0}:\,S_{0}\to B
which induces the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\sigma_{*}(\mathcal{F})
on
S_{0}
and satisfies
f=f_{0}\circ\sigma
.

Proof.

This is clear, since any
\mathcal{F}
-invariant curve is by definition contained in fibers of
f
.
By contracting a vertical exceptional curve, it still induces a fibration
f_{0}:\,S_{0}\to B
such that
f=f_{0}\circ\sigma
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an algebraically integral reduced foliation of general type on
S
.
Suppose that

\pi=\sigma_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{n}:\,S\to S_{0}=\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e)\big)

is a birational morphism to a Hirzebruch surface
S_{0}
,
where each
\sigma_{i}
is a blowing-up.
Then
n\geq 9
, i.e.,
\pi
consists of at least
9
blowing-ups.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is reduced of general type, it is induced by a fibration
f:\,S\to B
of genus
g\geq 2
.
Let
F
be a general fiber of
f
and
A=\pi_{*}(F)
.
Let
C_{0}
be a section of the Hirzebruch surface with
C_{0}^{2}=-e
(unique if
e\geq 1
) and
\Gamma_{0}\subseteq S_{0}
be a general fiber.
Then
\text{{\rm Pic\,}}(S_{0})
is generated by
C_{0}
and
\Gamma_{0}
.
Let
A\sim aC_{0}+b\Gamma_{0}
.
Then
b-ae\geq 0
since
A\cdot C_{0}\geq 0
.
Let

F=\pi^{*}(A)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathcal{E}_{i},

where
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is the total transform of the exceptional curve of
\sigma_{i}
.
Then
a_{i}\leq a=\Gamma_{0}\cdot A
, and

a(2b-ae)=A^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}.

Hence

\displaystyle 0<2g-2=K_{S}F

\displaystyle=\Big(\pi^{*}(K_{S_{0}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{i}\Big)\Big(\pi^{*}(A)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathcal{E}_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle=K_{S_{0}}A-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}-(2b-ae+2a).

In particular,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}>2b-ae+2a=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{a_{i}^{2}}{a}+2a.

Hence

2a^{2}<\sum_{i=1}^{n}(aa_{i}-a_{i}^{2})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\frac{a^{2}}{4}-\big(a/2-a_{i}\big)^{2}\Big)\leq\frac{na^{2}}{4}.

It follows that
n\geq 9
as required.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an algebraically integral reduced foliation of general type on
S
.
Suppose that

\pi=\sigma_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{n}:\,S\to S_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{2}

is a birational morphism,
where each
\sigma_{i}
is a blowing-up.
Then
n\geq 10
, i.e.,
\pi
consists of at least
10
blowing-ups.

Proof.

Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\pi_{1}=\sigma_{2}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{n}:\,S\to S_{1}
.
Then
\pi=\pi_{1}\circ\sigma_{1}
and
S_{1}\cong\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)\big)
.
According to
Lemma 4.2
,
\pi_{1}
consists of at least
9
blowing-ups.
Hence
n\geq 10
as required.
∎

4.2.
Resolution of the non-reduced singularities

In this subsection, we study the resolution of non-reduced singularities of an algebraically integral foliation,
which will be crucial in proving the third Noether inequality (
1.8
) in
Section 8
.
We will not insist on resolving it to be a reduced foliation;
instead, we weaken a little sometimes: the singularities on the resolution are all non-degenerate.
Remark that any reduced singularity of an algebraically integral foliation is non-degenerate,
since there is no saddle-node.
However, the converse is not true.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(S_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
be a foliated surface, and
p\in S_{0}
be a non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Let

\pi:\,S=S_{n}\overset{\sigma_{n}}{\longrightarrow}S_{n-1}\overset{\sigma_{n-1}}{\longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{\sigma_{2}}{\longrightarrow}S_{1}\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\longrightarrow}S_{0}

be a sequence of blowing-ups resolving the non-reduced singularity
p
,
such that
S\setminus\pi^{-1}(p)\cong S_{0}\setminus\{p\}
,
and that
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal over
\pi^{-1}(p)
,
i.e., all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
on
\pi^{-1}(p)
are reduced and there is no
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional curve contained in
\pi^{-1}(p)
.
Let

K_{\mathcal{F}}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i}.

where
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is the total inverse image of the exceptional curve of the blowing-up
\sigma_{i}
,
and
\mathcal{F}_{i}
be the induced foliation on the surface
S_{i}
.

(i)

It holds
\ell_{i}\geq 1
for any
1\leq i\leq n
.
In particular,
p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
.

(ii)

If the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is algebraically integral, then
\ell_{n}\geq 2
.

Proof.

(i). Let
\sigma_{i}:\,S_{i}\to S_{i-1}
be a blowing-up centered at
q_{i-1}\in S_{i-1}
for
1\leq i\leq n
with
q_{0}=p
.
Since
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal over
\pi^{-1}(p)
, it follows that each
q_{i-1}
is a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
;
otherwise,
\sigma_{i}
is a blowing-up centered at a smooth point of
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
,
and hence the inverse image of
q_{i-1}
in
S
would contain an
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional curve,
which contradicts the relative minimality of
\mathcal{F}
over
\pi^{-1}(p)
.
Hence
a_{q_{i-1}}\geq 1
,
where
a_{q_{i-1}}
is the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
around
q_{i-1}
.
According to (
2.15
),
\ell_{i}\geq a_{q_{i-1}}\geq 1
as required.

(ii). Replacing
\mathcal{F}_{0}
by
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
, we may assume that
\pi=\sigma:\,S\to S_{0}
is a blowing-up centered at
p
,
such that the exceptional curve
E
is not
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional, where
\mathcal{F}=\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
.
By blowing-ups centered at points away from
E
, we may assume that there is a fibration
f:\,S\to B
such that
\mathcal{F}
is induced by the fibration
f
.
If the exceptional curve
E
is not
\mathcal{F}
-invariant, then
\ell_{n}=a_{p}+1\geq 2
by (
2.15
).
Thus we may assume that
E
is
\mathcal{F}
-invariant, i.e., it is contained in fibers of
f
.
Since the exceptional curve
E
of
\sigma
is not
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional,
it follows that
E
would intersect other components of
F
at at least three points, where
F
is the fiber of
f
containing
E
.
Hence

3\leq Z(\mathcal{F},E)=K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot E+2=\big(\sigma^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-(\ell_{n}-1)E\big)\cdot E+2=\ell_{n}+1.

Thus
\ell_{n}\geq 2
as required. ∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(S_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
be a foliated surface,
and
p\in S_{0}
be a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Let
C
be an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant curve passing through
p
, and suppose that
C
is smooth at
p
.
Let
\sigma:\,S\to S_{0}
be the blowing-up centered at
p
with exceptional curve
E
,

\mathcal{F}=\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
, and
\overline{C}
be the strict transform of
C
with
q=E\cap\overline{C}
.
Let
K_{\mathcal{F}}=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\ell_{p}-1)E
. Then

Z(\mathcal{F},\overline{C},q)=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)-(\ell_{p}-1).

(4.4)

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition 2.3
.
Indeed, let
p_{1}=p,p_{2},\cdots,p_{m}
be all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
C
,
and let
q_{1}=q
and
q_{i}=\sigma^{-1}(p_{i})
for
i\geq 2
.
It is clear that
\chi(\overline{C})=\chi(C)
, and
Z(\mathcal{F},\overline{C},q_{i})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p_{i})
for
i\geq 2
.
Thus by
Proposition 2.3
,

Z(\mathcal{F},\overline{C})-\big(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}C-(\ell_{p}-1)\big)=Z(\mathcal{F},\overline{C})-K_{\mathcal{F}}\overline{C}=\chi(\overline{C})=\chi(C)=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C)-K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}C.

It follows that

\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z(\mathcal{F},\overline{C},q_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p_{i})-(\ell_{p}-1).

Combining this with the equalities
Z(\mathcal{F},\overline{C},q_{i})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p_{i})
for
i\geq 2
, we prove (
4.4
).
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an algebraically integral reduced foliation on
S
,
and
\pi:\,S\to S_{0}
be a birational morphism with
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\pi_{*}(\mathcal{F})
.
Let

|K_{\mathcal{F}}|=|M|+Z,

where
M
(resp.
Z
) is the moving (resp. fixed) part of
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|
.
Suppose that
M=\pi^{*}(M_{0})
for some divisor
M_{0}
on
S_{0}
.

(i)

All the possible non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
are on
Z_{0}=\pi_{*}(Z)
.
In particular, if
Z_{0}=0
, then
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is also reduced;
and
\pi
is an isomorphism if moreover
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.

(ii)

Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S_{0}
be a blowing-up centered at a non-reduced singularities
p
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
.
Then
\pi
factors through
\sigma_{1}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\ell_{p}-1)E_{1}
.
Then
m_{p}(Z_{0})\geq\ell_{p}-1
, where
m_{p}(Z_{0})
is the multiplicity of
Z_{0}
at
p
.

Proof.

(i).
By assumption,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=\pi_{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}})=\pi_{*}\big(\pi^{*}(M_{0})+Z\big)=M_{0}+Z_{0}.

On the other hand, by
Lemma 4.4
,

M+Z=K_{\mathcal{F}}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i}=M+\pi^{*}(Z_{0})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i},

where
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is the total inverse image of the exceptional curve of the
i
-th blowing-up
\sigma_{i}
contained in
\pi
.
Thus

\pi^{*}(Z_{0})=Z+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i}.

(4.5)

Suppose that there is a non-reduced singularity
p
which is not on
Z_{0}
.
By
Lemma 4.4
(ii), there is at least one exceptional curve
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, which is over
p
with
\ell_{i}\geq 2
.
This is a contradiction to (
4.5
),
since the total inverse image
\pi^{*}(Z_{0})
is the strict transform of
Z_{0}
plus some curves contracted to points on
Z_{0}
.
This proves that all the possible non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
are on
Z_{0}
. The rest statements follow immediately.

(ii).
Since
p
is non-reduced,
\pi
is not an isomorphism around
p
.
By the universal properties of blowing-ups,

\pi
factors through
\sigma_{1}
.
Let
\pi_{1}:\,S\to S_{1}
be the induced birational morphism with
\pi=\sigma_{1}\circ\pi_{1}
. Then

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=(\pi_{1})_{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}})=(\pi_{1})_{*}(M)+(\pi_{1})_{*}(Z)=\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0})+Z_{1},\qquad\text{where~}Z_{1}=(\pi_{1})_{*}(Z).

By assumption,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\ell_{p}-1)E_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0})+\sigma_{1}^{*}(Z_{0})-(\ell_{p}-1)E_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{Z}_{0}+\big(m_{p}(Z_{0})-(\ell_{p}-1)\big)E_{1},

where
\overline{Z}_{0}
is the strict transform of
Z_{0}
in
S_{1}
.
It follows that

Z_{1}=\overline{Z}_{0}+\big(m_{p}(Z_{0})-(\ell_{p}-1)\big)E_{1}.

Hence
m_{p}(Z_{0})\geq\ell_{p}-1
as required.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be an algebraically integral foliation on
S_{0}
,
and
p\in S_{0}
be a non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Let
C
be an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant curve passing through
p
.
Suppose that
C
is smooth at
p
.
If
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=1
, then locally around
p
,
there exists a coordinate
(z,w)
such that
C=\{z=0\}

and that
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is generated by the vector field

v=z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\lambda w\frac{\partial}{\partial w},\qquad\text{where $\lambda=\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}$}.

If moreover there exists another
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant curve
C^{\prime}
passing through
p
and smooth at
p
, then the above local coordinate can be chosen such that
C^{\prime}=\{w=0\}
.

Proof.

Let
v
be a local vector field defining
\mathcal{F}_{0}
around
p
.
We first show that the two eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of the linear part
(Dv)(p)
are both non-zero with
\lambda:=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
.
To this aim, we choose a local coordinate
(x,y)
with
C=\{x=0\}
.
Let
v=A\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+B\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
.
Since
C
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant,
A=xA_{1}
for some holomorphic function
A_{1}
.
Hence

(Dv)(p)=\left(\begin{aligned} A_{1}(p)~&~\quad 0\\
B_{x}(p)~&~B_{y}(p)\end{aligned}\right),\qquad\text{where $B_{x}=\frac{\partial B}{\partial x}$ and $B_{y}=\frac{\partial B}{\partial y}$}.

(4.6)

On the other hand,
\omega:=xA_{1}dy-Bdx
would be a local one-form defining
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Hence by definition,
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)
equals to the vanishing order of the restriction
(-B)|_{C}
at
p
.
Since
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=1
by assumption, it follows that

B(x,y)=xB_{1}(x,y)+yB_{2}(y),

where
B_{1}(x,y)
and
B_{2}(y)
are both holomorphic with
B_{2}(0)=\lambda_{2}\neq 0
.
In particular,
B_{y}(p)=\lambda_{2}\neq 0
.
According to (
4.6
), the two eigenvalues of the linear part
(Dv)(p)
are equal to
A_{1}(p)
and
\lambda_{2}
.
It follows that the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p
is equal to
\lambda=\frac{A_{1}(p)}{\lambda_{2}}
.
Since
p
is assumed to be a non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
,
it follows that
A_{1}(p)=\lambda_{1}\neq 0
(otherwise it is a saddle-node),
and that
\lambda=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
as required.

Now we have proved that, locally around
p
the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{0}
can be a generated by a vector field whose linear part has eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
,
both non-zero with
\lambda=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
.
Such a singularity can be resolved by a standard way as recalled above
Lemma 2.8
.

If
\lambda\not\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\cup\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}^{+}}
,
then
\mathcal{F}_{0}
can be generated by a local vector field

v=z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\lambda w\frac{\partial}{\partial w}
,
where
\lambda=\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
with
\gcd(m_{1},m_{2})=1
.
Equivalently,
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is given by the levels of the meromorphic function
\frac{z^{m_{1}}}{w^{m_{2}}}
.
In particular, the local separatrices passing through
p
are defined by

\frac{z^{m_{1}}}{w^{m_{2}}}=c
or
z^{m_{1}}-cw^{m_{2}}=0
, where
c\in\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Since
C
is a separatrix passing through
p
and
C
is smooth at
p
by assumption,
it follows that
C
is defined by
z=0
or
w=0
.
By exchange
z
and
w
(this would change
\lambda
to
\lambda^{-1}
), we may assume that
C=\{z=0\}
as required.

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\cup\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}^{+}}
,
then
\mathcal{F}_{0}
