can be generated by a local vector field

v=z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+(mw+\epsilon z^{m})\frac{\partial}{\partial w}
,
where
m=\lambda
or
\lambda^{-1}
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
.
If
\epsilon=0
, then the situation is almost the same as the previous case, and we are done by a similar argument.
If
\epsilon=1
, then the resolution of the non-reduced singularity
p
would create a saddle-node of multiplicity two.
This is impossible since
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is assumed to be algebraically integral.

Finally, it remains to prove that
C^{\prime}=\{w=0\}
if there exists another
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant curve
C^{\prime}
passing through
p
and smooth at
p
.
If
\lambda=\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}\neq 1
, then by the above argument, the local separatrices passing through
p
are defined by
z^{m_{1}}-cw^{m_{2}}=0
.
Hence
C^{\prime}=\{w=0\}
as required.
If
\lambda=1
, then the local separatrices passing through
p
are defined by
z-cw=0
. Hence
C^{\prime}=\{z-c_{0}w=0\}
for some
c_{0}\neq 0
.
Replacing
(z,w)
by
(z,w^{\prime})=(z,c_{0}w-z)
, one sees that the local vector field defining
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is

v=z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\lambda w\frac{\partial}{\partial w}=z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\lambda w^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{\prime}},

and
C=\{z=0\}
and
C^{\prime}=\{w^{\prime}=0\}
as required.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be an algebraically integral foliation on
S_{0}
,
and
C,C^{\prime}
be two
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant curves which intersect transversely at
p
.
Suppose that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=k\geq 2
.

(i)

It holds that
k^{\prime}:=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C^{\prime},p)\geq 2
.

(ii)

If moreover the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p
is
a_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\leq 2
,
then
a_{p}=\min\{k,k^{\prime}\}=2
.

Proof.

(i). Since
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=k\geq 2
,
p
is a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
, and hence

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C^{\prime},p)=k^{\prime}\geq 1
.
Suppose that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C^{\prime},p)=1
. Then by
Lemma 4.7
, it follows that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=1
too, which is a contradiction.
Hence
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C^{\prime},p)=k^{\prime}\geq 2
as required.

(ii).
Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S_{0}
be the blowing-up centered at
p
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
.
If
E_{1}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
-invariant,
then
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-a_{p}E_{1}
by (
2.15
).
Hence
\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=a_{p}-1\leq 1
by
Proposition 2.2
.
It follows that there is at most one singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
on
E_{1}
.
We may thus assume without loss of generality that
q=\overline{C}\cap E_{1}
is not a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
(the arguments would be similar if
q^{\prime}=\overline{C}^{\prime}\cap E_{1}
is not a singularity), where
\overline{C}
(resp.
\overline{C}^{\prime}
) is the strict transform of
C
(resp.
C^{\prime}
).
By (
4.4
),

0=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C},q)=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)-a_{p},\quad\Longrightarrow\quad k=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=a_{p}\leq 2.

It follows that
a_{p}=k=2
as required, since
k
is assumed to be
\geq 2
.
Suppose next that
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant.
Then
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(a_{p}-1)E_{1}
by (
2.15
).
Hence
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=a_{p}+1\leq 3
by
Proposition 2.3
.
Note that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q)\geq 1
and
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q^{\prime})\geq 1
.
We may thus assume without loss of generality that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q)=1
;
otherwise,
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})\geq Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q)+Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q^{\prime})\geq 4
, which is a contradiction.
By
Lemma 4.7
, one sees that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C},q)=1
too.
According to (
4.4
),

1=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C},q)=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)-(a_{p}-1)=k+1-a_{p}.

As
k\geq 2
and
a_{p}\leq 2
by assumption,
it follows also that
a_{p}=k=2
as required.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be an algebraically integral foliation on
S_{0}
,
and
p\in S_{0}
be a non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S_{0}
be the blowing-up centered at
p
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
.
Suppose that
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant,
and that there are exactly two singularities
q_{1},q_{2}
of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
on
E_{1}
with

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{2})=1.

(4.7)

Then
p
is a non-degenerate singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-(\ell-1)E_{1}
.
By
Proposition 2.3
,

2=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\cdot E_{1}+2=(\ell-1)+2.

Hence
\ell=1
, i.e.,
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
.

Suppose first that both singularities
q_{1},q_{2}
are reduced.
Then
E_{1}
is an
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-exceptional curve by the assumption (
4.7
), see the text after
[
Bru04
, Definition 5.2]
.
Hence there is at least one point, say
q_{1}
, is non-reduced.
By
Lemma 4.7
, locally around
q_{1}
, there exists a local coordinate
(z,w)
such that

E_{1}=\{z=0\}
and that
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is generated by the vector field

v=z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\lambda w\frac{\partial}{\partial w},\qquad\text{where $\lambda=\frac{m}{n}\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}$}.

Let
C_{1}=\{w=0\}
be the local holomorphic curve which is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant and
C_{0}=\sigma_{1}(C_{1})
.
Then
C_{0}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant and smooth around
p
and by
Lemma 4.5
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C_{0},p)=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},C_{1},q_{1})=1.

This implies that
p
is non-degenerate by
Lemma 4.7
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be an algebraically integral foliation on
S_{0}
,
and
p\in S_{0}
be a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Let
C
be an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant curve passing through
p
with
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=k\geq 2

Suppose that
C
is smooth at
p
.

(i)

Locally around
p
,
there exists a coordinate
(x,y)
such that
C=\{x=0\}

and that
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is generated by the vector field

v=xA_{1}(x,y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\big(xB_{1}(x,y)+y^{k}B_{2}(y)\big)\frac{\partial}{\partial y},

where
A_{1}(x,y)
,
B_{1}(x,y)
and
B_{2}(y)
are all holomorphic with

A_{1}(0,0)=0
and
B_{2}(0)\neq 0
.
In particular,
p
is a non-reduced singularity.

(ii)

Suppose moreover that the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p
is equal to
1
. Then there is a sequence of blowing-ups:

\pi:\,S_{k}\overset{\sigma_{k}}{\longrightarrow}S_{k-1}\overset{\sigma_{k-1}}{\longrightarrow}~\cdots~\overset{\sigma_{2}}{\longrightarrow}S_{1}\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\longrightarrow}S_{0},

where
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S_{0}
is the blowing-up centered at
p
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
,
and
\sigma_{i}:\,S_{i}\to S_{i-1}
(for
2\leq i\leq k
) is the blowing up centered at the intersection
q_{i-1}=E_{i-1}\cap\overline{C}
with exceptional curve
E_{i}\subseteq S_{i}
.
Here we denote by
\overline{C}
the strict transform in each
S_{i}
(
i\geq 1
) by abuse of notations, and similarly for
\overline{E}_{j}
with
j<i
. The inverse image of
C
around
p
is as follows:

p
C
\sigma_{1}
q_{1}
\overline{C}
E_{1}
\sigma_{2}
q_{2}
\bar{q}_{1}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{C}
E_{2}
\sigma_{3}
q_{3}
\bar{q}_{2}
\bar{q}_{1}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{C}
\overline{E}_{2}
E_{3}
\sigma_{4}
\sigma_{k}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{E}_{2}
\overline{E}_{3}
\overline{E}_{4}
\overline{E}_{k-1}
E_{k}
\overline{C}
\bar{q}_{1}
\bar{q}_{2}
\bar{q}_{3}
\bar{q}_{k-1}
q_{k}^{\prime}
q_{k}

On
S_{k}
, all the singularities over
\pi^{-1}(p)
are non-degenerate (might not be reduced).
Moreover, let
\mathcal{F}_{i}
be the induced foliation on
S_{i}
. Then

\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}=\pi^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-\sum_{i=2}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\pi^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-\bigg(\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}(i-1)\overline{E}_{i}+(k-1)E_{k}\bigg),\\
&\pi^{*}(C)=\overline{C}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\overline{C}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}i\overline{E}_{i}+kE_{k},\end{aligned}\right.

(4.8)

where
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=i}^{k-1}\overline{E}_{j}+E_{k}
is the total transform of the exceptional curve of
\sigma_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
.

Proof.

(i).
Let
(x,y)
be a local coordinate around
p
such that
C=\{x=0\}
.
Let
v=A\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+B\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
.
Since
C
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant,
A=xA_{1}
for some holomorphic function
A_{1}
.
Moreover, similar as in the proof of
Lemma 4.7
,
one shows that the linear part of
(Dv)(p)
is of the form in (
4.6
), and that

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=\text{vanishing order of $(-B)|_{C}$ at $p$}.

Thus

B(x,y)=xB_{1}(x,y)+y^{k}B_{2}(y),

where
B_{1}(x,y)
and
B_{2}(y)
are both holomorphic with
B_{2}(0)\neq 0
.
In particular,
B_{y}(p)=0
since
k\geq 2
.
According to (
4.6
), the two eigenvalues of the linear part
(Dv)(p)
are equal to
A_{1}(p)
and
B_{y}(p)=0
.
Since
p
is assumed to be a non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
,
it follows that
A_{1}(p)=A_{1}(0,0)=0
as required; otherwise it is a saddle-node.

(ii).
By (i), the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is defined by the one-form

\omega=\big(xB_{1}(x,y)+y^{k}B_{2}(y)\big)dx-xA_{1}(x,y)dy.

Since the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p
is equal to
1
,
it follows that
B_{1}(0,0)\neq 0
.
Let
\sigma_{1}
be given by
(x,y)=(x_{1}y_{1},y_{1})
.
Then
\overline{C}=\{x_{1}=0\}
and
E_{1}=\{y_{1}=0\}
.
Moreover,

\sigma_{1}^{*}(\omega)=y_{1}^{2}\big(x_{1}B_{1}(x_{1}y_{1},y_{1})+y_{1}^{k-1}B_{2}(y_{1})\big)dx_{1}+x_{1}y_{1}\big(x_{1}B_{1}(x_{1}y_{1},y_{1})+y_{1}^{k-1}B_{2}(y_{1})-A_{1}(x_{1}y_{1},y_{1})\big)dy_{1}.

Since
A_{1}(0,0)=0
,
B_{1}(0,0)\neq 0
and
B_{2}(0)\neq 0
,
it follows that the vanishing order of
\sigma_{1}^{*}(\omega)
along
E_{1}=\{y_{1}=0\}
is exactly
1
.
Hence the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is defined by the one-form

\omega_{1}=\frac{\sigma_{1}^{*}(\omega)}{y_{1}}
, namely,

\omega_{1}=y_{1}\big(x_{1}B_{1}(x_{1}y_{1},y_{1})+y_{1}^{k-1}B_{2}(y_{1})\big)dx_{1}+x_{1}\big(x_{1}B_{1}(x_{1}y_{1},y_{1})+y_{1}^{k-1}B_{2}(y_{1})-A_{1}(x_{1}y_{1},y_{1})\big)dy_{1}.

Hence the vanishing order of
\omega_{1}
at
\overline{C}\cap E_{1}=q_{1}=(0,0)
is
2
.
Note that the exceptional curve
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant since
y
divides the two-form
\omega_{1}\wedge dy
.
By (
2.15
) and (
4.4
),

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}),\qquad Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=2,\qquad Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C},q_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},C,p)=k.

Combining with
Corollary 4.8
, one obtains that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=2
, and hence

q_{1}
is the unique singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
on
E_{1}
.

Claim 4.11
.

For any
2\leq i\leq k-1
, let
\sigma_{i}:\,S_{i}\to S_{i-1}
be the blowing-up centered at
q_{i-1}=E_{i-1}\cap\overline{C}
with exceptional curve
E_{i}\subseteq S_{i}
,
and let
\mathcal{F}_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{i-1})
.

(1).

Locally around around
q_{i}
,
\mathcal{F}_{i}
can be defined by the one-form

\displaystyle\omega_{i}=

\displaystyle y_{i}\big(x_{i}B_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})+y_{i}^{k-i}B_{2}(y_{i})\big)dx_{i}

(4.9)

\displaystyle+x_{i}\Big(i\big(x_{i}B_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})+y_{i}^{k-i}B_{2}(y_{i})\big)-\frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}\Big)dy_{i}.

Moreover, the function
\frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}
takes value equal to
0
when
x_{i}=y_{i}=0
.
In other words, one can express
A_{1}(x,y)
as

A_{1}(x,y)=xA_{2}(x,y)+y^{i}A_{3}(y)
for some holomorphic functions
A_{2}(x,y)
and
A_{3}(y)
.
In particular, the vanishing order of
\omega_{i}
at
q_{i}
is
2
,

E_{i}
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-invariant, and

K_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}=\sigma_{i}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{i-1}})-E_{i}
.

(2).

The two points
\bar{q}_{i-1}=E_{i}\cap\overline{E}_{i-1}
and
q_{i}=E_{i}\cap\overline{C}
are the only two singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
on
E_{i}
, where
\bar{q}_{i-1}
is non-degenerate, and

Z(\mathcal{F}_{i},\overline{C},q_{i})=k-(i-1)\geq 2,\qquad Z(\mathcal{F}_{i},E_{i},q_{i})=2.

Proof of
Claim 4.11
.

We prove this by induction.
Clearly
\omega_{1}
has the form as in (
4.9
).
Suppose that
\omega_{i-1}
has the form as in (
4.9
).
Locally,
\sigma_{i}
is given by
(x_{i-1},y_{i-1})=(x_{i}y_{i},y_{i})

with
\overline{C}=\{x_{i}=0\}
and
E_{i}=\{y_{i}=0\}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}^{*}(\omega_{i-1})=

\displaystyle y_{i}^{3}\big(x_{i}B_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})+y_{i}^{k-i}B_{2}(y_{i})\big)dx_{i}

\displaystyle+x_{i}y_{i}^{2}\Big(i\big(x_{i}B_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})+y_{i}^{k-i}B_{2}(y_{i})\big)-\frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}\Big)dy_{i}.

Since
B_{1}(0,0)\neq 0
, it follows that the multiplicity of
E_{i}=\{y_{i}=0\}
in
\sigma_{i}^{*}(\omega_{i-1})
is exactly
2
.
Hence
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is defined by

\displaystyle\omega_{i}=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{*}(\omega_{i-1})}{y_{i}^{2}}=

\displaystyle y_{i}\big(x_{i}B_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})+y_{i}^{k-i}B_{2}(y_{i})\big)dx_{i}

\displaystyle+x_{i}\Big(i\big(x_{i}B_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})+y_{i}^{k-i}B_{2}(y_{i})\big)-\frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}\Big)dy_{i}.

Equivalently,
\mathcal{F}_{i}
can be defined locally by the vector field

\displaystyle v_{i}=x_{i}\Big(i\big(x_{i}B_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})+y_{i}^{k-i}B_{2}(y_{i})\big)-\frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+y_{i}\big(x_{i}B_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})+y_{i}^{k-i}B_{2}(y_{i})\big)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}.

Hence the linear part of
(Dv_{i})(q_{i})
is

(Dv_{i})(q_{i})=\left(\begin{aligned} \frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}\Big(0,0\Big)~&~\quad 0~\\
0~\qquad\,\quad&~\quad 0~\end{aligned}\right).

Here,
\frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}\Big(0,0\Big)
stands for the value of the function
\frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}
when
x_{i}=y_{i}=0
.
Since
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is algebraically integral, it follows that
\frac{A_{1}(x_{i}y_{i}^{i},y_{i})}{y_{i}^{i-1}}\Big(0,0\Big)=0
as required.
In particular, the vanishing order of
\omega_{i}
at
q_{i}
is
2
since
B_{1}(0,0)\neq 0
.
Moreover, from the expression of
\omega_{i}
, one sees easily that
E_{i}
is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-invariant.
It follows that
K_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}=\sigma_{i}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{i-1}})-E_{i}
by (
2.15
),
and hence by
Lemma 4.5
and
Proposition 2.3
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{i},\overline{C},q_{i})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\overline{C},q_{i-1})-1=k+1-i,\qquad Z(\mathcal{F}_{i},E_{i})=3.

As
\overline{E}_{i-1},E_{i},\overline{C}
are all
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-invariant,
the two intersection points
\bar{q}_{i-1}=E_{i}\cap\overline{E}_{i-1}
and
q_{i}=E_{i}\cap\overline{C}
are singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
.
Since
Z(\mathcal{F}_{i},\overline{C},q_{i})=k+1-i\geq 2
, it follows that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{i},E_{i},q_{i})\geq 2
by
Corollary 4.8
.
Thus

Z(\mathcal{F}_{i},E_{i},\bar{q}_{i-1})=1,\qquad Z(\mathcal{F}_{i},E_{i},q_{i})=2.

In particular,
\bar{q}_{i-1}
and
q_{i}
are the only two singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
on
E_{i}
.
∎

Come back to the proof of
Lemma 4.10
.
Let
\sigma_{k}:\,S_{k}\to S_{k-1}
be the blowing-up centered at
q_{k-1}=E_{k-1}\cap\overline{C}
with exceptional curve
E_{k}\subseteq S_{k}
.
Locally,
\sigma_{k}
is given by
(x_{k-1},y_{k-1})=(x_{k}y_{k},y_{k})

with
\overline{C}=\{x_{k}=0\}
and
E_{k}=\{y_{k}=0\}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\sigma_{k}^{*}(\omega_{k-1})=

\displaystyle y_{k}^{3}\big(x_{k}B_{1}(x_{k}y_{k}^{k},y_{k})+B_{2}(y_{k})\big)dx_{k}

\displaystyle+x_{k}y_{k}^{2}\Big(k\big(x_{k}B_{1}(x_{k}y_{k}^{k},y_{k})+B_{2}(y_{k})\big)-\frac{A_{1}(x_{k}y_{k}^{k},y_{k})}{y_{k}^{k-1}}\Big)dy_{k}.

Since
B_{1}(0,0)\neq 0
and
B_{2}(0)\neq 0
, it follows that the multiplicity of
E_{k}=\{y_{k}=0\}
in
\sigma_{k}^{*}(\omega_{k-1})
is exactly
2
.
Hence
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is defined by

\displaystyle\omega_{k}=\frac{\sigma_{k}^{*}(\omega_{k-1})}{y_{k}^{2}}=

\displaystyle y_{k}\big(x_{k}B_{1}(x_{k}y_{k}^{k},y_{k})+B_{2}(y_{k})\big)dx_{k}

\displaystyle+x_{k}\Big(k\big(x_{k}B_{1}(x_{k}y_{k}^{k},y_{k})+B_{2}(y_{k})\big)-\frac{A_{1}(x_{k}y_{k}^{k},y_{k})}{y_{k}^{k-1}}\Big)dy_{k}.

In particular,
E_{k}
is
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-invariant, and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{k}}=\sigma_{k}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{k-1}})-E_{k}
.
By
Lemma 4.5
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{k},\overline{E}_{k-1},\bar{q}_{k-1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},E_{k-1},q_{k-1})-1=1,\quad Z(\mathcal{F}_{k},\overline{C},q_{k})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\overline{C},q_{k-1})-1=1.

Combining this with
Corollary 4.8
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{k},E_{k},\bar{q}_{k-1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{k},E_{k},q_{k})=1.

On the other hand, by
Proposition 2.3
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{k},E_{k})=3
.
Hence there must be a third singularity
q_{k}^{\prime}
with
Z(\mathcal{F}_{k},E_{k},q_{k}^{\prime})=1
, which is non-degenerate by
Lemma 4.7
.
Finally, the first formula in (
4.8
) follows from the equalities
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})
and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}=\sigma_{i}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{i-1}})-E_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq k
;
and the second formula in (
4.8
) follows by a direct computation.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be an algebraically integral foliation on
S_{0}
,
and
p\in S_{0}
be a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Let
C
be an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant curve passing through
p

such that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=2
and that
C
is smooth at
p
.
Suppose that the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p
is
2
. Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S_{0}
be the blowing-up centered at
p
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
. Then

(i)

either
E_{1}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, in which case
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-2E_{1}
and
q_{1}=\overline{C}\cap E_{1}

is not a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
;

(ii)

or
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, in which case
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-E_{1}
,
q_{1}=\overline{C}\cap E

is a non-degenerate singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, and there are exactly another two singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
on
E_{1}

with
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1}^{\prime})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1}^{\prime\prime})=1
,
where
\overline{C}
is the strict transform of
C
;

p
C
\sigma_{1}
q_{1}
q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
q_{1}^{\prime}
\overline{C}
E_{1}

(iii)

or
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, in which case

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-E_{1}
,
q_{1}=\overline{C}\cap E

is a non-degenerate singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, and there is exactly another one singularity
q_{1}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
on
E_{1}

with
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1}^{\prime})=2
.

p
C
\sigma_{1}
q_{1}
q_{1}^{\prime}
\overline{C}
E_{1}

Proof.

If
E_{1}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, then we are in case (i).
In this case,
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-2E_{1}
by (
2.15
),
and hence by
Lemma 4.5
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C},q_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)-2=0.

Hence
q_{1}
is not a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

If
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant,

By (

),
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-E_{1}
in this case,
and hence

, and by

,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C},q_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)-1=1.

Hence
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=1
by

.
It follows that either there are another two singularities

and

with

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1}^{\prime})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1}^{\prime\prime})=1
,
or there is another one singularity
q_{1}^{\prime}
with

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1}^{\prime})=2
.
∎

By repeating the process for the singularity
q_{1}^{\prime}
with
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1}^{\prime})=2
in case (iii) above,
we can reach the following situation.

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be an algebraically integral foliation on
S_{0}
,
and
p\in S_{0}
be a non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Let
C
be an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant curve passing through
p
.
Suppose that
C
is smooth at
p
, and that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=2
.
Then there exist a sequence blowing-ups

\pi=\sigma_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{n}:~S_{n}\overset{\sigma_{n}}{\longrightarrow}S_{n-1}\overset{\sigma_{n-1}}{\longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{\sigma_{2}}{\longrightarrow}S_{1}\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\longrightarrow}S_{0},

such that the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
on
\pi^{-1}(p)
belong to one of the following,
where a solid curve (resp. dotted curve) means
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant (resp. non-
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant).

(i)

A chain of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant curves (
n\geq 1
) with all the singularities on
\pi^{-1}(p)
being non-degenerate,

\overline{C}^{2}=C^{2}-1
,

\overline{E}_{i}
is the strict transform of the exceptional curve of
\sigma_{i}
and

\overline{E}_{i}^{2}=-2
for

,
and

. Moreover,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}i\overline{E}_{i},

(4.10)

where
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=i}^{n}\overline{E}_{j}
is total inverse image of the exceptional curve of
\sigma_{i}
, (here we understand
\mathcal{E}_{n}=E_{n}=\overline{E}_{n}
).

q_{1}
\overline{C}
q_{2}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{E}_{2}
q_{n}
\overline{E}_{n-1}
q_{n}^{\prime\prime}
q_{n}^{\prime}
E_{n}

(ii)

A chain of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant curves (
n\geq 2
) with all the singularities on
\pi^{-1}(p)
being non-degenerate,

\overline{C}^{2}=C^{2}-1
if
n\geq 3
(resp.
\overline{C}^{2}=C^{2}-2
if

),

\overline{E}_{i}

\sigma_{i}
and

\overline{E}_{i}^{2}=-2
for

or
i=n-1
,

\overline{E}_{n-2}^{2}=-3
,
and
E_{n}^{2}=-1
. Moreover,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}\mathcal{E}_{i}-\mathcal{E}_{n}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}i\overline{E}_{i}-(n-2)\overline{E}_{n-1}-(2n-3)E_{n},

(4.11)

where
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\sum\limits_{j=i}^{n-1}\overline{E}_{j}+2E_{n}
is the total inverse image of the exceptional curve of
\sigma_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n-2
, and
\mathcal{E}_{n}=E_{n}
the exceptional curve of
\sigma_{n}
.

q_{1}
\overline{C}
q_{2}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{E}_{2}
q_{n-1}
\overline{E}_{n-2}
q_{n}^{\prime}
q_{n}
E_{n}
\overline{E}_{n-1}

(iii)

A chain of rational curves (
n\geq 1
),
such that
\overline{E}_{i}
is
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant for
1\leq i\leq n-1
,
E_{n}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant,
all the singularities on
\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\overline{E}_{i}
are non-degenerate,

\overline{C}^{2}=C^{2}-1
,

\overline{E}_{i}
is the strict transform of the exceptional curve of
\sigma_{i}
with

\overline{E}_{i}^{2}=-2
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
, and
E_{n}^{2}=-1
.

q_{1}
\overline{C}
q_{2}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{E}_{2}
q_{n-1}
\overline{E}_{n-1}
E_{n}

Proof.

If
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=2
, it follows that the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p
is at most
2
by the definition of the
Z
-index, cf.
Section 2
.
Almost all the statements follow from
Lemma 4.12
and
Lemma 4.10
(for
k=2
) by induction.
We remark that in case (iii), except these singularities
\{q_{1},\cdots q_{n-1}\}
, which are all non-degenerate,
there might exist singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
on
E_{n}
(which is not
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant in this case).
Nevertheless, the intersection point
q_{n}=\overline{E}_{n-1}\cap E_{n}
is not a singularity by
Lemma 4.12
(i).
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be an algebraically integral foliation on
S_{0}
,
and
p\in S_{0}
be a non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Let
C
be an algebraic curve passing through
p
, which not
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant.
Suppose that
C
is smooth at
p
, and that
\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=1
.
Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S_{0}
be the blowing-up centered at
p
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}
.

(i)

If
E_{1}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, then
\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=0
.
In particular, there is no singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
on
E_{1}
.

(ii)

If
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, then
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=2
.
Moreover, the intersection
q=\overline{C}\cap E_{1}
is not a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
(x,y)
be a local coordinate such that
C=\{x=0\}
.
Let
v=A\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+B\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
be a vector field generating
\mathcal{F}_{0}
around
p
.
Then

1=\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)=I_{p}\big(x,v(x)\big)=I_{p}\big(x,A(x,y)\big).

Hence
\frac{\partial A}{\partial y}(0,0)\neq 0
.
In particular, the vanishing order of
v
at
p
is
a_{p}=1
.

If
E_{1}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, then
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-E_{1}
by (
2.15
).
Hence
\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}E_{1}+E_{1}^{2}=0
as required.

If
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, then
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
by (
2.15
).
Hence
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}E_{1}+2=2
by
Proposition 2.3
.
Moreover,

\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\overline{C}+\overline{C}^{2}=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}C+(C^{2}-1)=\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{0},C)-1.

It follows that
\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C},q)=\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{0},C,p)-1=0
,
since
\sigma_{1}
induces an isomorphism between
S_{1}\setminus E_{1}

and
S_{0}\setminus\{p\}
.
In particular, the intersection
q=\overline{C}\cap E_{1}
is not a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
.
∎

5.
The Chern numbers of foliations

The main purpose of this section is to introduce three birational invariants for a foliated surface, which will be called the Chern numbers.
Some basic properties of these three invariants will be also discussed.
In particular,
Theorem 1.2
will be proved in this section.
More precisely, in
Section 5.1
we will introduce the three Chern numbers and prove the Noether equality (
1.1
).
In
Section 5.2
we show that the three Chern numbers are well-defined birational invariants. In particular, we will prove that the first Chern number
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})
is nothing but the volume of
\mathcal{F}
.
Finally, in
Section 5.3
we consider the case when
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
is induced by a family
f:\,S\to B
of curves.
In this case, we prove that the three Chern numbers are just the corresponding modular invariants of
f
.

5.1.
Definitions and the Noether equality

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, and
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
be a relatively minimal model, i.e.,
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
is birational to
(S,\mathcal{F})
and
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
is relatively minimal.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
(S^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
be a two relatively minimal model of a foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
.
Then either

(i)

both the canonical divisors
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
are non-pseudo-effective; or

(ii)

both the canonical divisors
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
are pseudo-effective.

Proof.

Suppose for instance
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is not pseudo-effective.
Then
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is induced by a family of rational curves by Miyaoka’s
Theorem 1.1
, and so is
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
.
It implies that
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
is not pseudo-effective either.
This completes the proof.
∎

Suppose that
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is pseudo-effective. Let

K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=P^{\prime}+N^{\prime},

be the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
, where
P^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
are respectively the nef and negative parts.
We will use the following notations.

Notations.

For a singular point
p
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, we denote by
p\in N^{\prime}
if
p

lies on the support of the negative part
N^{\prime}
.
In case
N^{\prime}=0
,
p\in N^{\prime}
means
p
is in an empty set.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface, and
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
be a relatively minimal model as above.

(i)

Suppose that the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is not pseudo-effective. We define all the three Chern numbers of
\mathcal{F}
to be zero:

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=\chi(\mathcal{F})=0.

(ii)

Suppose that the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is pseudo-effective. The Chern numbers of
\mathcal{F}
is defined by
the following formulas.

\left\{\begin{aligned} c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})&\,:=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),\\
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})&\,:=\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),\\
\chi(\mathcal{F})&\,:=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}})+\dfrac{1}{4}K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}+\sum_{p}\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),\end{aligned}\right.

(5.1)

where
N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is the normal bundle of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, and
\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
are defined in
Definition 2.6
.

We will prove in
Section 5.2
that the definition of Chern numbers is independent of the choices of the relatively minimal models,
i.e., one can use any relatively minimal model to compute the Chern
numbers.

Theorem 5.3
.

The following Noether equality holds for the three Chern numbers of a foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
:

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})+c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=12\chi(\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

Let
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
be a relatively minimal model of
(S,\mathcal{F})
.
To prove the Noether equality, we may assume that
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is pseudo-effective; otherwise all the three Chern numbers are zeros, and the Noether equality holds automatically.
By the Noether equality

c_{1}^{2}(S^{\prime})+c_{2}(S^{\prime})=12\chi(\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}})
for the projective surface
S^{\prime}

together with the Baum-Bott formula (cf.
[
Bru04
, Theorem 3.1]
) and (
2.4
), one obtains that

\displaystyle c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})+c_{2}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=(K_{S^{\prime}}+N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})^{2}+\sum_{p}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=K_{S^{\prime}}^{2}+c_{2}(S^{\prime})+2K_{S^{\prime}}N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}+N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}-c_{2}(S^{\prime})+\sum_{p}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=12\chi(\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}})+3K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}-N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}-m(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+\sum_{p}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=12\chi(\mathcal{O}_{S})+3K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}-\sum_{p}BB_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})-\sum_{p}m_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+\sum_{p}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=12\chi(\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}})+3K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}+12\sum_{p}\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=12\chi(\mathcal{F}).

The last two equalities follow from the definitions of
\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

in
Definition 2.6
and
\chi(\mathcal{F})
in (
5.1
).
∎

Remark 5.4
.

In the definition of

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})
and
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
for foliated surfaces with pseudo-effective divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
, we can assume that

p
runs over the singular points whose eigenvalues
\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}^{-}

are
negative rational numbers.

\left\{\begin{aligned} c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})&\,=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N^{\prime},\,\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}^{-}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N^{\prime},\,\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}^{-}}\beta(-\lambda_{p}),\\
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})&\,=\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime},\,\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}^{-}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime},\,\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}^{-}}\beta(-\lambda_{p}).\end{aligned}\right.

Because the other singular
points
q
satisfy
\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=0
by definition, which have no contributions
to
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})
and
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
.
In particular, saddle-nodes have no contributions to the Chern numbers.
This is reasonable because
the existence of a saddle-node implies that
\mathcal{F}
is non-algebraic,
and hence Poincaré problem is solved automatically.

5.2.
Birational invariance of the Chern numbers

In this subsection, we will prove that the Chern numbers are well-defined birational invariants.
By their definitions in
Definition 5.2
, if these Chern numbers are well-defined,
then they are naturally birationally invariants.
Let
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
(S^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
be two relatively minimal models of a given foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
,
both
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
(S^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
are relatively minimal, and birational to
(S,\mathcal{F})
.
According to
Lemma 5.1
, we may assume that
both the canonical divisors
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
are pseudo-effective.
By the Noether equality,
it suffices to prove that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N^{\prime\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime});\\
&\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}),\end{aligned}\right.

where
N^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime\prime}
are the nef parts in the Zariski decompositions of
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
respectively.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
be a relatively minimal foliated surface with pseudo-effective canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
as above.
Then

K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=\vol(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}).

Proof.

Let

K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=P^{\prime}+N^{\prime},

be the Zariski decomposition, where
P^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
are respectively the nef and negative parts.
Since
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is relatively minimal, the support of
N^{\prime}
is a disjoint union of maximal
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-chains by
Theorem 3.4
.
By contracting all such maximal
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-chains, one obtains a singular surface
S_{0}^{\prime}
with finitely many singularities of Hirzebruch-Jung type,
and hence we can write

N^{\prime}=\sum_{Q}N_{Q}^{\prime},

where the sum runs over all singularities
Q
’s on
S_{0}^{\prime}
, and
\mathrm{Supp}(N_{Q}^{\prime})
is supported on the inverse image of
Q
in
S
.
According to (
2.16
),

\vol(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=(P^{\prime})^{2}=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}-(N^{\prime})^{2}=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}-\sum_{Q}(N_{Q}^{\prime})^{2}.

In view of the definition of
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
in (
5.1
),
it suffices to prove that for each singularity
Q\in S_{0}^{\prime}
,

\sum_{p\in N_{Q}^{\prime}}\beta(-\lambda_{p})=-(N_{Q}^{\prime})^{2}.

(5.2)

The sum above is taken over all singularities of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
lying on the support of
N_{Q}^{\prime}
(namely the inverse image of
Q
).
To this aim, we first claim that

Claim 5.6
.

Let
C=C_{1}+C_{2}+\cdots+C_{r}
be a maximal
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-chain contracted to a singularity
Q\in S_{0}^{\prime}
.
Let
\mu_{0}=0
,
\mu_{1}=1
, and the rest
\mu_{k}
’s be given by the following recursion formula:

\mu_{k-1}-e_{k}\mu_{k}+\mu_{k+1}=0,\hskip 28.45274ptk=1,2,\cdots,r,

(5.3)

where
e_{k}=-C_{k}^{2}\geq 2
.
Then around each singularity
p_{k}\in C_{k}\cap C_{k+1}
(
1\leq k\leq r
),
there exists local coordinate
(x_{k},y_{k})
, such that

(i)

C_{k}
is locally defined by
x_{k}=0
, and
C_{k+1}
is locally defined by
y_{k}=1
;

(ii)

the foliation
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is locally defined by
\omega_{k}=\mu_{k}y_{k}dx_{k}+\mu_{k+1}x_{k}dy_{k}
.

Here
p_{r}
is the singularity on
C_{r}
different from the point
p_{r-1}=C_{r-1}\cap C_{r}
, and we understand that
C_{k+1}
is the local separatrix of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
passing through
p_{r}
other than
C_{r}
. In particular,
C_{r+1}
might not be algebraic, but only locally holomorphic.

Proof of
Claim 5.6
.

This is in fact implicitly contained in
[
Bru04
, § 8.2]
, the context following Definition 8.1 loc. cit.
One can prove inductively the statements hold for each singularity.
Consider first the case around the singularity
p_{1}=C_{1}\cap C_{2}
.
Because
C_{1}\setminus p_{1}
is simply connected,
the point
p_{1}
is a reduced non-degenerate singularity of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
with a separatrix inside
C_{1}
without holonomy.
Hence locally the foliation is of type
d(x_{1}y_{1}^{\ell_{1}})=0
, i.e.,
the foliation
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is defined around
p_{1}
by

\omega_{1}=y_{1}dx_{1}+\ell_{1}x_{1}dy_{1},

where
C_{1}=\{x_{1}=0\}
and
C_{2}=\{y_{1}=0\}
around
p_{1}
, and
\ell_{1}=e_{1}=-C_{1}^{2}
by the Camacho-Sad formula (
2.3
) and (
2.14
).
By (
5.3
),
\mu_{2}=\ell_{1}=e_{1}
.
This proves the statement for the singularity
p_{1}
.

Look now at the singularity
p_{2}
.
The separatrix
C_{2}
has
\mu_{2}
-periodic holonomy around
p_{2}
,
and hence locally the foliation is of type
d(x_{2}^{\mu_{2}}y_{2}^{\ell_{2}})=0
, i.e.,
the foliation
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is defined around
p_{2}
by

\omega_{2}=\mu_{2}y_{2}dx_{2}+\ell_{2}x_{2}dy_{2},

where
C_{2}=\{x_{2}=0\}
and
C_{3}=\{y_{2}=0\}
around
p_{2}
. By the Camacho-Sad formula (
2.3
) and (
2.14
) one has

e_{2}=-C_{2}^{2}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},C_{2})=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},C_{2},p_{1})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},C_{2},p_{2})=\frac{1}{\mu_{2}}+\frac{\ell_{2}}{\mu_{2}}.

Hence
\ell_{2}=\mu_{3}
in view of (
5.3
).
This proves the statement for the singularity
p_{2}
.
The rest cases can proved inductively and we omitted here.
∎

Come back to the proof of
Proposition 5.5
.
Suppose that
C=C_{1}+C_{2}+\cdots+C_{r}
is a maximal
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-chain contracted to a singularity
Q\in S_{0}^{\prime}
of type
A_{n,q}
.
Let
\xi_{i}
’s and
\mu_{i}
’s are defined respectively in
Lemma 3.5
and
Claim 5.6
.
Since
\mu_{0}=\xi_{r+1}=0
and
\mu_{1}=\xi_{r}=1
, one deduces inductively by (
3.3
) and (
5.3
) that

\mu_{k}=\xi_{r+1-k},\qquad\forall~0\leq k\leq r+1,

and that (recall that
\xi_{0}=n
and
\xi_{1}=q
as proved in
[
BHPV04
, § III.5]
)

\xi_{k}\mu_{k+1}-\xi_{k+1}\mu_{k}=n,\qquad\forall~0\leq k\leq r.

In particular,

\frac{\xi_{k}}{\mu_{k}}-\frac{\xi_{k+1}}{\mu_{k+1}}=\frac{n}{\mu_{k}\mu_{k+1}},\qquad\forall~1\leq k\leq r.

Taking the summation, we get

\sum_{k=1}^{r}\dfrac{1}{\mu_{k}\mu_{k+1}}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(\dfrac{\xi_{k}}{\mu_{k}}-\dfrac{\xi_{k+1}}{\mu_{k+1}}\right)=\dfrac{1}{n}\left(\dfrac{\xi_{1}}{\mu_{1}}-\dfrac{\xi_{r+1}}{\mu_{r+1}}\right)=\dfrac{q}{n}.

By
Claim 5.6
, the foliation
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is locally defined by
\omega_{k}=\mu_{k}y_{k}dx_{k}+\mu_{k+1}x_{k}dy_{k}
around
p_{k}
.
It follows that the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
at
p_{k}
is
\lambda_{p_{k}}=-\frac{\mu_{k+1}}{\mu_{k}}
.
Moreover, according to (
5.3
) one proves inductively that
\gcd(\mu_{k},\mu_{k+1})=1
.
Hence

\beta(-\lambda_{p_{k}})=\frac{1}{\mu_{k}\mu_{k+1}}
by (
2.5
).
Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in N_{Q}^{\prime}}\beta(-\lambda_{p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\beta(-\lambda_{p_{k}})=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\dfrac{1}{\mu_{k}\mu_{k+1}}=\dfrac{q}{n}=-(N_{Q}^{\prime})^{2}.

The last equality above follows from (
3.4
).
This proves (
5.2
) and hence completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface.
Then the first Chern number
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})
defined in
Definition 5.2
is a well-defined birational invariant satisfying
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})
, which is a non-negative rational number.

Proof.

Let
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
be any relatively minimal model of
(S,\mathcal{F})
.
If the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is not pseudo-effective, then the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
is not pseudo-effective for any relatively minimal model
(S^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
by
Lemma 5.1
. It follows that
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=0=\vol(\mathcal{F})
in this case.
If
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is pseudo-effective, then the corollary follows from
Proposition 5.5
and the fact that the volume
\vol(\mathcal{F})
is
a birational invariant, which is a non-negative rational number.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a reduced foliated surface (not necessarily relatively minimal)
with pseudo-effective canonical divisor.
Let

K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N,

be the Zariski decomposition, where
P
and
N
are respectively the nef and negative parts.
Then

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=K_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}).

In other words, one can compute the first Chern number
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})
over any reduced model (not necessarily relatively minimal).

Proof.

By contracting possible
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional curves on
S
, one can obtain a relatively minimal model.
Hence it is enough to prove that if
\sigma:\,S\to S^{\prime}
is a blowing-up centered at some point
p
, then

K_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F})=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),

(5.4)

where
(S,\mathcal{F})
and
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=(S^{\prime},\sigma_{*}\mathcal{F})
are both reduced, and
N
and
N^{\prime}
are respectively the negative parts of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
.
By construction, the exceptional curve
\mathcal{E}
is always
\mathcal{F}
-invariant (cf.
Lemma 2.7
) and

K_{\mathcal{F}}=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})+(1-\ell_{p})\mathcal{E},

where
\ell_{p}=0
(resp.
\ell_{p}=1
) if
p
is a regular point (resp. singularity).
Remark also that if
p
is a saddle-node, then it does not lie on the negative part
N
.
Hence (
5.4
) follows by
Lemma 2.7
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

One can prove similarly that

K_{\mathcal{F}}N_{\mathcal{F}}=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}N_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}},

for any two reduced foliated surfaces
(S,\mathcal{F})
and
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
birational to each other.
Suppose moreover that there are no saddle-nodes. Then
one shows also with the help of
Lemma 2.7
that

\sum_{p\in Sing(\mathcal{F})}\chi_{p}(\mathcal{F})=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in Sing(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})}\chi_{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),

for any two reduced foliated surfaces
(S,\mathcal{F})
and
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
birational to each other.
In other words, one can compute
\chi(\mathcal{F})
over any reduced model (not necessarily relatively minimal) if its reduced model has no saddle-nodes.
In particular,
\chi(\mathcal{F})
(as well as
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
by the Noether equality (
1.1
)) is well-defined for any arbitrary reduced foliation
\mathcal{F}
without saddle-nodes.
In general, we prove that
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
(and hence also
\chi(\mathcal{F})
) is well-defined
in the following.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
(S^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})
be two relatively minimal foliated surfaces birational to each other.
Then either

(i)

both
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
are non-pseudo-effective, in which case
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
by definition;

(ii)

both
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
are pseudo-effective, in which case

\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}).

(5.5)

In particular, the second Chern number
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
is well-defined for any arbitrary foliation
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

The two possibilities follow from
Lemma 5.1
.
And in the first case,
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
by definition.
It remains to prove (
5.5
), if both
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
are pseudo-effective.
According to
[
Bru04
, Theorem 5.1]
, a foliated surface admitting two different relatively minimal model only if it is either the
very special foliation

or
a Riccati foliation
.

Suppose first that both
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
are the very
special foliations,
then the eigenvalues on a singularity
p
away from the support of the negative parts are all non-rational, cf.
[
Bru04
, § 4.2 and Example 8.1]
.
It follows that

\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=0=\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}).

Consider next the case when both
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
are Riccati foliations.
Let
\varphi:\,S^{\prime}\dashrightarrow S^{\prime\prime}
be the birational map with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\varphi^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
.
Our aim is to prove that the birational map
\varphi
between
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
is
biholomorphic near any singular point whose eigenvalue
is a negative rational number.
Then according to the definitions (
5.1
) and (
2.5
), it follows that

\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=\sum_{p\not\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime})

as required.
Suppose on the contrary that the birational map
\varphi
is not
biholomorphic near some singular point
p^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{p^{\prime}}=-\frac{n^{\prime}}{m^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{-}
.
By exchanging
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
if possible, we may assume that
\varphi
is not defined at
p^{\prime}
. Let
\sigma^{\prime}:X\to S^{\prime}
be the minimal resolution of the
indeterminacies of
\varphi:S^{\prime}\dashrightarrow S^{\prime\prime}
. Then there is a
sequence of blowing-ups
\sigma^{\prime\prime}:X\to S^{\prime\prime}
such that

\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\varphi\circ\sigma^{\prime}
, and the pullback foliations on
X
are
the same,
\mathcal{G}:=(\sigma^{\prime\prime})^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}=(\sigma^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{S^{\prime\prime}}

By assumption, the exceptional curve
C
of the last blowing-up of

\sigma^{\prime}
above
p^{\prime}
is not contracted by
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover,
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
is not an
isomorphism near
C
; otherwise, as
C
is a
\mathcal{G}
-exceptional curve,

\sigma^{\prime\prime}(C)
would be an
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
-exceptional curve, which contradicts the relative minimality of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
.
Hence
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
can be factorized as

(X,\mathcal{G})\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}(X^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\overset{h}{\longrightarrow}(S^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}),

where
g
is the contraction of

(-1)
-curves disjoint from
C
.
Then
g
is a biholomorphism in a neighborhood of
C
,
and hence
C_{1}=g(C)
is a
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
-exceptional curve.
Moreover, since
h
is not an isomorphism near
C_{1}
by construction,

C_{1}
intersects another
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
-exceptional curve

C_{2}
at one point
q
.
According to the proof of
[
Bru04
, Theorem 5.1]
, one sees that
\#(C_{1}\cap C_{2})=1
,
and that the image
h(C_{1})
must be a fiber
F_{1}
of
f
,
where
f:\,S^{\prime\prime}\to B
is the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration attached to the Riccati foliation
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
such that the general fiber of
f
is transverse to
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
p^{\prime}
is a reduced singularity with negative rational eigenvalue
\lambda_{p^{\prime}}=-\frac{n^{\prime}}{m^{\prime}}
,
the eigenvalues of any possible singularities on
(\sigma^{\prime})^{-1}(p^{\prime})
are all negative rational numbers by
Lemma 2.7
(ii).
It follows that the image
h(C_{2})
would be a singularity
p
on
F_{1}
.
Since
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
is reduced, the eigenvalue at
p
is a negative rational eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}=-\frac{n}{m}
.
Since
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot F_{1}=F_{1}^{2}=0
, by the two formulas in
Proposition 2.3
one sees that there is exactly one another singularity
\tilde{p}
(other than
p
) on
F_{1}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{\tilde{p}}=-\lambda_{p}=\frac{n}{m}
. This gives a contradiction since
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
is assumed to be reduced.
∎

To end this subsection,
we prove a useful estimation on the lower bound of
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})

for a foliation with non-degenerate singularities.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
(S_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
be a foliated surface with pseudo-effective canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
, and

K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=P_{0}+N_{0},

be the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
.
Suppose that all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
are non-degenerate,
and denote by
T
the set of singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
which either lie on the support of
N_{0}
or are non-reduced.
Then

\vol(\mathcal{F})=c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in T}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0}).

(5.6)

Proof.

Let
\pi:\,S^{\prime}\to S_{0}
be a minimal resolution of non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
,
i.e., the induced foliation
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
is reduced and there is no
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
-exceptional curve contained in the inverse image of non-reduced singularities under
\pi
.
Of course,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
might not be relatively minimal. However it is reduced and birational to
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Hence one can compute the volume (or the first Chern number) using the reduced model
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
based on
Proposition 5.5
and
Corollary 5.8
.

Let

K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=P^{\prime}+N^{\prime},

(5.7)

be the Zariski decompositions of
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
.
We claim

Claim 5.12
.

(i). Let
p
be a reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
N_{0}
.
Then the inverse image
\pi^{-1}(p)
is a reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
lying on the negative part
N^{\prime}
.

(ii). Let
p
be a non-reduced degenerate singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Suppose that there is no saddle-node of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
lying on
\pi^{-1}(p)
.
Then all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
\pi^{-1}(p)
lie on the negative part
N^{\prime}
.

We first prove
Lemma 5.11
based on
Claim 5.12
, whose proof is postponed.
Let
p_{1},\cdots,p_{k}
be the non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
,
such that there is no saddle-node of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
lying on
\pi^{-1}(p_{i})
.
According to (
2.11
),

K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}E_{p_{i}},

(5.8)

where
E_{p_{i}}
is the last exceptional curve lying on
\pi^{-1}(p_{i})
.
Note that
\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})<0
for any non-reduced non-degenerate singularity
p
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Hence it suffices to prove

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}+\sum_{\text{$p$~is~reduced~}\in N_{0}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{F}_{0}).

(5.9)

By definition,
\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\geq 0
for any reduced singularity
p
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
Hence by
Corollary 5.8
and
Claim 5.12
together with (
2.12
),

\displaystyle\vol(\mathcal{F})=c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}}\beta_{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}^{2}+\sum_{\text{$p$~is~reduced~}\in N_{0}}\beta_{\pi^{-1}(p)}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(p_{i})}\beta_{p^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\big(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}-k\big)+\sum_{\text{$p$~is~reduced~}\in N_{0}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(\beta_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{F}_{0})+1\big)

\displaystyle=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}+\sum_{\text{$p$~is~reduced~}\in N_{0}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{F}_{0}).

In the above, if
p\in N_{0}
is a reduced singularity,
then
\pi
is an isomorphism around
p
, and hence
\beta_{\pi^{-1}(p)}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
.
This proves (
5.9
), and hence also (
5.6
).
It remains to prove
Claim 5.12
.
∎

Proof of
Claim 5.12
.

(i). Consider the Zariski decompositions of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
and
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
in (
5.7
).
By (
5.8
),

\pi^{*}P_{0}+\pi^{*}N_{0}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}E_{k}=P^{\prime}+N^{\prime}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}E_{p_{i}}.

Note that the first equality is just the Zariski decomposition of
\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
.
It follows that
P^{\prime}\leq\pi^{*}P_{0}
, or equivalently,

\pi^{*}N_{0}\leq N^{\prime}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}E_{p_{i}}.

In particular, the inverse image of any reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
N_{0}

is still reduced (since
\pi
is an isomorphism around reduced singularities) lying on
\text{Supp}\big(N^{\prime}\big)
.

(ii). This is in fact follows from the resolution process of a non-reduced degenerate singularity presented above
Lemma 2.8
.
Indeed, fixing
p=p_{i}
,
the inverse image
\pi^{-1}(p)
consists of a chain of rational curves

C_{1}+\cdots+C_{n}
with
C_{j}=E_{p_{i}}
being the last exceptional curve.
Then there is no singularity of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
lying on
C_{j}=E_{p_{i}}
, and

C_{j-1}\cdot K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=C_{j-1}\cdot\Big(\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}E_{p_{i}}\Big)=-C_{j-1}C_{j}=-1.

Hence
C_{j-1}\subseteq\text{Supp}(N^{\prime})
.
Suppose
N^{\prime}=b_{j-1}C_{j-1}+N_{j-1}^{\prime}

Thus

0\leq C_{j-2}\cdot P^{\prime}=C_{j-2}\cdot(K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}-N^{\prime})=-C_{j-2}N^{\prime}<-C_{j-2}\cdot N_{j-1}^{\prime}.

Hence
C_{j-2}\subseteq\text{Supp}(N_{j-1}^{\prime})\subseteq\text{Supp}(N^{\prime})
.
One proves then inductively that
C_{s}\subseteq\text{Supp}(N^{\prime})
for any
1\leq s\leq j-1
.
Similarly one can prove inductively that
C_{s}\subseteq\text{Supp}(N^{\prime})
for any
j+1\leq s\leq n
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

Let

T^{\prime}=\big\{p\in\text{Sing}(\mathcal{F}_{0})~\big|~p\in N_{0},\text{~or $p$ is non-reduced and there is no saddle-node in $\pi^{-1}(p)$}\big\},

where
\sigma:\,S^{\prime}\to S_{0}
is a minimal resolution of the non-reduced singularity
p
. Then
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
, and
T^{\prime}=T
if
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is algebraically integral.
We have in fact proved in the above that

\vol(\mathcal{F})=c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in T^{\prime}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0}).

5.3.
The Chern numbers of an algebraically integral foliation

In this subsection, we compute the Chern numbers of an algebraically integral foliation
\mathcal{F}
.
The main purpose
is to prove that the Chern numbers are exactly the modular
invariants of the corresponding fibration.
Since the Chern numbers are birational invariants,
we may thus always assume that
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal in this subsection.
A relatively algebraically integral minimal foliation
\mathcal{F}
is defined by a normal-crossing
fibration
f:S\to B
of genus
g\geq 0
.
If
g=0
, then by definition the Chern numbers are all zero, and all the modular invariants of
f
are also zero.
Thus we may assume
g\geq 1
in the following.

Let
f:\,S\to B
be a surface fibration defining a relatively minimal foliation
\mathcal{F}

by taking the saturation of
\ker(df:\,T_{S}\to f^{*}T_{B})
in
T_{S}
.
Let
F
be a singular fiber of
f
. Then
F
is a
normal-crossing divisor containing no redundant
(-1)
-curves. Let

p
be a singular point of
F_{\rm red}
. Then
(F,p)
is defined
locally by
x^{a}y^{b}=0
for some positive integers
a,b
. Let

\beta_{p}:=\frac{\gcd(a,b)^{2}}{ab},\hskip 28.45274pt\beta_{F}:=\sum_{p\in F}\beta_{p}.

Locally around
p
, the corresponding foliation
\mathcal{F}
is defined by
d(x^{a}y^{b})=0
,
or equivalently by
\omega=aydx+bxdy=0
.
As a singular point of
\mathcal{F}
, the eigenvalue

\lambda_{p}=-\frac{a}{b}\in\mathbb{Q}^{-}
. Thus

\beta_{p}=\beta(-\lambda_{p})=\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}).

We can also talk about the maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chains in the fibers. We
use
p\in N
to denote a singular point on some maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chain.
We define

c_{-1}(F)=\sum_{p\in F\cap N}\beta(-\lambda_{p}).

(5.10)

Let

\mu_{F}=\sum\limits_{p\in F}\mu_{p}(F_{\rm red}),\qquad\alpha_{F}=\sum_{p\in F}(m_{p}(F_{\rm red})-2)^{2},

where
\mu_{p}(F_{\rm red})
is the Milnor number of the reduced part
F_{\rm red}
at
p
,

m_{p}(F_{\rm red})
is the multiplicity of
F_{\rm red}
at
p
, and the sums run
over all singular points of
F_{\rm red}
.
The number
\mu_{F}
is called the total Milnor
number of the singular points of
F_{\rm red}
.
Because
F
is normal-crossing, it follows that

\mu_{F}=\sum_{p\in F}1=\#\{\text{singularities of $F_{\rm red}$}\},\qquad\alpha_{F}=0.

(5.11)

In
[
Tan96
, Definition II]
, the local Chern
numbers
c_{1}^{2}(F)
,
c_{2}(F)
and
\chi_{F}
are introduced for a fiber
F
.
They satisfy Noether’s equality

\chi_{F}=\frac{1}{12}(c_{1}^{2}(F)+c_{2}(F)).

Moreover, we have the following formulas to compute them (cf.
[
Tan96
, Theorem 3.1]
)

\begin{cases}c_{1}^{2}(F)=4(g-p_{a}(F_{\rm red}))+F_{\rm red}^{2}+\alpha_{F}-c_{-1}(F),&\\
c_{2}(F)=2(g-p_{a}(F_{\rm red}))+\mu_{F}-\beta_{F}+c_{-1}(F),&\end{cases}

where
p_{a}(F_{\rm red})
is the arithmetic genus of
F_{\rm red}
.
These local Chern numbers are closely related to the modular invariants recalled in
Section 4.1
,
cf. the corollary after
[
Tan96
, Theorem A]
.

\left\{\begin{aligned} \kappa(f)&\,=K_{S}^{2}-8(g-1)(g(B)-1)-\sum_{F}c_{1}^{2}(F)=K_{f}^{2}-\sum_{F}c_{1}^{2}(F),\\
\delta(f)&\,=c_{2}(S)-4(g-1)(g(B)-1)-\sum_{F}c_{2}(F)=e_{f}-\sum_{F}c_{2}(F),\\
\chi(f)&\,=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{S})-(g-1)(g(B)-1)-\sum_{F}\chi_{F}=\chi_{f}-\sum_{F}\chi_{F}.\end{aligned}\right.

We refer to
[
LT13
]
for more properties of these local Chern numbers.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
f:\,S\to B
be a surface fibration of genus
g\geq 1
defining a relatively minimal foliation
\mathcal{F}

by taking the saturation of
\ker(df:\,T_{S}\to f^{*}T_{B})
in
T_{S}
.
Then the Chern numbers of
\mathcal{F}
are exactly the modular Chern numbers of the surface fibration
f
,

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\kappa(f),\hskip 14.22636ptc_{2}(\mathcal{F})=\delta(f),\hskip 14.22636pt\chi(\mathcal{F})=\lambda(f).

Proof.

Let

\Delta(f)=\sum\limits_{F}(F-F_{\rm red})
be the discriminant divisor of

f
, where the sum runs over all singular fibers.
By (
4.2
),

K_{\mathcal{F}}=K_{S/B}-\Delta(f),\qquad N_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}=K_{S}-K_{\mathcal{F}}=f^{*}K_{B}+\Delta(f).

Note that
\Delta(f)^{2}=\sum\limits_{F}F_{\rm red}^{2}
,
and that the support of the negative part
N
of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is contained in singular fibers of
f
.
By (
5.10
) and (
5.11
),

\displaystyle\sum_{F}c_{1}^{2}(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{F}4(g-p_{a}(F_{\rm red}))+\sum_{F}F_{\rm red}^{2}-\sum_{F}\sum_{p\in F\cap N}\beta(-\lambda_{p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{F}2K_{S}(F-F_{\rm red})-\sum_{F}F_{\rm red}^{2}-\sum_{p\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p})

\displaystyle=2K_{S/B}\cdot\Delta(f)-\Delta(f)^{2}-\sum_{p\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p}).

Hence

\displaystyle\kappa(f)

\displaystyle=K_{f}^{2}-\sum_{F}c_{1}^{2}(F)=K_{S/B}^{2}-2K_{S/B}\cdot\Delta(f)+\Delta(f)^{2}+\sum_{p\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p})

\displaystyle=(K_{S/B}-\Delta(f))^{2}+\sum_{p\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p})=K_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p})=c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}).

Similarly,

\displaystyle c_{2}(F)

\displaystyle=2(g-p_{a}(F_{\rm red}))+\mu_{F}-\sum_{p\in F,\,p\not\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p})=e_{F}-\sum_{p\in F,\,p\not\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p}),

where we use the following formula (cf.
[
Tan94
, Formula (7) on p.229]
):

e_{F}:=\chi_{\rm top}(F_{\rm red})+2g-2=2(g-p_{a}(F_{\rm red}))+\mu_{F}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\delta(f)

\displaystyle=e_{f}-\sum_{F}c_{2}(F)=\sum_{F}(e_{F}-c_{2}(F))

\displaystyle=\sum_{F}\sum_{p\in F,\,p\not\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p})=\sum_{p\not\in N}\beta(-\lambda_{p})=c_{2}(\mathcal{F}).

Finally, by Noether’s equalities (
1.1
) and (
4.3
),
we get
\lambda(f)=\chi(\mathcal{F})
.
∎

6.
Foliations of non-general type

In this section, we compute the Chern numbers of foliations of non-general type.
By the birational invariance of the Chern numbers, we may consider only the relatively minimal foliated surfaces.
It depends on the classification of the foliated surface of non-general type; see the tabular in
Section 2.3
.

Theorem 1.3
will be proved at the end of this section;
while
Corollary 1.4
is a direct consequence whose proof will be thus omitted.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an algebraically integral relatively minimal foliation of non-general type. Then

\left\{\begin{aligned} &c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=12\chi(\mathcal{F})=0,&&\text{if $\mathcal{F}$ is induced by an isotrivial fibration of genus $g\geq 0$};\\
&c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=12\chi(\mathcal{F})>0,&&\text{if $\mathcal{F}$ is induced by a non-isotrivial fibration of genus $g=1$}.\end{aligned}\right.

(6.1)

Proof.

By (
1.2
), the Chern numbers of
\mathcal{F}
are equal to the corresponding modular invariants of the fibration
f
.
When the fibration
f
is isotrivial, then all these modular invariants are zero by definition.
When
f
is a non-isotrivial fibration of genus
g=1
,
it is well-known that
\delta(f)=12\lambda(f)\neq 0
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Hilbert modular foliation. Then

c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

Proof.

We first briefly recall the construction of Hilbert modular foliations,
and refer to
[
Bru04
, § 9.5]
and
[
Bru03
, § 4]
for more details.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be the unit disc,
and
\Gamma\subseteq\text{Aut}(\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D})=PSL(2,\mathbb{R})\times PSL(2,\mathbb{R})

be a subgroup such that it does not contain any finite index subgroup of type

\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}
with
\Gamma_{i}\subseteq PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
.
The quotient surface
\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}/\Gamma
admits at most quotient singularities, resolved by Hirzebruch-Jung chains of rational curves (denoted by
D_{i}
’s).
This quotient may not be compact, but it can be compactified by adding one or more cycles of rational curves (denoted by
E_{j}
’s).
By a minimal resolution of these possible singularities and compactification,
one obtains a smooth projective surface
S
, which is called a Hilbert modular surface.
There are two natural foliations, denoted by
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
respectively,
arising from the vertical and the horizontal ones on
\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}
.
Both foliations will be referred as Hilbert modular foliations.
The Hirzebruch-Jung chains
D_{i}
’s and
the cycles of rational curves
E_{j}
’s are the only
compact leaves of both
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
.
Moreover, both foliations are reduced whose singularities are all non-degenerate and equal to the nodes of
D_{i}
’s and
E_{j}
’s.
These Hirzebruch–Jung chains
D_{i}
’s are maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chains and
\mathcal{G}
-chains in sense of
Definition 3.3
.
Outside
D_{i}
’s and
E_{j}
’s,
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are transverse to each other.
Hence one has

K_{\mathcal{F}}=N_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(H),

where
H
is a positive integral divisor whose support is equal to the union of
these compact leaves
D_{i}
’s and
E_{j}
’s.

By the above construction, the singularities of
\mathcal{F}
are exactly the nodes of
D_{i}
’s and
E_{j}
’s, and the Hirzebruch-Jung chains
D_{i}
’s are maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chains.
It follows that the singularities of
\mathcal{F}
which are not on the support of
N
are exactly the nodes of
E_{j}
’s, where
N
is the negative part of the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N
.
By the definition of the second Chern number
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
in
Definition 5.2
,
it is enough to prove that for any node
p
of some cycle
E_{j}
of rational curves,

\lambda_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\},

(6.2)

where
\lambda_{p}
is the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}
at
p
.

Let
E_{j}=C_{1}+C_{2}+\cdots+C_{r}
be a cycle of rational curve as follows.

C_{1}
C_{2}
C_{3}
C_{r}
p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
p_{4}
p_{r}

By
Lemma 2.8
, we may assume that

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C_{i},p_{i})=\lambda_{i},\qquad\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C_{i-1},p_{i})=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}},\qquad\forall\,1\leq i\leq r,

where
\lambda_{i}
is the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}
at
p_{i}
,
and we under stand the subscript
0
as
r
(resp.
r+1
as
1
):
for instance, we understand
C_{0}=C_{r}
.
Note that
\mathcal{F}
is reduced. Hence
\lambda_{i}
can not be a positive rational number.
We will prove by contradiction that
\lambda_{i}
can not be negative rational number either.

Suppose that there exists some
1\leq j\leq r
, such that the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i_{0}}=-\frac{m_{i_{0}}}{n_{i_{0}}}
is a negative rational number with
m_{i_{0}}\geq 1,n_{i_{0}}\geq 1
and
\gcd(m_{i_{0}},n_{i_{0}})=1
.
By the formula (
2.3
),

\lambda_{i}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{i+1}}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C_{i},p_{i})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F},C_{i},p_{i+1})=C_{i}^{2}\leq-2,\qquad\forall\,1\leq i\leq r.

(6.3)

If
\lambda_{i_{0}}\geq-1
, then one show by induction that
\lambda_{i}
is a negative rational number with
\lambda_{i}\geq-1
for
j\leq i\leq r+1
.
In particular,
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{r+1}\geq-1
.
Then by repeatedly using (
6.3
), one shows that every eigenvalue

\lambda_{i}
is a negative rational number with
\lambda_{i}\geq-1
.
If
\lambda_{i_{0}}\leq-1
, then one show by induction that
\lambda_{i}
is a negative rational number with
\lambda_{i}\leq-1
for
0\leq i\leq j
.
In particular,
\lambda_{r}=\lambda_{0}\leq-1
.
Similarly, one shows that every eigenvalue

\lambda_{i}
is a negative rational number with
\lambda_{i}\leq-1
.
In any case, one shows that every eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}=-\frac{m_{i}}{n_{i}}
is a negative rational number with
m_{i}\geq 1,n_{i}\geq 1
and
\gcd(m_{i},n_{i})=1
.
Let
e_{i}=-C_{i}^{2}\geq 2
. Then by (
6.3
), for every
1\leq i\leq r
, it holds

\frac{m_{i}}{n_{i}}+\frac{n_{i+1}}{m_{i+1}}=e_{i},\quad\Longrightarrow\quad m_{i}m_{i+1}+n_{i}n_{i+1}=n_{i}m_{i+1}e_{i}.

Since
\gcd(m_{i},n_{i})=1
and
n_{i}
divides both
n_{i}n_{i+1}
and
n_{i}m_{i+1}e_{i}
,
it follows that
n_{i}
divides
m_{i+1}
.
Similarly, one shows that
m_{i+1}
divides
n_{i}
.
Hence
m_{i+1}=n_{i}
, and the above equality becomes

n_{i-1}+n_{i+1}=n_{i}e_{i},\qquad\forall\,1\leq i\leq r.

Let
D=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}C_{i}
be an effective divisor supported on
E_{j}=C_{1}+C_{2}+\cdots+C_{r}
.
The above relation shows that
D\cdot C_{i}=0
for any
1\leq i\leq r
.
On the other hand, it is known that the intersection matrix
\big(C_{i}\cdot C_{j}\big)
is negatively definite, cf.
[
Bru03
, p.146]
or
[
Bru04
, p.124]
.
This gives a contradiction,
and the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a transcendental Riccati foliation. Then

c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

Proof.

First, recall that a (transcendental) Riccati foliation
(S,\mathcal{F})
is a foliation
associated with a rational fibration
\pi:S\to B
whose general
fiber
F
is transverse to
\mathcal{F}
. Every Riccati foliation has a
standard form (
[
Bru04
, Proposition 4.2]
) where
\pi

is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle. The singularities of
\mathcal{F}
are on one of the
invariant fibers
F
of the following forms.

I
λ
)

Non-degenerate fiber:

F

admits two non-degenerate singularities with eigenvalues
\pm\lambda\neq 0
.

II)

Semi-degenerate fiber:

F
admits two saddle-nodes of the same multiplicity, or one
saddle-node of multiplicity two.

III)

Nilpotent fiber:

F
admits only one nilpotent singularity.

Based on
Lemma 2.7
(ii), by choosing proper flippings, one can further assume that
the non-degenerate fibers I
λ
satisfy

0\leq\mathrm{Re}\,\lambda\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Then the minimal
resolution
\sigma:\,\widetilde{S}\to S
of this standard model is a relatively minimal
model
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
(relatively minimal model for a Riccati foliation may not be unique!), and the canonical divisor
K_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}
is described explicitly
below
[
Bru04
, Proposition 4.2]
. Based on this relatively
minimal model, one sees that any singular point
p
with

\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}^{-}
is contained in
\widetilde{N}
, the negative part of
the Zariski decomposition
K_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\widetilde{P}+\widetilde{N}
.
Indeed, any fiber admitting a non-reduced singularity is either of type I
λ
) of type III).
Let
F_{1},\cdots,F_{s}
be all such fibers, and let
\widetilde{F}_{i}
be its corresponding fiber in its relatively minimal model as above.
Then there is exists a unique component
C_{i}\subseteq\widetilde{F}_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq s
(cf. the text at the end of
[
Bru04
, § 4.1]
), such that

K_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}})-\sum_{i=1}^{s}C_{i}=\tilde{\pi}^{*}(\mathcal{L})-\sum_{i=1}^{s}C_{i}=\widetilde{P}+\widetilde{N},

where

\widetilde{P}=\tilde{\pi}^{*}(\mathcal{L})-\sum_{i=1}^{s}\frac{\widetilde{F}_{i}}{m_{i}},\qquad\widetilde{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{F}_{i}}{m_{i}}-C_{i}\Big),

where
m_{i}
is the multiplicity of
C_{i}
in
\widetilde{F}_{i}
.
This shows that all the singularities on
\widetilde{F}_{i}
lie on the negative part
\widetilde{N}
as required.
By
Proposition 5.10
, one can compute the second Chern number using this relatively minimal model.
Thus
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=c_{2}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})=0
by definition.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a transcendental turbulent foliation. Then

c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is a turbulent foliation,
there is an isotrivial elliptic fibration
\pi:\,S\to B
whose general
fiber
F
is transverse to
\mathcal{F}
, equivalently,
K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot F=0
.

We can choose a model such that
\pi
is relatively minimal
normal-crossing fibration, i.e., the singular fibers

F_{1},\cdots,F_{s}
of
\pi
are normal-crossing divisors without
redundant
(-1)
-curves, see the list in
[
Bru04
, § 4.3,
p.57]
. Then the singularities of
\mathcal{F}
are reduced and lie on the nodes of the singular fibers.
Contracting the
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional curves in the fiber, we get a
relatively minimal model, still denoted by
\mathcal{F}
by abuse of notations.
We claim that all the singularities of the
relatively minimal model
\mathcal{F}
are in the negative part
N
of the Zariski
decomposition
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N
.
Indeed, let
F_{i,red}
be the reduced part of the singular fiber
F_{i}
.
Then

K_{\mathcal{F}}=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{L})+\sum_{i=1}^{s}F_{i,red}=P+N,

where

P=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{L})+\sum_{i=1}^{s}\frac{F_{i}}{m_{i}},\qquad N=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\Big(F_{i,red}-\frac{F_{i}}{m_{i}}\Big),\qquad\text{with~$m_{i}=\max\limits_{j}\{m_{ij}\}$ if $F_{i}=\sum\limits_{j}m_{ij}C_{ij}$.}

Note that each
F_{i}
can not be of type
I_{b}
or
I_{b}^{*}
with
b\geq 1
;
see the argument on
[
Bru04
, § 4.3,
p.57-58]
.
According to the list in
[
Bru04
, § 4.3,
p.57]
,
for each singular fiber
F_{i}
, there is at most one irreducible component
C_{ij}\subseteq F_{i}
with
C_{ij}\not\subseteq\text{Supp}(N)
.
In particular, all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}
on
F_{i}
are contained in
N
as required.
Thus
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
by definition.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a transcendental foliation generated by a global vector field. Then

c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is generated by a global vector field
v
,
it follows that the attached surface
S
is of non-general type, cf.
[
Bru04
, § 6.3]
.
Moreover, if
S
has non-negative Kodaira dimension, the vector field
v

has no zeros; equivalently,
\mathcal{F}
has no singularities and hence
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
automatically by definition.
We assume next that
S
has negative Kodaira dimension, i.e.,
S
is a ruled surface.
Note that
\mathcal{F}
is transcendental by assumption.
In particular,
\mathcal{F}
is not induced by a rational fibration.
According to
[
Bru04
, Proposition 6.6]
,

\mathcal{F}
is transverse to the ruling on
S
,
i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
is a Riccati foliation.
Hence
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
by
Lemma 6.3
.
∎

We can now prove
Theorem 1.3
.
The proof makes use of the classification of foliations of non-general type;
see the tabular in
Section 2.3
.

Proof of
Theorem 1.3
.

First,
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
since
\mathcal{F}
is of non-general type and
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})
by
Theorem 1.2
(iii).
This proves (i).
If
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral, (ii) follows from
Lemma 6.1
.
Thus, it remains to prove
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
if
\mathcal{F}
is transcendental in view of the Noether equality (
1.1
).

If
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})=-\infty
, then
\mathcal{F}
is a Hilbert modular foliation, and
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
by
Lemma 6.2
.

If
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})=0
, by
[
Bru04
, Theorem 8.2]
and its poof, there exists a ramified cover
\pi^{\prime}:\,S^{\prime}\to S
together with a minimal resolution of singularities
\sigma:\,\widetilde{S}\to S^{\prime}
,
such that the induced foliation
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{F})
is birational to a foliation generated by a global section with isolated zeros,
where
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\circ\sigma
.
Moreover, the cover
\pi^{\prime}
is branched over
\text{Supp}(N)
,
where
N
is the negative part of the Zariski decomposition
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N
.
According to
Lemma 6.5
,
c_{2}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})=0
.
In other words, any non-degenerated singularity
\tilde{p}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{\tilde{p}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{<0}
lies on the negative part
\widetilde{N}

of the Zariski decomposition
K_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\widetilde{P}+\widetilde{N}
.
Note also that
K_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\pi^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}})
according to the proof of
[
Bru04
, Theorem 8.2]
.
It follows that any non-degenerated singularity
p
of
\mathcal{F}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}_{<0}
lies on
N
;
otherwise, such singularity
p
would produces
d=\deg(\pi)
singularities with the same
eigenvalues and do not lie on
\widetilde{N}
.
Hence
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
by definition.

Finally, if
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})=1
, then
\mathcal{F}
must be either a Riccati or a Turbulent foliation according to the classification of foliations of non-general type.
Thus
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=0
by
Lemma 6.3
and
Lemma 6.4
.
∎

7.
The slope inequalities for a foliation of general type

In this section, we consider the slope inequalities for a foliation
\mathcal{F}
of general type, and prove
Theorem 1.5
and
Corollary 1.6
.
The slope of
\mathcal{F}
is defined as

\lambda(\mathcal{F}):=\frac{c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})}{\chi(\mathcal{F})}.

We remark that if
\mathcal{F}
is of general type, then
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})>0
and also
\chi(\mathcal{F})>0
by the Noether equality (
1.1
) with the non-negativity of
c_{2}(\mathcal{F})
.
Hence the slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})
is well-defined and
0<\lambda(\mathcal{F})\leq 12
.

Proof of
Theorem 1.5
.

This follows from
Theorem 1.2
(iv) and the slope inequality for a semi-stable family of curves by Cornalba-Harris-Xiao (cf.
[
CH88
,
Xia87
]
).
Indeed,
by the stable reduction theorem (cf.
[
DM69
]
),
there exists a base change
\phi:\widetilde{B}\to B
of finite degree, possibly ramified,
such that the pull-back fibration
\tilde{f}:\widetilde{S}\to\widetilde{B}
is semi-stable.
We refer to
Section 4.1
for the explanation of the base change process.
Moreover, by
Theorem 1.2
(iv), it holds

K_{\tilde{f}}^{2}=\deg(\phi)\cdot c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}),\qquad\chi_{\tilde{f}}=\deg(\pi)\cdot\chi(\mathcal{F}).

Thus by the slope inequality for the semi-stable fibration
\tilde{f}
(cf.
[
CH88
,
Xia87
]
),
it follows that

\lambda(\mathcal{F})=\frac{c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})}{\chi(\mathcal{F})}=\frac{K_{\tilde{f}}^{2}}{\chi_{\tilde{f}}}\geq\frac{4(g-1)}{g}.\qed

Proof of
Corollary 1.6
.

Both statements follow from the slope inequality (
1.3
).
Indeed, if
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral with
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<4
, then
\lambda(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{4(g-1)}{g}=4-\frac{4}{g}
; equivalently the inequality (
1.4
) holds.
Moreover, since
g\geq 2
, it follows that
\lambda(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{4(g-1)}{g}\geq 2
if
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral. This proves (ii).
∎

To end this section, we construct a transcendental foliation of general type whose slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<2
.

Example 7.1
.

In this example, we construct a relatively minimal foliation
\mathcal{F}
of general type whose slope is

\lambda(\mathcal{F})=\frac{12}{7}<2.

Let
X_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
\mathcal{G}_{0}
be the foliation on
S_{0}
defined by
the global vector field
v_{0}=x^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
,
where
x
and
y
are respectively the affine coordinates of the factors
\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
X_{0}
.
One checks easily that
K_{\mathcal{G}_{0}}=0
, and there are exactly two singularities

p_{0}=(0,0)
and
p_{\infty}=(0,\infty)
, both of which are saddle-nodes of multiplicity two.
In particular,
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is relatively minimal.

Let
D=\{y+x^{2}(1+y^{2})=0\}
,
C_{0}=\{y=0\}
and
C_{\infty}=\{y=\infty\}
.
Then
R_{0}=D+C_{0}+C_{\infty}
is clearly an even divisor and decides a double cover

\pi_{0}:\,S_{0}\to X_{0}
,
whose branched divisor is exactly
R_{0}
.
By a canonical resolution of the singularities of
R_{0}
, one obtains a smooth surface
S
.

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{S_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{X_{0}}

The two points
\{p_{0},p_{\infty}\}
are the two singularities of
R_{0}
, and the resolution of these two singularities are exhibited as follows.

C_{0}
C_{\infty}
