q_{8}
q_{1}
q_{3}
q_{2}
q_{4}

In the above,
F_{0}=\{x=0\}
, and

\overline{F}_{0},\overline{D},\overline{C}_{0},\overline{C}_{\infty}
are respectively the strict transforms of
F_{0},D,C_{0},C_{\infty}
.
A solid curve is branched; while a dotted line is not branched.
One of the dotted line is
\overline{F}_{0}
, and the rest four dotted ones are the exceptional curves of
\sigma
.
The points
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}
are the four points on
D
with

\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{G}_{0},D,p_{1})=\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{G}_{0},D,p_{2})=\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{G}_{0},D,p_{3})=\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{G}_{0},D,p_{4})=1,

and
\{q_{i}=\sigma^{-1}(p_{i}),~1\leq i\leq 4\}
are the inverse images on
\overline{D}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{G}_{0})
and
\mathcal{F}=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{G})
be the induced foliations.
Then
K_{\mathcal{G}}=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{G}_{0}})=0
.
Note that the two branched curves
C_{0},C_{\infty}
are
\mathcal{G}_{0}
-invariant,
and
D
is not
\mathcal{G}_{0}
-invariant.
Hence

K_{\mathcal{F}}=\pi^{*}\big(K_{\mathcal{G}}+\overline{D}/2\big)=\pi^{*}(\overline{D})/2.

(7.1)

It follows immediately that
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is nef with
K_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}=\frac{\overline{D}^{2}}{2}=2
.

Let’s next compute the singularities of
\mathcal{F}
.
First,
\mathcal{G}_{0}
admits exactly two singularities
\{p_{0},p_{\infty}\}
, both of which are saddle-nodes as mentioned above.
The birational map
\sigma
is composed of four blowing-ups.
By
Lemma 2.7
(iv) and its proof, one checks immediately that

\mathcal{G}
admits exactly six singularities
\{q_{0},q_{\infty},q_{5},q_{6},q_{7},q_{8}\}
.
The two singularities
\{q_{0},q_{\infty}\}
are saddle-nodes, and the rest four singularities
\{q_{5},q_{6},q_{7},q_{8}\}
are non-degenerate with eigenvalues all equal to
-1
.
Besides the two saddle-nodes
\{q_{0},q_{\infty}\}
with

\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{G},\overline{D},q_{0})=\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{G},\overline{D},q_{\infty})=2,

there are four tangent points of
\mathcal{G}
on the branch divisor
\overline{R}=\overline{D}+\overline{C}_{0}+\overline{C}_{\infty}
, which are
q_{i}=\sigma^{-1}(p_{i})
(
1\leq i\leq 4
) with

\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{G},\overline{D},q_{i})=1,\qquad\forall~1\leq i\leq 4.

Thus
\mathcal{F}
admits
14
singularities, which are the inverse images of
\{q_{i}\}
’s with
0\leq i\leq 8
and
i=\infty
.
It is clear that
\pi^{-1}(q_{i})
is still a saddle-node for
i=0
or
\infty
,
and that
\pi^{-1}(q_{i})
consists two singularities with the same eigenvalues both equal to
-1
for
5\leq i\leq 8
.
Moreover, a local computation (cf.
[
Bru04
, Example 3.4]
) shows that

\pi^{-1}(q_{i})
is a reduced singularity with eigenvalue equal to
-1
for
1\leq i\leq 4
.
In conclusion,
\mathcal{F}
admits
14
singularities, two of which are saddle-nodes,
and twelve of which are reduced with eigenvalues all equal to
-1
.

We claim that
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.
Note first that there exist exactly three
\mathcal{G}_{0}
-invariant curves, which are
C_{0},C_{\infty},F_{0}
.
Indeed, for any possible
\mathcal{G}_{0}
-invariant curve
C
, other than
\{C_{0},C_{\infty},F_{0}\}
,
the intersection
C\cap F_{0}
would be singularities of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
.
Hence
C\cap F_{0}\subseteq\{p_{0},p_{\infty}\}
.
However, there are at most two separatrices through a saddle-node.
This shows that such a
\mathcal{G}_{0}
-invariant curve
C
does not exist.
It follows that all the
\mathcal{G}
-invariant curves are

\{\overline{F}_{0},\overline{C}_{0},\overline{C}_{\infty},\overline{E}_{1},E_{2},\overline{E}_{3},E_{4}\}.

Hence all the
\mathcal{F}
-invariant curves are the inverse images of the above seven curves.
On the other hand, let
C
be any possible
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional curve.
Then
C
is an
\mathcal{F}
-invariant smooth rational curve, and
either there is one singularity
p
on
C
with
Z(\mathcal{F},C,p)=1
,
or there is two singularities
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
on
C
with
Z(\mathcal{F},C,p)=Z(\mathcal{F},C,p^{\prime})=1
.
By a direct computation, one checks that
inverse images of the above seven
\mathcal{G}
-invariant curves are not
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional.
This proves that
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.

Finally, let’s compute the Chern numbers of
\mathcal{F}
.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal and
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is nef, it follows that

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=K_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}=2.

Moreover, the nefness of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
implies that the negative part in the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is
N=0
.
Hence by
Definition 5.2
,

c_{2}(\mathcal{F})=\sum_{q\not\in N}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F})=\sum_{q}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F})=12.

By (
1.1
), one obtains
\chi(\mathcal{F})=\frac{7}{6}
, and hence

\lambda(\mathcal{F})=\frac{c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})}{\chi(\mathcal{F})}=\frac{12}{7}.

Remark 7.2
.

We refer to
[
LLTX24
]
for more examples of transcendental foliations with slope
\lambda(\mathcal{F})<2
.

8.
The Noether inequalities for a foliation of general type

In this section, we study the Noether type inequalities for a foliation of general type, and prove
Theorem 1.9
;
while both
Corollary 1.10
and
Corollary 1.11
are direct consequence of
Theorem 1.9
, and the proofs of these two corollaries will be omitted.

As the invariants involved in
Theorem 1.9
are birational invariants,
we may assume that the foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
is reduced (or even relatively minimal if necessary).
Since the volume
\vol(\mathcal{F})>0
,
in order to prove the three Noether type inequalities in
Theorem 1.9
,
one may assume that
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})\geq 2
.
As in the case proving the classical Noether inequality (
1.5
),
the starting point is to analyze the canonical map
\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}
defined by the complete linear system
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|
:

\varphi=\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}:\,S\dashrightarrow\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}.

(8.1)

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface of general type, i.e.,
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})=2
.
Suppose that the image
\Sigma=\varphi(S)
is of dimension two.

(i).

The following inequality holds.

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2.

(8.2)

Moreover, if the equality in (
8.2
) holds,
then
\deg(\varphi)=1
and the image
\Sigma
is a surface of minimal degree
(
equal to
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2)
in
\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}
.

(ii).

If the equality in (
8.2
) does not hold,
then

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}.

(8.3)

(iii).

If moreover
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral,
then

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F})=\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{5}{4}.

(8.4)

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{F})
be a foliated surface of general type, i.e.,
\mathrm{Kod}(\mathcal{F})=2
.
Suppose that the image
\Sigma=\varphi(S)
is of dimension one.

(i).

The following inequality holds.

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2+\frac{1}{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})}.

(8.5)

(ii).

If moreover
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral, then

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{3}{2\big(2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1\big)}.

(8.6)

Proof of
Theorem 1.9
.

It follows directly from the above two propositions.
We just remark two points:
firstly, we may assume that
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})\geq 2
since
\vol(\mathcal{F})>0
;
secondly,
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})\geq 3
if
\Sigma=\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}(S)
is of dimension two.
∎

To complete the proof of the Noether type inequalities in
Theorem 1.9
,
it remains to prove the above two propositions.
Before going to the detailed proofs, let’s explain the main difficulties
compared to the case of
\varphi_{|K_{S}|}
:

(1). The canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
might not be nef for a foliation
\mathcal{F}
of general type on a smooth surface
S
, even if the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is reduced or relatively minimal.
It makes some trouble in estimating the lower bound of
\vol(\mathcal{F})
.
For instance, suppose that

|K_{\mathcal{F}}|=|M|+Z,

(8.7)

where
Z
is the fixed part of
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|
, and
M
is the moving part.
Then it is no longer true that

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot M
, cf.
Remark 9.3
.
Of course, one can contract the support of the negative part of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
to a normal surface
S_{0}
, such that the induced foliation
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
S_{0}
has the advantage that
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
is nef
[
Bru99
,
McQ08
]
.
However, the surface
S_{0}
would be singular with klt singularities
and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
is no longer a line bundle (but a
\mathbb{Q}
-bundle).

(2). The second difficulty occurring in the case when
the image
\Sigma=\varphi(S)
is of dimension one;
namely the canonical map
\varphi
induces a fibration
f:\,S\to B
.
In the case proving the classical Noether inequality (
1.5
),
the general fiber
F
of
f
is of genus at least two,
and hence
K_{S}\cdot F=2g(F)-2\geq 2
.
However, in our case it is only known that
K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot F>0
since
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is big.
It can happen that
K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot F=1
, even when the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral
(see
Example 9.2
and
Remark 9.3
for such an example).
Suppose that

K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N,

(8.8)

is the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
,
where
P
is the nef part and
N
is the negative part of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
.
It might happen that
N\cdot F>0
, and hence

P\cdot F<K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot F=1
(see
Example 9.2
for such an example).
This will cause trouble in estimating the lower bound of
\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}
along the usual way, cf.
Remark 3.7
.

(3). The third difficulty happens when proving the Noether type inequalities
(
8.4
) and (
8.6
) for algebraically integral foliations.
This is completely new situation compared to the case of
\varphi_{|K_{S}|}
.
A priori, it is not clear what is the difference of the canonical map
\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}

between algebraically integral and transcendental foliations.
Indeed, any difference would give a positive solution to Poincaré’s problem.

To overcome the above difficulties,
we need to control the negative part in the Zariski decomposition of the canonical divisor
K_{\mathcal{F}}
,
as well as the structure of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
when the canonical map
\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}
induces a fibration or when the foliation is algebraically integral.

As pointed out at the beginning of this section,
we may assume that
\mathcal{F}
is reduced.
Let
\varphi=\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}
be the rational map defined by
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|
as in (
8.1
).

When the image
\Sigma
is of dimension two,
(
8.2
) follows by an easy argument.
To prove the rest two inequalities,
first by some elementary reduction,
we may assume that
\varphi
is birational such that the image
\Sigma
is a surface of minimal degree in
\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}
,
and that

K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=M_{0}+Z_{0},

where
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\phi_{*}(\mathcal{F})
is the induced foliation on the minimal desingularization
Y
of
\Sigma
,

M_{0}
is the pulling-back of a hyperplane section on
\Sigma
,
and
Z_{0}=\phi_{*}(Z)
with
\phi:\,S\to Y
the induced birational map.
A key observation is that the divisor
Z_{0}
is non-zero either if the equality does not hold in (
8.2
)
or if
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral.
With the help of
Proposition 3.6
and
Lemma 5.11
,
we can estimate the contribution of
Z_{0}
to the lower bound on the volume
\vol(\mathcal{F})
and hence prove (
8.3
).
The proof of (
8.4
) is the most technical part of
Proposition 8.1
.
Besides
Proposition 3.6
and
Lemma 5.11
mentioned above,
we have to more carefully analyze the possible non-reduced singularities lying on
Z_{0}
.
The difficult case is when
Z_{0}
contains one unique irreducible rational curve with
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\cdot C_{0}=1
.
There are finitely many possibilities for the possible singularities on such a curve
C_{0}
.
The resolution of such singularities on
C_{0}
presented in
Section 4.2
helps us to finish the proof of (
8.4
)
case-by-case.

When the image
\Sigma
is of dimension one,
then the canonical map induces a fibration
f:\,S\to B
by taking the normalization and Stein factorization:

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}}

Consider the two decompositions in (
8.7
) and (
8.8
).
Since the moving part
M
is always nef,
it follows that
M\leq P
, or equivalently
N\leq Z
.
Let
F
be a general fiber of
f
.
The moving part
M
consists of several fibers, whose cardinality is at least
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1
by the Riemann-Roch theorem.
Hence

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq P\cdot M\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)P\cdot F=\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)\big(K_{\mathcal{F}}-N\big)\cdot F.

To obtain the Noether type inequality (
8.5
),
it suffices to get a lower bound on
\big(K_{\mathcal{F}}-N\big)\cdot F
,
or equivalently, an upper bound on
N\cdot F
,
which will be done with the help of
Proposition 3.6
.
However, the proof of (
8.6
) for an algebraically integral foliation
is much more subtle.
First we may assume that
S
is ruled on
B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
based on
[
Lü24
, Theorem 1.7 (ii)]
and its proof.
Let
\psi:\,S\to S_{0}
be a sequence of blowing-down the exceptional curves in fibers
such that
f_{0}:\,S_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
with
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\psi_{*}(\mathcal{F})
and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=M_{0}+Z_{0}
,
where
M_{0}=\psi_{*}(M)
and
Z_{0}=\psi_{*}(Z)
.
The key observation is, similar to the case when
\dim\Sigma=2
, that the divisor
Z_{0}
contains at least one fiber of
f_{0}
.
By resolving the possible non-reduced singularities on such a fiber,
which is exhibited in
Section 4.2
,
one proves that such a fiber would give a positive contribution
(almost
\frac{1}{2}
) to the lower bound on the volume
\vol(\mathcal{F})
,
based on
Lemma 5.11
.
This helps us to complete the proof of
Proposition 8.2
.

The detailed proofs of
Proposition 8.1
and
Proposition 8.2
will be completed respectively in

Section 8.1
and
Section 8.2
.

8.1.
The canonical map is generically finite

The aim of this subsection is to prove
Proposition 8.1
.
Before going to the proof, let’s do some general preparations.
Given any foliated surface
(S,\mathcal{F})
,
by Seidenberg’s
Theorem 2.5
together with
[
Bru04
, Proposition 5.1]
,
one may assume that
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.
Let
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|=|M|+Z
be the decomposition into moving and fixed parts as in (
8.7
).
Let
\rho:\,Y\to\Sigma
be the minimal desingularization of
\Sigma
,
where
\Sigma=\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}(S)
is the image of
S
under the canonical map as in (
8.1
).
By a sequence of blowing-ups
\sigma:\,\widetilde{S}\to S
centered on the base points of
|M|
, we obtain a well-defined morphism
\phi:\,\widetilde{S}\to Y
with the following diagram.

\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi=\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
desingularization
\textstyle{\Sigma\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}}

Proof of
Proposition 8.1
.

(i). By construction, the map

\rho\circ\phi:\,\widetilde{S}\longrightarrow\Sigma\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N},

is defined by the complete linear system
|\widetilde{M}|
with
\widetilde{M}=\sigma^{*}M-\sum a_{j}\mathcal{E}_{j}
.
Let
M_{0}=\rho^{*}(H)
be the pulling-back of a hyperplane section
H
on
\Sigma
. Then
\widetilde{M}=\phi^{*}(M_{0})
, and

M^{2}=\widetilde{M}^{2}+\sum a_{j}^{2}\geq\widetilde{M}^{2}=\deg(\phi)\cdot M_{0}^{2}=\deg(\varphi)\cdot\deg(\Sigma).

On the other hand, let
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N

be the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
as in (
8.8
).
Then

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq M^{2}\geq\deg(\varphi)\cdot\deg(\Sigma)\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2.

The last inequality follows from
[
Bea79
, Lemme 1.4]
.
Moreover, if the equality holds, then
\deg(\varphi)=1
and

\deg(\Sigma)=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2
in
\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}
.

(ii).
According to the above argument, we may assume that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{$M^{2}=\widetilde{M}^{2}$, ~i.e.,~ $S=\widetilde{S}$};\\
&\text{$\deg\varphi=1$, ~i.e.,~ $\varphi$ is birational};\\
&\text{$\deg\Sigma=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2$}.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.9)

Indeed, if any of the above three statements does not hold, then
\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1
according to the argument above.
Since
\Sigma
is of minimal degree in
\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}
,
its minimal desingularization
Y
is either isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or a Hirzebruch surface (cf.
Proposition 8.6
).
Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be the induced foliation on
Y
.
Then
\phi:\,S=\widetilde{S}\to Y
is composed of a sequence of blowing-ups.
By (
8.7
),

K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=\phi_{*}(M)+\phi_{*}(Z)=M_{0}+\phi_{*}(Z).

Claim 8.3
.

Denote by
Z_{0}=\phi_{*}(Z)
.
Suppose that
\vol(\mathcal{F})\neq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2
.
Then
Z_{0}\neq 0
, and there exists an irreducible curve
C_{0}\subseteq Z_{0}
with
M_{0}\cdot C_{0}>0
.

Proof of
Claim 8.3
.

We prove first that
Z_{0}\neq 0
by contradiction. Suppose that
Z_{0}=0
, i.e.,
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=M_{0}
.
Then by
Lemma 4.4
,

Z+\phi^{*}(M_{0})=Z+M=K_{\mathcal{F}}=\phi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i}=\phi^{*}(M_{0})-\sum(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i},

which implies that
Z=0
and
K_{\mathcal{F}}=\phi^{*}(M_{0})
.
This implies that
\vol(\mathcal{F})=M_{0}^{2}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2
, which contradicts the assumption.

We prove next that there exists an irreducible curve
D_{0}\subseteq Z_{0}
with
M_{0}\cdot D_{0}>0
.
Again, the proof is by contradiction.
First note that if
M_{0}\cdot D_{0}=0
, then
Y
is the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-e)\big)
and
D_{0}
is the rational curve with
D_{0}^{2}=-e\leq-2
.
Suppose that there is no irreducible curve
D\subseteq Z_{0}
with
M_{0}\cdot D>0
.
Then
Z_{0}=aD_{0}
for some
a>0
, and hence
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=M_{0}+aD_{0}
.
If
D_{0}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant, then
\mathrm{tang}(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\cdot D_{0}+D_{0}^{2}<0
, which is a contradiction.
Hence
D_{0}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant, and

0\leq Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0})=2+K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\cdot D_{0}=2+aD_{0}^{2}=2-ae.

It follows that
a=1
,
e=2
, and
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0})=0
.
Since
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\cdot D_{0}<0
, it follows that
D_{0}
is contained in the negative part
N_{0}
of the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=P_{0}+N_{0}
.
On the other hand,
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0})=0
implies that there is no singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
D_{0}
. This gives a contradiction to
Theorem 3.4
.
∎

Come back to the proof of
Proposition 8.1
.
Let
D_{0}\subseteq Z_{0}
be any irreducible curve with
M_{0}\cdot D_{0}=d\geq 1
, and
D\subseteq S
be its strict transform. Then
D
is clearly contained in the support of
Z
.
Note that
M=\phi^{*}(M_{0})
.

If
D
is not contained in the support of the negative part
N
,
then
D
is contained in the support of
P
, and hence

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq P\cdot M\geq(M+D)\cdot M=M^{2}+M\cdot D=M^{2}+M_{0}\cdot D_{0}\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1.

(8.10)

Therefore, we may assume that
D
is contained in the support of
N
.
Let

K_{\mathcal{F}}=M+Z=\phi^{*}(M_{0})+aD+Z^{\prime},

where
D
is not contained in
Z^{\prime}
.
If
a\geq 2
, then

\phi^{*}(M_{0})+D\subseteq P
, since
\lfloor N\rfloor=0
by
Theorem 3.4
.
Hence

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq\phi^{*}(M_{0})\cdot P\geq\phi^{*}(M_{0})\cdot\Big(\phi^{*}(M_{0})+D\Big)=M_{0}^{2}+M_{0}\cdot D_{0}\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1.

Hence we may assume that
a=1
.
Suppose that the irreducible curve
D
is contained in the maximal
\mathcal{F}
-chain
C_{1}+\cdots+C_{r}
. Let
b
be the coefficient of
D
in the negative part
N
.

(1)

If
r=1
and
D=C_{1}
, then
b=\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{1}{2}
by (
3.2
). Hence
\phi^{*}(M_{0})+\frac{1}{2}D\subseteq P
, from which it follows that

\displaystyle\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq\phi^{*}(M_{0})\cdot P

\displaystyle\geq\phi^{*}(M_{0})\cdot\Big(\phi^{*}(M_{0})+\frac{1}{2}D\Big)=M_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}M_{0}\cdot D_{0}=M_{0}^{2}+\frac{d}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2\big)+\frac{d}{2}\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}.

(2)

If
D=C_{r}
with
r\geq 2
, then
b=\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{1}{3}
by (
3.2
). Hence
\phi^{*}(M_{0})+\frac{2}{3}D\subseteq P
.
Similar as above, it follows that

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq M_{0}^{2}+\frac{2}{3}M_{0}\cdot D_{0}\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2\big)+\frac{2d}{3}\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{4}{3}.

(3)

If
D=C_{j}
with
1<j<r
, then

0=K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot D\geq D^{2}+\phi^{*}(M_{0})\cdot D+(C_{j-1}+C_{j+1})\cdot D\geq D^{2}+3.

It follows that
D^{2}\leq-3
, and hence
b<\frac{1}{3}
by (
3.5
). Hence
\phi^{*}(M_{0})+\frac{2}{3}D\subseteq P
.
Again, one obtains that

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq M_{0}^{2}+\frac{2}{3}M_{0}\cdot D_{0}\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2\big)+\frac{2d}{3}\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{4}{3}.

(4)

If
r\geq 2
and
D=C_{1}
, then

-1=K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot D\geq D^{2}+\phi^{*}(M_{0})\cdot D+D_{2}\cdot D\geq D^{2}+2.

It follows that
D^{2}\leq-3
, and hence
b<\frac{1}{2}
by (
3.5
). Hence
\phi^{*}(M_{0})+\frac{1}{2}D\subseteq P
.
Therefore,

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq M_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}M_{0}\cdot D_{0}\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2\big)+\frac{d}{2}\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}.

This completes the proof of (ii).

(iii).
We first claim the following.

Claim 8.4
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is algebraically integral, then
\vol(\mathcal{F})\neq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2
.

Proof of
Claim 8.4
.

We prove by contradiction. Suppose that
\vol(\mathcal{F})=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2
.
According to the proof of (i), the conditions in (
8.9
) are all satisfied, and
P^{2}=M^{2}
.
Note that
M\leq P
, and the negative part
N
satisfies
\lfloor N\rfloor=0
.
It follows that
N\leq Z
, and that
\text{Supp}(N)\subseteq\text{Supp}(Z-N)=\text{Supp}(Z)
.
Since both
P
and
M
are nef, one checks easily that

P^{2}=P\cdot(M+Z-N)\geq P\cdot M+P\cdot(Z-N)\geq M^{2}+(P+M)\cdot(Z-N).

Combining this with the condition that
P^{2}=M^{2}
, one obtains that

P\cdot(Z-N)=M\cdot(Z-N)=0.

Hence

M^{2}=P^{2}=\big(M+(Z-N)\big)^{2}=M^{2}+(Z-N)^{2},\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(Z-N)^{2}=0.

By the Hodge index theorem,
Z-N=0
.
Hence
N=Z=0
, since
\text{Supp}(Z-N)=\text{Supp}(Z)
.
Thus
K_{\mathcal{F}}=M=\phi^{*}(M_{0})
,
and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=\phi_{*}(M)=M_{0}
with
Z_{0}=\phi_{*}(Z)=0
.
On the other hand, by
Lemma 4.4
one has

\phi^{*}(M_{0})=M=K_{\mathcal{F}}=\phi^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-\sum(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i}=\phi^{*}(M_{0})-\sum(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i}.

It follows that
\sum(\ell_{i}-1)\mathcal{E}_{i}=0
, i.e., each
\ell_{i}=1
.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is assumed to be algebraically integral, it follows that all singularity on
Y
is reduced according to
Lemma 4.4
(ii).
It implies that
S=Y
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\mathcal{F}
, since
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.
As a relatively minimal algebraically integral foliation,
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\mathcal{F}
is induced by a fibration of genus
g\geq 2
with normal crossing fibers on the surface
S=Y
.
However, the surface
Y
, as the minimal desingularization of the surface
\Sigma
of minimal degree in
\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}
, is either
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or a Hirzebruch surface, cf.
Proposition 8.6
.
In particular, there is no fibration of genus
g\geq 2
on
Y
.
Therefore,
\vol(\mathcal{F})\neq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2
as required.
∎

According to
Claim 8.4
,
we can assume that the conditions in (
8.9
) are all satisfied;
otherwise,
\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1
by the proof of (ii) above.

Claim 8.5
.

Let
Y
be the minimal desingularization of
\Sigma
with an induced morphism
\phi:\,S\to Y
as in the proof of (ii).
Suppose that
\vol(\mathcal{F})<p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1
.

(A-1).

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\phi_{*}(\mathcal{F})
. Then
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=M_{0}+Z_{0}
with
Z_{0}\neq 0
.

(A-2).

There exists a unique irreducible curve
D_{0}\subseteq Z_{0}
with
M_{0}\cdot D_{0}>0
. Moreover, such a curve
D_{0}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant whose strict transform
D\subseteq S
is contained in the support of the negative part
N
in the Zariski decomposition
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N
,

d=M_{0}\cdot D_{0}=1
, and the coefficient of
D_{0}
in
Z_{0}
is
1
.

Proof of
Claim 8.5
.

This follows the proof of
Proposition 8.1
(ii) above.
Indeed, by
Claim 8.4
and
Claim 8.3
,

Z_{0}=\phi_{*}(Z)\neq 0
, and there exists at least one irreducible curve
D_{0}\subseteq Z_{0}

with
M_{0}\cdot D_{0}=d>0
.
Moreover, any such a curve
D_{0}
with
M_{0}\cdot D_{0}>0
satisfies that

(1)\,

D_{0}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant, whose strict transform
D\subseteq S
is contained in the support of the negative part
N
in the Zariski decomposition
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N
;

(2)\,

the coefficient of
D_{0}
in
Z_{0}
is
1
;

(3)\,

d:=M_{0}\cdot D_{0}=1
.

Otherwise, if any of the above three conditions does not hold,
then it is proved that
\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1
according the arguments in the proof of
Proposition 8.1
(ii) above.
Moreover, it is showed that any such a curve would contribute at least
1/2
to the lower bound of the volume
\vol(\mathcal{F})
.
Hence such a curve
D_{0}
with
M_{0}\cdot D_{0}>0
is unique.
∎

Come back to the proof of
Proposition 8.1
(iii).
In order to prove
Proposition 8.1
(iii),
besides the conditions in (
8.9
),
we may also assume that the two conditions (A-1) and (A-2) in
Claim 8.5
hold.
To go further, we recall the following description on a surface of minimal degree in the projective space (cf.
[
Hor76
, Lemma 1.2]
or
[
GH78
, Chapter 4.3]
).

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{m}
be non-degenerate surface of minimal degree (equal to
m-1
), and
\rho:\,Y\to\Sigma
its minimal desingularization with

M_{0}=\rho^{*}(H)
be the pulling-back of a hyperplane section
H
on
\Sigma
.
Then
(Y,\Sigma,M_{0})
belongs to one of the following four cases:

(1).

Y=\Sigma=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e)\big)
is a Hirzebruch surface,
and
M_{0}=C_{0}+(e+1+k)F
, where
C_{0}
is a section with
C_{0}^{2}=-e
(unique if
e\geq 1
),
F
is a general fiber of the ruling
Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
k\geq 0
;

(2).

\Sigma
is a cone over a rational curve of degree
m-1
in
\mathbb{P}^{m-1}
with
m\geq 3
,

Y=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m-1)\big)
, and
M_{0}=C_{0}+(m-1)F
,
where
C_{0}
is the unique section with
C_{0}^{2}=-(m-1)
, and
F
is a general fiber of the ruling
Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
;

(3).

Y=\Sigma=\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
m=2
and
M_{0}=L
, where
L
is a general line in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
;

(4).

Y=\Sigma=\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
m=5
and
M_{0}=2L
, where
L
is a general line in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

Since
\Sigma
is of minimal degree in
\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}
by (
8.9
).
Hence either
Y
is a Hirzebruch surface or
Y\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
by
Proposition 8.6
.

Case (a).

The surface
Y
is a Hirzebruch surface,
i.e.,
Y=\Sigma=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e)\big)
.
Let
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=M_{0}+Z_{0}
.
By (
8.9
) together with two conditions (A-1) and (A-2) in
Claim 8.5
and
Proposition 8.6
,
there are two subcases:

(a.1).

Z_{0}=F_{0}
, and
M_{0}=C_{0}+(e+k)F
for some
k\geq 0
,
where
F_{0}
is some fixed fiber of the ruling
Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, such that its strict transform in
S
is contained in the support of the negative part
N
of the Zariski decomposition
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N
;

(a.2).

Z_{0}=F_{0}+C_{0}
,
n=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2\geq 2
, and
M_{0}=C_{0}+eF=C_{0}+\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2\big)F
, where
C_{0}

is the unique section with
C_{0}^{2}=-e=2-p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
, and
F
(resp.
F_{0}
) is a general fiber (some fixed fiber) of the ruling
Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Moreover, the strict transform of
F_{0}
in
S
is contained in the support of the negative part
N
of the Zariski decomposition
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N

Case (a.1).

In this case,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}=(M_{0}+F_{0})^{2}=M_{0}^{2}+2=\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2\big)+2=p_{g}(\mathcal{F}).

By
Lemma 4.6
, all the possible non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
are on
F_{0}
.
Let
p_{1},\cdots,p_{k}
be all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
F_{0}
.
By
Proposition 2.3
,

\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{i})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}F_{0}+\chi(F_{0})=3.

(8.11)

In particular,
1\leq k\leq 3
.
We divide into three subcases to finish the proof according to the value of
k
.

Case (a.1.1).
Suppose that
k=1
.
In this case
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=3
by (
8.19
).
We will show that this subcase cannot happen.
First we claim that

Claim 8.7
.

The vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p_{1}
is
1
.

Proof of
Claim 8.7
.

Suppose that the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p_{1}
is
a_{p_{1}}\geq 2
. We will derive a contradiction.
To this aim, we will prove that

all the exceptional curves of the blowing-ups contained in
\phi:\,S\to Y
are
\mathcal{F}
-invariant.

(8.12)

We first derive a contradiction based on (
8.12
).
As a relatively minimal algebraically integral foliation of general type,
\mathcal{F}
is induced a fibration
f:\,S\to B
of genus
g\geq 2
.
By
Lemma 4.1
with (
8.12
), such a fibration induces a fibration
f_{0}:\,Y\to B
with
f=f_{0}\circ\phi
.
However,
Y
is a Hirzebruch surface, there is no fibration of genus
g\geq 2
on
Y
. This gives a contradiction.
Therefore, it suffices to prove (
8.12
).

Note that
p_{1}
is a non-reduced singularity since
a_{p_{1}}\geq 2
.
Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to Y
be the blowing-up centered at
p_{1}
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
.
Then
\phi:\,S\to Y
factors through
\sigma_{1}
as
\phi=\sigma_{1}\circ\phi_{1}
by
Lemma 4.6
(ii).
Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\ell_{p_{1}}-1)E_{1}
.
Then by
Lemma 4.6
(ii),

\ell_{p_{1}}\leq m_{p_{1}}(Z_{0})+1=2.

Combining this with (
2.15
),
\ell_{p_{1}}=a_{p_{1}}=2
and hence
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant.
It follows that

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-E_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0}+F_{0})-E_{1}=M_{1}+\overline{F}_{0},

where
M_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0})
, and
\overline{F}_{0}
is the strict transforms of
F_{0}
in
S_{1}
.
Since
p_{1}
is the unique non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
,
it follows that
q_{1}=E_{1}\cap\overline{F}_{0}
is the unique possible non-reduced singularity
of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, since all possible non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
would be on
Z_{1}:=\overline{F}_{1}
by
Lemma 4.6
.
Moreover, by
Lemma 4.5
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{0},q_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})-1=2.

This implies in particular that the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
at
q_{1}
is
a_{q_{1}}\leq 2
by the definition of
Z
-index.
By
Corollary 4.8
,
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})\geq 2
and
a_{q_{1}}=2
.
Note also that

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=3
by
Proposition 2.3
.
Hence
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=2
or
3
.

Let
\sigma_{2}:\,S_{2}\to S_{1}
be the blowing-up centered at
q_{1}
with exceptional curve
E_{2}
.
Then
\phi_{1}
factors through
\sigma_{2}
as
\phi_{1}=\sigma_{2}\circ\phi_{2}
by

Lemma 4.6
(ii).

K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=\sigma_{2}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})-(\ell_{q_{1}}-1)E_{2}
.
Then by
Lemma 4.6
(ii),

\ell_{q_{1}}\leq m_{q_{1}}(Z_{1})+1=2.

Combining this with (
2.15
),
\ell_{q_{1}}=a_{q_{1}}=2
and hence
E_{2}
is
\mathcal{F}_{2}
-invariant.
It follows that

K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=\sigma_{2}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})-E_{2}=\sigma_{2}^{*}(M_{1}+\overline{F}_{0})-E_{2}=M_{2}+\overline{F}_{0},

where
M_{2}=\sigma_{2}^{*}(M_{1})
, and we still denote by
\overline{F}_{0}
the strict transforms of
F_{0}
in
S_{2}
by abuse of notations.
Similar as above, one sees that the intersection
q_{2}=E_{2}\cap\overline{F}_{0}
is the only possible non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
based on
Lemma 4.6
.
Moreover, it cannot happen that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=3
; indeed, if
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=3
, then similar as above one shows that
q_{2}^{\prime}=\overline{E}_{1}\cap E_{2}
is a singularity with
a_{q_{2}^{\prime}}=2
(and hence non-reduced),
which is a contradiction because
q_{2}
is only possible non-reduced singularity.
Hence
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=2
, and hence there is another singularity
q_{1}^{\prime}
on
E_{1}
with

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1}^{\prime})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})-Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=\big(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}E_{1}+2\big)-2=1.

Moreover, by
Lemma 4.5
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},q_{2})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{0},q_{1})-1=1,\qquad Z(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{E}_{1},q_{2}^{\prime})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})-1=1.

Hence by
Corollary 4.8
,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=1.

Thus there is another singularity
q_{2}^{\prime\prime}
on
E_{2}
with

Z(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime\prime})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2})-Z(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})-Z(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=1.

Hence all the singularities on
S_{2}
are non-degenerate by
Lemma 4.7
; and except
q_{2}
, all the rest singularities are reduced.

p_{1}
F_{0}
\sigma_{1}
q_{1}
q_{1}^{\prime}
\overline{F}_{0}
E_{1}
\sigma_{2}
q_{2}
q_{2}^{\prime\prime}
q_{2}^{\prime}
q_{1}^{\prime}
\overline{F}_{0}
\overline{E}_{1}
E_{2}

By
Proposition 2.3
,

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},q_{2})=\overline{F}_{0}^{2}=-2.

It follows that
q_{2}
is also a reduced singularity.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal, it follows that the induced birational morphism
\phi_{2}:\,S\to S_{2}
is an isomorphism.
Hence the exceptional curves, which are
\overline{E}_{1}
and
E_{2}
, are both
\mathcal{F}
-invariant as required.
∎

Since the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p_{1}
is
1
.
The resolution of such a singularity
p_{1}
into non-degenerate ones is given in
Lemma 4.10
.
The picture is as follows (
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=3
in this case).

p_{1}
F_{0}
\sigma
q_{3}
q_{3}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{2}
\bar{q}_{1}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{F}_{0}
\overline{E}_{2}
E_{3}

By
Proposition 2.3
and
Lemma 2.8
, one obtains that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{F}_{0},q_{3})=\overline{F}_{0}^{2}=-3;\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{1},\bar{q}_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2;\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},\bar{q}_{2})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{2})=\overline{E}_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{1})=-2+\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{3}{2};\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})=E_{3}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},\bar{q}_{2})-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3})=-1+\frac{2}{3}+\frac{1}{3}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

This gives a contradiction, since
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})
is just the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}_{3}
at the non-degenerate singularity
q_{3}^{\prime}
.
Hence this subcase cannot happen.

Case (a.1.2).
Suppose that
k=2
.
By (
8.19
), we may assume without loss of generality that

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=2
and
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{2})=1
.
Since
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=2
, the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p_{1}
is
a_{p_{1}}\leq 2
.

Suppose first that
a_{p_{1}}=2
. Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to Y
be the blowing-up centered at
p_{1}
with exceptional curve
E_{1}
,
and let
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\ell_{p_{1}}-1)E_{1}
.
By
Lemma 4.6
(ii),
\phi:\,S\to Y
factors through
\sigma_{1}
as
\phi=\sigma_{1}\circ\phi_{1}
, and
\ell_{p_{1}}\leq 2
.
Combining with (
2.15
),
one sees that
\ell_{p}=a_{p}=2
and
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant.
Moreover,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\ell_{p_{1}}-1)E_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{F}_{0}.

Hence the possible non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
are all on
\overline{F}_{0}
by
Lemma 4.6
(i).
Let
q_{1}=\overline{F}_{0}\cap E_{1}
. By
Lemma 4.5
,
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{0},q_{1})=1
,
which implies that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=1
as well by

.
Note that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\cdot E_{1}+\chi(E_{1})=3
.
It follows that there are two another singularities
q_{1}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
on
E_{1}
, which are both reduced.

p_{1}
p_{2}
F_{0}
\sigma_{1}
q_{1}
q_{1}^{\prime}
q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
\overline{F}_{0}
p_{2}
E_{1}

Note that all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
are non-degenerate, and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}
is nef.
According to
Lemma 5.11
,

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in T}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{1})=\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)+\min\big\{0,\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\big\}+\min\big\{0,\beta_{p_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\big\},

(8.13)

where
T
is the set of all non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, which contains at most
q_{1}
and
p_{2}
by the above argument.
By
Proposition 2.3
,

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{1},q_{1})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{1},p_{2})=-\overline{F}_{0}^{2}=-1.

Hence there is at most one of the singularities
\{q_{1},p_{2}\}
non-reduced.
If both of them are reduced, then
\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1
by (
8.13
).
If
q_{1}
is non-reduced, then
p_{2}
is reduced and
\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\geq-\frac{1}{4}
;
indeed, if
\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})<-\frac{1}{4}
, then the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
at
q_{1}
is

\lambda_{q_{1}}\not\in\big\{1,2,1/2,3,1/3\big\}.

(8.14)

According to the resolution of a non-degenerate singularity above
Lemma 2.8
,
the minimal resolution
\tilde{\phi}:\,\widetilde{S}\to S_{1}
of the singularity
q_{1}
consists of at most
3
blowing-ups.
Moreover, the contraction
\pi_{1}
factors through
\tilde{\phi}
by
Lemma 4.6
(ii).
Since
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal, it follows that
S=\widetilde{S}
.
This contradicts
Lemma 4.2
.
Hence by (
8.13
),

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)+\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)-\frac{1}{4}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{5}{4}.

If
p_{2}
is non-reduced, the argument would be completely the same and we omit it here.

Suppose next that
a_{p_{1}}=1
. By
Lemma 4.10
, one can resolve
p_{1}
as follows.

p_{2}
p_{1}
F_{0}
\sigma_{1}
p_{2}
q_{1}
\overline{F}_{0}
E_{1}
\sigma_{2}
p_{2}
q_{2}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{1}
q_{2}
\overline{F}_{0}
\overline{E}_{1}
E_{2}

Let
\mathcal{F}_{i}
be the induced foliation on
S_{i}
as in
Lemma 4.10
.
By
Proposition 2.3
and
Lemma 2.8
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},\bar{q}_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{E}_{1},\bar{q}_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})^{-1}+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},p_{2})=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},q_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},p_{2})=\overline{F}_{0}^{2}=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})=E_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},\bar{q}_{1})=-1+\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{1}{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.15)

According to (
4.8
),

K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=\pi^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-E_{2}=\pi^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{F}_{0}+E_{2}+\overline{E}_{1}.

Hence the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}
is as follows.

K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=P_{2}+N_{2},\qquad\text{where~}P_{2}=\pi^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{F}_{0}+E_{2}+\frac{\overline{E}_{1}}{2},\,~\,N_{2}=\frac{\overline{E}_{1}}{2}.

By
Lemma 4.6
,
\phi:\,S\to Y
factors through
\pi=\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}
as
\phi=\pi\circ\phi_{2}
,
and that the possible non-reduced singularities are all on
\overline{F}_{0}+E_{2}+\overline{E}_{1}
.
Let
T_{2}
be the set of non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
.
According to
Lemma 5.11
,

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}^{2}+\beta_{\bar{q}_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})+\sum_{q\in T_{2}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{2}).

(8.16)

By (
8.15
),
\bar{q}_{1}
is reduced with
\beta_{\bar{q}_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})=\frac{1}{2}
.

If
q_{2}
is non-reduced, i.e.,
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},q_{2})
is a positive rational number, then by (
8.15
), both
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},p_{2})
are negative rational,
which implies that both
q_{2}^{\prime}
and
p_{2}
are reduced.
Moreover, the eigenvalue
\lambda_{q_{2}}\neq 1
; otherwise,

\phi:\,S\to Y
consists of at most
3
blowing-ups by a similar argument in proving (
8.14
), a contradiction to
Lemma 4.2
.
Hence by (
8.16
),

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)+\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{q\in T_{2}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{2})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1.

Suppose now that
q_{2}
is reduced, from which it follows that
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})<0
.
If
T_{2}
contains at most one singularity, i.e.,
T_{2}=\{p_{2}\}
or
T_{2}=\{q_{2}^{\prime}\}
,
then similarly by
Lemma 4.2
, one shows that the eigenvalue
\lambda_{q}\neq 1
for such a non-reduced singularity
q\in T_{2}
, and hence
\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1
.
Suppose next that
T_{2}=\{p_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}\}
, i.e., both
p_{2}
and
q_{2}^{\prime}
are non-reduced.
In this case, the eigenvalue
\lambda_{q}\not\in\{1,2,1/2\}
for any
q\in T_{2}
.
Indeed, if
\lambda_{p_{2}}\in\{1,2,1/2\}
, then
\lambda_{q_{2}^{\prime}}<0
by (
8.15
); and similarly if
\lambda_{q_{2}^{\prime}}\in\{1,2,1/2\}
, then
\lambda_{p_{2}}<0
. Hence by (
8.16
),

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)+\frac{1}{2}+\beta_{p_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})+\beta_{q_{2}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{7}{6}.

Case (a.1.3).
Suppose that
k=3
.
By (
8.19
),

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{2})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{3})=1.

In particular, all singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
are non-degenerate.
According to
Proposition 2.3
,

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{3})=F_{0}^{2}=0.

(8.17)

It follows in particular that at most two of the above
\mathrm{CS}
-indices are positive rational.
We may assume without loss of generality that
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{3})
is not positive rational.
By
Lemma 5.11
,

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}+\sum_{p\in T_{0}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+\sum_{p\in T_{0}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0}),

(8.18)

where
T_{0}
is the set of non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Suppose first that one of the
\mathrm{CS}
-indices equal to
1
, saying for instance
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=1
. Then similar to prove (
8.14
), by
Lemma 4.2
one shows that

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{2})\not\in\big\{1,2,3,1/2,1/3\}.

Hence by (
8.18
),

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1-\frac{1}{4}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{5}{4}.

If
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})\neq 1
and
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{2})\neq 1
, then by (
8.18
),

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1.

Case (a.2).

By
Proposition 2.2
one checks easily that
C_{0}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant.
As
F_{0}
is also
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant, it follows that the intersection
p_{0}=F_{0}\cap C_{0}
is a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Hence by
Proposition 2.3
,

1\leq Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C_{0})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\cdot C_{0}+\chi(C_{0})=3-e.

Since
e\geq 2
by assumption, it follows that
e=2
,
and hence
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})=2+e=4
and

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C_{0})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C_{0},p_{0})=1.

In particular,
p_{0}
is the unique singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
C_{0}
,
and by (
2.3
),

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},C_{0},p_{0})=C_{0}^{2}=-2.

In particular,
p_{0}
is a reduced singularity, and
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{0})=1
by
Corollary 4.8
.
In particular, all the possible non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
are on
F_{0}\setminus\{p_{0}\}
by
Lemma 4.6
.
Let
p_{0},p_{1},\cdots,p_{k}
be all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
F_{0}
.
By
Proposition 2.3
,

\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{i})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})-Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{0})=3.

(8.19)

In particular,
1\leq k\leq 3
. One can then apply a similar argument as in Case (a.1) to finish the proof according to the value of
k
.
We outline the main points in the following and omit the details.
Since
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})=4
in this case, it suffices to show
\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{11}{4}
.

Case (a.2.1).
Suppose that
k=1
.
In this case
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=3
by (
8.19
).
First similar to
Claim 8.7
, one shows that the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p_{1}
is
a_{p_{1}}=1
.
By
Lemma 4.10
, one can resolve
p_{1}
into non-degenerate singularities as follows.

p_{1}
p_{0}
F_{0}
C_{0}
\sigma
p_{0}
q_{3}
q_{3}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{2}
\bar{q}_{1}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{F}_{0}
\overline{E}_{2}
E_{3}
\overline{C}_{0}

Moreover, by (
4.8
),

\displaystyle K_{\mathcal{F}_{3}}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\mathcal{E}_{2}+\mathcal{E}_{3})=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\overline{E}_{2}+2E_{3})

\displaystyle=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{C}_{0}+\overline{F}_{0}+\overline{E}_{1}+\overline{E}_{2}+E_{3}=P_{3}+N_{3},

where
P_{3}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\frac{1}{5}\overline{C}_{0}+\frac{2}{5}\overline{F}_{0}+\frac{1}{3}\overline{E}_{1}+\frac{2}{3}\overline{E}_{2}+E_{3}
and
N_{3}=\frac{4}{5}\overline{C}_{0}+\frac{3}{5}\overline{F}_{0}+\frac{2}{3}\overline{E}_{1}+\frac{1}{3}\overline{E}_{2}

are respectively the nef and negative parts of the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{3}}
.
By
Proposition 2.3
and
Lemma 2.8
, one obtains that

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{F}_{0},q_{3})=\overline{F}_{0}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{F}_{0},p_{0})=-3+\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{5}{2};\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{1},\bar{q}_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2;\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},\bar{q}_{2})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{2})=\overline{E}_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{1})=-2+\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{3}{2};\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})=E_{3}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},\bar{q}_{2})-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3})=-1+\frac{2}{3}+\frac{2}{5}=\frac{1}{15}.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.20)

Hence by
Lemma 5.11
,

\displaystyle\vol(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{3}}^{2}+\beta_{p_{0}}(\mathcal{F}_{3})+\beta_{q_{3}}(\mathcal{F}_{3})+\beta_{\bar{q}_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{3})+\beta_{\bar{q}_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{3})+\beta_{q_{3}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{3})

\displaystyle=2+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{10}+\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{15}=\frac{16}{5}.

Case (a.2.2).
Suppose that
k=2
.
By (
8.19
), we may assume without loss of generality that

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=2
and
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{2})=1
.
Since
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=2
, the vanishing order of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
at
p_{1}
is
a_{p_{1}}\leq 2
.

If the vanishing order
a_{p_{1}}=2
, then one can resolve
p_{1}
into non-reduced singularities as follows.

p_{0}
p_{1}
p_{2}
F_{0}
C_{0}
\sigma_{1}
p_{0}
q_{1}
q_{1}^{\prime}
q_{1}^{\prime\prime}
\overline{F}_{0}
p_{2}
E_{1}
\overline{C}_{0}

Moreover, it holds that
q_{1}^{\prime}
and
q_{2}^{\prime\prime}
are reduced by
Lemma 4.6
, since

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-E_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{C}_{0}+\overline{F}_{0}.

The Zariski decomposition is
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\Big(\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\overline{C}_{0}+\overline{F}_{0}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\overline{C}_{0}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{0},p_{0})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{C}_{0},p_{0})=-\overline{C}_{0}^{2}=-2,

\displaystyle\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{0},q_{1})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{0},p_{2})=\overline{F}_{0}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{0},p_{0})=-\frac{1}{2}.

Hence at most one of the two singularities
\{q_{1},p_{2}\}
is non-reduced; say
q_{1}
.
Similar to the proof of (
8.14
), one shows by
Lemma 4.2
that

\lambda_{q_{1}}\not\in\{1,2,1/2,3,1/3\}.

Hence

\displaystyle\vol(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}^{2}+\beta_{p_{0}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})+\beta_{q_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\geq 3+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4}=\frac{13}{4}.

If the vanishing order
a_{p_{1}}=1
, then by
Lemma 4.10
, one can resolve
p_{1}
as follows.

p_{2}
p_{0}
p_{1}
F_{0}
C_{0}
\sigma_{1}
p_{2}
p_{0}
q_{1}
\overline{F}_{0}
\overline{C}_{0}
E_{1}
\sigma_{2}
p_{0}
p_{2}
q_{2}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{1}
q_{2}
\overline{F}_{0}
\overline{C}_{0}
\overline{E}_{1}
E_{2}

Moreover,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},\bar{q}_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{E}_{1},\bar{q}_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})^{-1}+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},p_{2})=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},q_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},p_{2})\\
&\hskip 160.75789pt=\overline{F}_{0}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{0},p_{0})=-\frac{3}{2},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})=E_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},\bar{q}_{1})=-1+\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{1}{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

According to (
4.8
),

K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=\pi^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-E_{2}=\pi^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{C}_{0}+\overline{F}_{0}+E_{2}+\overline{E}_{1}.

Hence the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}
is as follows.

K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=P_{2}+N_{2},\qquad\text{where~}P_{2}=\pi^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{F}_{0}+E_{2}+\frac{\overline{C}_{0}+\overline{E}_{1}}{2},\,~\,N_{2}=\frac{\overline{C}_{0}+\overline{E}_{1}}{2}.

Let
T_{2}
be the set of non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
.
Similar to the proof in Case (a.1.2), one shows that
T_{2}
contains at most two singularities and
\sum\limits_{q\in T_{2}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{2})\geq-1
.
Hence by
Lemma 5.11
one obtains

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}^{2}+\beta_{\bar{p}_{0}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})+\beta_{\bar{q}_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{1})+\sum_{q\in T_{2}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{2})=4+\sum_{q\in T_{2}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{2})\geq 3.

Case (a.2.3).
Suppose that
k=3
. Then

\displaystyle Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{2})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{3})=1,

\displaystyle\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{1})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{3})=F_{0}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0},p_{0})=\frac{1}{2}.

Let
T_{0}
be the set of non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Similar to Case (a.1.3), one has

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}+\beta_{p_{0}}(\mathcal{F}_{0})+\sum_{p\in T_{0}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=4+\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{p\in T_{0}}\beta_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\geq\frac{9}{2}-\frac{3}{2}=3>\frac{11}{4}.

This completes the proof of Case (a).

Case (b).

The surface
Y\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

Proposition 8.6
together with the two conditions (A-1) and (A-2) in
Claim 8.5
imply that
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=M_{0}+Z_{0}
,
where
M_{0}=L
is a general line in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and
Z_{0}=D_{0}
is some fixed line in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Moreover, the strict transform of the curve
D_{0}
in
S
is contained in the support of the negative part in the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
.
According to
Lemma 4.6
, all the possible non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
are on
D_{0}
.

Let
p
be a non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
, and
let
\sigma_{1}:\,Y_{1}\to Y
be the blowing-up centered at
p
with exceptional curve
E
.
Then
Y_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)\big)
is the Hirzebruch surface with value
e=1
.
Moreover, the birational map
\phi:\,S\to Y
factors through
\sigma_{1}
as
\phi=\sigma_{1}\circ\phi_{1}
by
Lemma 4.6
(ii). Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
and

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-(\ell_{p}-1)E_{1}=M_{1}+F_{0}+(2-\ell_{p})E_{1},

where
M_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}(M_{0})=\sigma_{1}^{*}(L)
, and
F_{0}
is the strict transform of
D_{0}
, which is nothing but a fiber of the ruling on
Y_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
In particular,
\ell_{p}\leq 2
by
Lemma 4.6
(ii).
If
\ell_{p}=2
, then
K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=M_{1}+F_{0}
.
Note that
M_{1}\sim C_{0}+F
, where
C_{0}=E
is the section with negative self-intersection number equal to
-e=-1
, and
F
is a general fiber of the ruling on
Y_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Replacing
(Y,\mathcal{F}_{0})
by
(Y_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
, we reach the situation as in Case (a.1).
Applying a similar argument in Case (a.1), one proves (
8.4
).
Thus we may assume that
\ell_{p}=1
for any non-reduced singularity
p
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
In particular, for any non-reduced singularity
p
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
,
the vanishing order
a_{p}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=1
by (
2.15
),
and the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p}\neq 1
, cf. the resolution process above
Lemma 2.8
.

Let
p_{1},\cdots,p_{k}
be all the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
D_{0}
.
By
Proposition 2.3
,

\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{i})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}D_{0}+\chi(D_{0})=4.

We can prove (
8.4
) case-by-case depending on the value of
k
(most arguments would be similar to that in Case (a.1)).
Since
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})=3
, it suffices to show
\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{7}{4}
.

Case (b.1).

Suppose
k=1
. Then by
Lemma 4.10
, we can resolve the singularity
p_{1}
as follows.

p_{1}
D_{0}
\sigma
q_{4}
q_{4}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{3}
\bar{q}_{2}
\bar{q}_{1}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{D}_{0}
\overline{E}_{3}
\overline{E}_{2}
E_{4}

Moreover,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{4}}=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-\sum_{i=2}^{4}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{D}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\overline{E}_{i}+E_{4}.

Hence the Zariski decomposition is
K_{\mathcal{F}_{4}}=P_{4}+N_{4}
, where

P_{4}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\frac{\overline{D}_{0}}{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{i\overline{E}_{i}}{4}+E_{4},\qquad N_{4}=\frac{2\overline{D}_{0}}{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{(4-i)\overline{E}_{i}}{4}.

By
Proposition 2.3
with
Lemma 2.8
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},q_{4})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{D}_{0},q_{4})=\overline{D}_{0}^{2}=-3,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{1},\bar{q}_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{3},\bar{q}_{2})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{2})=\overline{E}_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{1})=-\frac{3}{2},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},\bar{q}_{3})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{3},\bar{q}_{3})=\overline{E}_{3}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{3},\bar{q}_{2})=-\frac{4}{3},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},\bar{q}_{4}^{\prime})=E_{4}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},\bar{q}_{3})-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},q_{4})=\frac{1}{12}.\end{aligned}\right.

Thus by
Lemma 5.11
,

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{4}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\beta_{\bar{q}_{i}}(\mathcal{F}_{4})+\beta_{q_{4}}(\mathcal{F}_{4})+\beta_{q_{4}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{4})=1+\frac{3}{4}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{12}=2.

Case (b.2).

Suppose
k=2
.
Assume without loss of generality that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{1})\geq Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{2})
.
Then there are two possibilities:
either
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{1})=3
and
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{2})=1
,
or
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{2})=2
.
If it is the first possibility, then we can resolve the singularity
p_{1}
as follows by
Lemma 4.10
.

p_{1}
p_{2}
D_{0}
\sigma
q_{3}
q_{3}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{2}
\bar{q}_{1}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{D}_{0}
\overline{E}_{2}
E_{3}
p_{2}

Moreover,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{3}}=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-\sum_{i=2}^{3}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{D}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overline{E}_{i}+E_{3}.

Hence the Zariski decomposition is
K_{\mathcal{F}_{4}}=P_{4}+N_{4}
, where

P_{4}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{D}_{0}+\frac{\overline{E}_{1}}{3}+\frac{2\overline{E}_{2}}{3}+E_{4},\qquad N_{4}=\frac{2\overline{E}_{1}}{3}+\frac{\overline{E}_{2}}{3}.

By
Proposition 2.3
with
Lemma 2.8
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{1},\bar{q}_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},\bar{q}_{2})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{2})=\overline{E}_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},\bar{q}_{1})=-\frac{3}{2},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})=E_{3}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},\bar{q}_{2})=-\frac{1}{3},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{D}_{0},q_{3})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},D_{0},p_{2})=\overline{D}_{0}^{2}=-2.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.21)

Hence the possible non-reduced singularities belong to
\{p_{2},q_{3},q_{3}^{\prime}\}
.
Let
T_{3}
be the set of all non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{3}
.
By
Lemma 5.11
,

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{3}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\beta_{\bar{q}_{i}}(\mathcal{F}_{3})+\sum_{q\in T_{3}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{3})=\frac{8}{3}+\sum_{q\in T_{3}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{3}).

Note that
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3})=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{D}_{0},q_{3})^{-1}
by
Lemma 2.8
.
Hence
\mathcal{F}_{3}
admits at most two non-reduced singularities by
the last two equalities in (
8.21
).
Moreover, by
Corollary 4.3
, one shows that the eigenvalue
\lambda_{q}\neq 1
for any
q\in T_{3}
.
If the eigenvalue
\lambda_{q}\not\in\{1,2,1/2\}
for any
q\in T_{3}
, then

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{8}{3}+\sum_{q\in T_{3}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{3})\geq\frac{8}{3}-\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}=2.

If there is an eigenvalue
\lambda_{q}=2
or
1/2
for some
q\in T_{3}
, one can
prove (
8.4
) case-by-case.
We prove for instance the case when
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},D_{0},p_{2})=\lambda_{p_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}
.
Then
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{D}_{0},q_{3})=-\frac{5}{2}
, and

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},E_{3},q_{3}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{15}
.
Hence

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{8}{3}+\sum_{q\in T_{3}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{3})\geq\frac{8}{3}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{15}=\frac{21}{10}.

We consider next the second possibility where
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{2})=2
.
The singularities
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
can be resolved as follows by
Lemma 4.10
.

p_{1}
p_{2}
D_{0}
\sigma
q_{4}
q_{4}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{3}
q_{2}
q_{2}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{1}
\overline{D}_{0}
E_{4}
E_{2}
\overline{E}_{3}
\overline{E}_{1}

Moreover, let
\mathcal{F}_{4}
be the induced foliation. Then

K_{\mathcal{F}_{4}}=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-E_{2}-E_{4}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{D}_{0}+\overline{E}_{1}+\overline{E}_{3}+E_{2}+E_{4}.

Hence the Zariski decomposition is
K_{\mathcal{F}_{4}}=P_{4}+N_{4}
, where

P_{4}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{D}_{0}+\frac{\overline{E}_{1}+\overline{E}_{3}}{2}+E_{2}+E_{4},\qquad N_{4}=\frac{\overline{E}_{1}+\overline{E}_{3}}{2}.

By
Proposition 2.3
with
Lemma 2.8
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{2},\bar{q}_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},\bar{q}_{3})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{1},\bar{q}_{1})=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{E}_{3},\bar{q}_{3})=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{2},q_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=E_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{2},\bar{q}_{1})=-\frac{1}{2},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},q_{4})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},q_{4}^{\prime})=E_{4}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},\bar{q}_{3})=-\frac{1}{2},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{2},q_{2})^{-1}+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},E_{4},q_{4})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{D}_{0},q_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{4},\overline{D}_{0},q_{4})=\overline{D}_{0}^{2}=-3.\end{aligned}\right.

Hence the possible non-reduced singularities belong to
\{q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime},q_{4},q_{4}^{\prime}\}
,
and
\mathcal{F}_{4}
admits at most two non-reduced singularities.
Let
T_{4}
be the set of all non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{4}
.
By
Lemma 5.11
,

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{4}}^{2}+\beta_{\bar{q}_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{4})+\beta_{\bar{q}_{3}}(\mathcal{F}_{4})+\sum_{q\in T_{4}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{4})=3+\sum_{q\in T_{4}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{4}).

Similarly, by
Corollary 4.3
, one shows that the eigenvalue
\lambda_{q}\neq 1
for any
q\in T_{4}
.
If the eigenvalue
\lambda_{q}\not\in\{1,2,1/2\}
for any
q\in T_{4}
, then

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\frac{8}{3}+\sum_{q\in T_{3}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{3})\geq 3-\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{3}=\frac{7}{3}.

If there is an eigenvalue
\lambda_{q}=2
or
1/2
for some
q\in T_{3}
, one can
prove (
8.4
) case-by-case similar as above.

Case (b.3).

Suppose
k=3
.
In this case, we can assume without loss of generality that

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{1})=2,\qquad Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{2})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{3})=1.

By
Lemma 4.10
, we can resolve the singularity
p_{1}
as follows by
Lemma 4.10
.

p_{3}
p_{2}
p_{1}
D_{0}
\sigma
p_{3}
p_{2}
q_{2}
q_{2}^{\prime}
\bar{q}_{1}
\overline{D}_{0}
E_{2}
\overline{E}_{1}

Moreover, let
\mathcal{F}_{2}
be the induced foliation. Then

K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=\sigma^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})-E_{2}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{D}_{0}+\overline{E}_{1}+E_{2}.

Hence the Zariski decomposition is
K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=P_{2}+N_{2}
, where

P_{2}=\sigma^{*}(M_{0})+\overline{D}_{0}+\frac{\overline{E}_{1}}{2}+E_{2},\qquad N_{2}=\frac{\overline{E}_{1}}{2}.

By
Proposition 2.3
with
Lemma 2.8
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},\bar{q}_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{E}_{1},\bar{q}_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=E_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},\bar{q}_{1})=-\frac{1}{2},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{D}_{0},q_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{D}_{0},p_{2})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{D}_{0},p_{3})=\overline{D}_{0}^{2}=-1.\end{aligned}\right.

Hence the possible non-reduced singularities belong to
\{q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime},p_{2},p_{3}\}
,
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
admits at most three non-reduced singularities.
Let
T_{2}
be the set of all non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
.
By
Lemma 5.11
,

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}^{2}+\beta_{\bar{q}_{1}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})+\sum_{q\in T_{4}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{2})=\frac{7}{2}+\sum_{q\in T_{2}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{2}).

Similar as above, one can prove (
8.4
) with the help of
Corollary 4.3
.

Case (b.4).

Suppose
k=4
.
In this case,

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{2})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{3})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},D_{0},p_{4})=1.

By the arguments at the beginning of Case (b), we may assume that the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p_{i}}\neq 1
for any
1\leq i\leq 4
.
Let
T_{0}
be the set of all non-reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
By
Lemma 5.11
,

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}+\sum_{q\in T_{0}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=4+\sum_{q\in T_{0}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\geq 4-4\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{2}=2.

Thus we complete the proof of (
8.4
). ∎

8.2.
The canonical map induces a fibration

We aim to prove
Proposition 8.2
in this subsection.
Thus we will assume that
\dim\Sigma=1
, where
\Sigma
is the image of the canonical map
\varphi
.
Similar to the arguments at the beginning of
Section 8.1
,
we may assume that
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.
By the Stein factorization, we obtain a diagram as follows.

\textstyle{\widetilde{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi=\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
desingularization
\textstyle{\Sigma}

Here
\pi:\,B\to Y
is finite, and
f:\,\widetilde{S}\to B
is a family of curves with connected fibers.
By construction, the map

\rho\circ\phi:\,\widetilde{S}\longrightarrow\Sigma\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N},

is defined by the complete linear system
|\widetilde{M}|
,
where
|\widetilde{M}|
is obtained by blowing-up the base points of
|M|
,
where
M
is the moving part of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
as in (
8.7
).
Since
|\widetilde{M}|
is base-point-free and induces a fibration
f:\,\widetilde{S}\to B
,
it follows that

p_{g}(\mathcal{F})=h^{0}(S,M)=h^{0}(\widetilde{S},\widetilde{M})=h^{0}\big(B,f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{S}}(\widetilde{M})\big).

According to the Riemann-Roch theorem,

\deg(L)\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1,

where
L=f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{S}}(\widetilde{M})
is a line bundle on
B
.
Note that
\widetilde{M}=f^{*}(L)
.
Hence, we have numerically,

\widetilde{M}\equiv_{num}\deg(L)\,F,\qquad\text{with~}\deg(L)\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1,

(8.22)

where
F
is a general fiber of
f
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
M
be the moving part of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
as in (
8.7
).
If the linear system
|M|
has a base point, then

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)^{2}.

Proof.

Let
A=\sigma_{*}(F)
. If
|M|
has a base point, then
A^{2}\geq 1
.
Hence

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq P^{2}\geq M^{2}\geq\big((p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1)A\big)^{2}\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)^{2}.\qed

To prove
Proposition 8.2
, we assume from now on that
|M|
is base-point-free.
In other words,

\varphi:\,S\to\Sigma
is already a morphism.
Hence one gets a commutative diagram as follows.

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\varphi=\varphi_{|K_{\mathcal{F}}|}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
desingularization
\textstyle{\Sigma\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}}

Proof of
Proposition 8.2
.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is of general type, i.e.,
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is big,
it follows that
K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot F\geq 1
.

(i).
As explained at the beginning of this section, we may assume that the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.
By
Lemma 8.8
, we may assume that
|M|
is base-point-free.
The relation in (
8.22
) rephrases as

M\equiv_{num}\deg(L)\,F,\qquad\text{with~}\deg(L)\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1.

The moving part
M
is clearly nef,
from which it follows that
M\leq P
, or equivalently
N\leq Z
,
where
P
is the nef part and
N
is the negative part of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
in its Zariski decomposition, and
Z
is the fixed part of
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|
as in (
8.7
).
Hence

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}\geq P\cdot M\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)P\cdot F=\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)\,(K_{\mathcal{F}}-N)\cdot F.

(8.23)

Therefore, it suffices to prove a lower bound on
P\cdot F
, or equivalently an upper bound on
N\cdot F
.

The fibration
f:\,S\to B
defines a natural foliation
\mathcal{G}

by taking the saturation of the kernel
\ker(\mathrm{d}f:\,T_{S}\to f^{*}T_{B})
in
T_{S}
.
The given foliation
\mathcal{F}
may be equal to or different from
\mathcal{G}
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{G}
. Then the genus
g(F)\geq 2
since
\mathcal{F}
is assumed to be of general type.
Moreover, the canonical divisor is easy to compute:

K_{\mathcal{F}}=K_{\mathcal{G}}=K_{S}\otimes f^{*}K_{B}^{-1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}\Big(\sum(1-a_{i})C_{i}\Big),

where the sum is taken over all components in fibers of
f
, and
a_{i}
is the multiplicity of
C_{i}
in its fiber.
Note that the support of the negative part
N
is a sum of
\mathcal{F}
-chains (cf.
Theorem 3.4
), and thus contained in fibers of
f
.
Combining this with (
8.23
),

\displaystyle\vol(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)\,(K_{\mathcal{F}}-N)\cdot F

\displaystyle=\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)\,K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot F

\displaystyle=\big(2g(F)-2)\big)(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1)\geq 2(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1).

In the rest part of the proof, we will always assume that
\mathcal{F}
is different from the foliation
\mathcal{G}
defined by taking the saturation of the kernel
\ker(\mathrm{d}f:\,T_{S}\to f^{*}T_{B})
in
T_{S}
.

Let
Z=Z_{h}+Z_{v}
,
where
Z
is the fixed part of
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|
as in (
8.7
),
and each component in
Z_{v}
is contained in fibers of
f
, while each component in
Z_{h}
maps surjectively to the base
B
.
Similarly, we can decompose the negative part as
N=N_{h}+N_{v}
.
Let

\displaystyle Z_{h}=\sum a_{C}C,

\displaystyle Z_{v}=\sum a_{D}D,

\displaystyle N_{h}=\sum b_{C}C,

\displaystyle N_{v}=\sum b_{D}D.

Then the coefficients
\{a_{C},a_{D}\}
’s are positive integers; while
\{b_{C},b_{D}\}
’s belong to
[0,1)
by
Theorem 3.4
, since the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is assumed to be relatively minimal.

If there exists an irreducible component
C\subseteq Z_{h}

with
b_{C}=0
(i.e.,
C
is NOT contained in the support of
N
),
then
P\cdot F\geq a_{C}\geq 1
, from which with (
8.23
) it follows that

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1.

(8.24)

Hence we may assume that the supports of
Z_{h}
and
N_{h}
are the same.
Let
C\subseteq\text{Supp}(Z_{h})=\text{Supp}(N_{h})
be an irreducible component.
If
a_{C}\geq 2
, then

P\cdot F\geq a_{C}-b_{C}>1
since
b_{C}<1
.
Hence one shows by (
8.23
) that

\vol(\mathcal{F})>p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1.

(8.25)

Therefore, we may assume that
a_{C}=1
.
Let
C_{1}+\cdots+C_{r}
be the
\mathcal{F}
-chain containing
C
.
Consider first the case when
r=1
. Then
C=C_{1}
and

-1=K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot C\geq\Big(C+\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)F\Big)\cdot C.

Hence
C^{2}\leq-p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
.
As
r=1
, one sees easily that
b_{C}=\frac{1}{-C^{2}}
by
Lemma 3.5
.
Hence by (
8.23
)

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)(1-b_{C})C\cdot F\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2+\frac{1}{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})}.

(8.26)

Consider next the case when
r\geq 2
and
C=C_{1}
.
Then

-1=K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot C_{1}\geq\Big(a_{C_{1}}C_{1}+a_{C_{2}}C_{2}+\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)F\Big)\cdot C_{1}\geq a_{C_{1}}C_{1}^{2}+p_{g}(\mathcal{F})=C_{1}^{2}+p_{g}(\mathcal{F}),

since
a_{C_{1}}=a_{C}=1
by assumption, and
a_{C_{2}}\geq 1
.
Hence
C^{2}=C_{1}^{2}\leq-(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1)
.
By
Proposition 3.6
,
b_{C}<\frac{1}{-C^{2}-1}\leq\frac{1}{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})}
.
Hence by (
8.23
)

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)(1-b_{C})C\cdot F>p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2+\frac{1}{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})}.

(8.27)

Consider thirdly the case when
r\geq 2
and
C=C_{i}
with
1<i<r
.
Then

0=K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot C_{i}\geq\Big(a_{i}C_{i}+a_{C_{i-1}}C_{i-1}+a_{C_{i+1}}C_{i+1}+\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)F\Big)\cdot C_{i}\geq a_{i}C_{i}^{2}+p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1=C_{i}^{2}+p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1,

since
a_{C_{i}}=a_{C}=1
by assumption,
a_{i-1}\geq 1
and
a_{i+1}\geq 1
.
Hence
C^{2}=C_{i}^{2}\leq-(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1)
.
By
Proposition 3.6
,
b_{C}<\frac{1}{-2C^{2}-3}\leq\frac{1}{2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}
.
Hence by (
8.23
)

\displaystyle\vol(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)(1-b_{C})C\cdot F

(8.28)

\displaystyle>p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1-\frac{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}{2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2\big(2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)}.

Finally, we consider the case when
r\geq 2
and
C=C_{r}
.
Then

0=K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot C\geq\Big(C+a_{C_{r-1}}C_{r-1}+\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)F\Big)\cdot C\geq C^{2}+p_{g}(\mathcal{F}).

Hence
C^{2}=C_{r}^{2}\leq-p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
.
In this case, according to
Lemma 3.5
,

b_{C}=b_{r}=\frac{1}{n}
, where by the proof of
Proposition 3.6
,

n\geq 2\xi_{r-1}-\xi_{r}=-2C_{r}^{2}\xi_{r}-\xi_{r}=-(2C_{r}^{2}+1)\geq 2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1.

Hence by (
8.23
)

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)(1-b_{C})C\cdot F\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2\big(2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)}.

(8.29)

(ii).
We use the same notations above.
Similar as in (i), one may assume that

\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal and different from the foliation
\mathcal{G}
defined by taking the saturation of the kernel
\ker(\mathrm{d}f:\,T_{S}\to f^{*}T_{B})
in
T_{S}
.
Furthermore, according to the proof of (i), we may also assume that

Z_{h}=C
consists of only one component such that
C
is contained in the support of
N_{h}
and
C\cdot F=1
.
In fact, according to the above proof, we may first assume that the coefficient
C
is contained in the support of
N_{h}
and
a_{C}=1
for any possible component
C\subseteq Z_{h}
; otherwise
\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1

by (
8.24
) and (
8.25
).
Moreover, if
Z_{h}
contains one component
C
with
C\cdot F\geq 2
, or contains at least two components,
then by a similar argument as above, one shows that

\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq 2\Big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2+\frac{1}{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})}\Big).

Thus one may write

K_{\mathcal{F}}=M+C+Z_{v},

(8.30)

where
C
is a section (i.e.,
C\cdot F=1
) of
f
contained in the support of
N_{h}
,

Z_{v}
is contained in fibers of
f
,
and
M=\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)F
with
F
being a general fiber of
f
.
All the statements are clear except the last one, which we simply argue as follows.
Since
C
is contained in the support of
N_{h}
, it is a rational curve,
which implies that the base curve
B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Note that
h^{0}(S,M)=h^{0}(B,L)=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
.
By the Riemann-Roch theorem,
\deg(L)=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1
, and hence

M=\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)F
as required.
Let
C_{1}+\cdots+C_{r}
be the
\mathcal{F}
-chain containing
C
.
Then by (
8.28
) and (
8.29
),
we may assume that
C=C_{1}
is the first component of the
\mathcal{F}
-chain.
In other words,

there is exactly one singularity of
\mathcal{F}
on
C
.

(8.31)

According to
[
Lü24
, Corollay 4.9]
, we may assume that the general fiber of
f
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
In other words,
f
is a ruled surface over
B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Let
\psi:\,S\to S_{0}
be a contraction of the vertical exceptional curves to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle such that
C_{0}^{2}=C^{2}
;
namely we contract exceptional curve disjoint with
C
,
where
C_{0}=\psi(C)
is the image of
C
.
It implies that
\psi
induces an isomorphism around neighborhoods of
C
and
C_{0}
.
Hence there is exactly one singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\psi_{*}(\mathcal{F})
on
C_{0}
by (
8.31
).

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}}

By (
8.30
),

K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=\psi_{*}K_{\mathcal{F}}=M_{0}+C_{0}+Z_{v0},

(8.32)

where
M_{0}=(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1)F_{0}
with
F_{0}
being a general fiber of
f_{0}
,
and
Z_{v0}=\psi_{*}Z_{v}
is contained in fibers of
f_{0}
.
We claim first that
Z_{v0}\neq\emptyset
;

Since
f_{0}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle, we may write

Z_{v0}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}F_{i0},

(8.33)

where
t_{i}\geq 1
and
F_{i0}
’s are pairwise different fibers of
f_{0}
.
By construction, there is no non-reduced singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
C_{0}
,
i.e.,
\psi
consists of no blowing-up center at a point on
C_{0}
.
Hence
Z_{v0}\neq 0
according to
Lemma 4.6
;
indeed, if
Z_{v0}=0
, then
\mathcal{F}_{0}
has no non-reduced singularity by
Lemma 4.6
, and hence
\psi:\,S\cong S_{0}
since
\mathcal{F}
is assumed to be relatively minimal.
However, this is impossible, since
S\cong S_{0}
is a Hirzebruch surface, which
does not admit any fibration of genus
g\geq 2
; see also
Lemma 4.2
.
Conversely,

Claim 8.9
.

For any fiber
F_{i0}\subseteq Z_{v0}
, there exists at least one non-reduced singularity on
F_{i0}\setminus C_{0}
.

Proof.

Suppose that there is no non-reduced singularity on
F_{i0}\setminus C_{0}
.
Then all the possible singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
F_{i0}
is reduced.
This implies that
\psi
is an isomorphism in a neighborhood of
F_{i0}
,
since
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.
Hence
\psi^{-1}(F_{i0})\cong F_{i0}
is a fiber of
f
, which is movable.
On the other hand, it is also contained in the fixed part
Z_{v}
of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
, because the moving part of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is
M=\psi^{*}(M_{0})
.
This gives a contradiction.
∎

Based on the resolution of non-reduced singularities in
Section 4.2
,
we can resolve the possible singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
Z_{v0}
in the following two lemmas.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
F_{i0}
be a fiber of
f_{0}
contained in
Z_{v0}
as in (
8.33
),
p_{0}=C_{0}\cap F_{i0}
, and
e=-C_{0}^{2}
.
Suppose that
F_{i0}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant.
Then there exist a sequence blowing-ups centered over
F_{i0}

\pi=\sigma_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{n}:~S_{n}\overset{\sigma_{n}}{\longrightarrow}S_{n-1}\overset{\sigma_{n-1}}{\longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{\sigma_{2}}{\longrightarrow}S_{1}\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\longrightarrow}S_{0},

such that the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
on the fiber
\pi^{-1}(F_{i0})
are all non-degenerate and belong to one of the following (We denote by
T_{n}
the set of singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
over
\pi^{-1}(F_{i0})
other than
\pi^{-1}(p_{0})
, which are either non-reduced or contained in
\text{Supp}(N_{n})
, where
K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=P_{n}+N_{n}
is the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}
)).

(i)

There are two non-degenerate singularities
p_{1}
and
p_{2}

(other than
p_{0}
) on
F_{i0}
, i.e., there is no need to blow up.
In this case,

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{1})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{2})=\frac{1}{e}.

(8.34)

Moreover,
CS(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{1})\neq 1
and
CS(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{2})\neq 1
.
Hence

\sum_{p_{j}\in T_{0}}\beta_{p_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\geq-\frac{1}{2}.

(8.35)

(ii)

There is a unique singularity
p
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
other than
p_{0}
on
F_{i0}
,
and the inverse image
\pi^{-1}(p)
is two rational
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant curves as in
Lemma 4.12
(iii).
In particular,
n=2
, and using the same notations in
Lemma 4.12
,
it holds that

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})&\,=-\frac{1}{2},\\
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})&\,=-\frac{e}{2e-1},\\
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime\prime})&\,=\frac{1}{2(2e-1)}.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.36)

Hence

\displaystyle K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}^{2}+\sum_{q\in T_{2}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{2})

\displaystyle=K_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}^{2}+\beta_{q_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})+\beta_{q_{2}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})+\beta_{q_{2}^{\prime\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})

(8.37)

\displaystyle=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}-1+\frac{e^{2}-e+1}{e(2e-1)}.

In any case, it holds

K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}^{2}+\sum_{q\in T_{n}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}_{n})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}-1+\frac{e^{2}-e+1}{e(2e-1)}.

(8.38)

Proof.

Note that
K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\cdot F_{i0}=1
.
Hence
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}F_{i0}+\chi(F_{i0})=3
.
Moreover, since
p_{0}
is a reduced singularity by assumption.
It follows that either there are two other singularities
\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
F_{i0}

with

Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{2})=1,
