or there is only one other singularity
p
with
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p)=2
.

If it is the first case, the formula (
8.34
) follows from (
2.3
) and (
2.14
).
Indeed,
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},C_{0},p_{0})=-e
by (
2.3
),
where
e=-C_{0}^{2}\geq 2
.
Hence
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{0})=-\frac{1}{e}
by (
2.14
).
Using again (
2.3
), one obtains (
8.34
).
Finally, suppose for instance that
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{1})=1
.
Then
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{2})=\frac{1-e}{e}<0
, which implies that
p_{2}
is a reduced singularity in view of (
2.14
).
Let
\rho^{\prime}:\,S^{\prime}\to S_{0}
be the blowing-up centered at
p_{1}
,
and
\overline{F}_{i0}
be its strict transform.
Clearly the contraction
\psi
factorizes through
\rho^{\prime}
.

\psi:~S\overset{\rho}{\longrightarrow}S_{1}\overset{\rho^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}S_{0}.

Moreover, there are exactly two singularities of
(\rho^{\prime})^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
\overline{F}_{i0}
,
which are the inverse image of
p_{0}
and
p_{2}
.
Both singularities are reduced.
It follows that
\rho
is an isomorphism in a neighborhood of
\overline{F}_{i0}
.
We denote its inverse image in
S
still by
\overline{F}_{i0}
by abuse of notations.
According to the above arguments,

\overline{F}_{i0}
is smooth rational curve with
\overline{F}_{i0}^{2}=-1
and two reduced non-degenerate singularities.
Hence
\overline{F}_{i0}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-exceptional curve, cf.
[
Bru04
, § 5.1]
.
This contradicts the relative minimality of
\mathcal{F}
.
Note that the CS-indices of an algebraically integral foliation are just the eigenvalues of the corresponding singularities by
Lemma 2.8
together with
Lemma 4.7
.
Hence (
8.35
) follows from the definition of
\beta
-invariant (
2.5
) and (
8.34
) with restrictions that
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{1})\neq 1
and
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p_{2})\neq 1
.

We consider next the second case, i.e., there is only one other singularity
p
with
Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0},p)=2
.
The resolution of such a singularity
p
was presented in
Corollary 4.13
,
where
C=F_{i0}
in our case.
Except the case stated in (ii) of our lemma,
in all other cases,
\overline{F}_{i0}^{2}=F_{i0}^{2}-1=-1
and there are exactly two singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
on
\overline{F}_{i0}
which are reduced non-degenerate.
By a similar argument as above, one shows that this is impossible since
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal.
This proves that the inverse image
\pi^{-1}(p)
is two rational
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-invariant curves as in
Lemma 4.12
(iii) (equivalently the case when
n=2
in
Corollary 4.13
(ii)).
In particular,
n=2
.
It remains to prove (
8.36
) and (
8.37
).
By (
2.3
),

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{E}_{1},q_{2})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2.

Hence
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2})=-\frac{1}{2}
by
Lemma 2.8
.
Again by (
2.3
),

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},C_{0},p_{0})=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{0},C_{0},p_{0})=C_{0}^{2}=-e.

Hence
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{i0},p_{0})=-\frac{1}{e}
by
Lemma 2.8
.
Thus

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{i0},q_{2}^{\prime})=\overline{F}_{i0}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\overline{F}_{i0},p_{0})=-\frac{2e-1}{e}.

So
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime})=-\frac{e}{2e-1}
by
Lemma 2.8
.
And
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{2},E_{2},q_{2}^{\prime\prime})=\frac{1}{2(2e-1)}
again by (
2.3
).
Clearly both
q_{2}
and
q_{2}^{\prime}
lies on the support of the negative part, and
q_{2}^{\prime\prime}
is non-reduced.
Hence
T_{2}=\{q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime\prime}\}
.
Finally, (
8.37
) follows by a similar argument as proving (
8.35
) with the help of (
4.11
).
∎

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
F_{i0}
be a fiber of
f_{0}
contained in
Z_{v0}
as in (
8.33
) and
e=-C_{0}^{2}
.
Suppose that
F_{i0}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant.
Then there exist a sequence blowing-ups center over
F_{i0}

\pi=\sigma_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{n}:~S_{n}\overset{\sigma_{n}}{\longrightarrow}S_{n-1}\overset{\sigma_{n-1}}{\longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{\sigma_{2}}{\longrightarrow}S_{1}\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\longrightarrow}S_{0},

such that the singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
on
\pi^{-1}(F_{i0})
are all non-degenerate and belong to one of the following
(
\overline{F}_{i0}
is the strict transform of
F_{i0}
,
\overline{E}_{i}
is the strict transform of the
i
-th blowing-up (
E_{n}=\overline{E}_{n}
by abuse of notation), all the non-degenerate singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
on
\pi^{-1}(F_{i0})
are marked with dark dots in the figures of
\pi^{-1}(F_{i0})
, and
T_{n}
denotes the set of singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
over
\pi^{-1}(F_{i0})
which are either non-reduced or contained in
\text{Supp}(N_{n})
, where
K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=P_{n}+N_{n}
is the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}
).

(i)

There is exactly one singularity
q_{0}
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
F_{i0}
, which is already non-degenerate, i.e., there is no need to blow up.
In this case,
\beta_{q_{0}}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\geq-\frac{1}{2}
.

(ii)

\pi^{-1}(F_{i0})=\overline{F}_{i0}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\overline{E}_{j}
,
n\geq 2
, and

\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}^{2}=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}-(n-1),\\
&\sum_{q_{j}\in T_{n}}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})>(n-1)-t_{i}+\frac{t_{i}-1}{t_{i}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.39)

q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
\overline{F}_{i0}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{E}_{3}
\overline{E}_{2}
\overline{E}_{4}
\overline{E}_{n-1}
E_{n}=\overline{E}_{n}
q_{n-1}
q_{n-2}
q_{n}
q_{n+1}

(iii)

\pi^{-1}(F_{i0})=\overline{F}_{i0}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\overline{E}_{i}
,
n\geq 3
, and

\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}^{2}=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}-(n-2),\\
&\sum_{q_{j}\in T_{n}}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{3}=\frac{5}{6},&&\text{if~}n=3,\\[2.0pt]
&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{(2n-1)(n-1)}+\frac{n-3}{n-2},&&\text{if~}n\geq 4,\end{aligned}\right.\\
&t_{i}\geq n-2.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.40)

q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
\overline{F}_{i0}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{E}_{3}
\overline{E}_{2}
\overline{E}_{4}
\overline{E}_{n-2}
E_{n}=\overline{E}_{n}
\overline{E}_{n-1}
q_{n-2}
q_{n-3}
q_{n}
q_{n-1}

In any case, it holds

K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}^{2}+\sum_{q_{j}\in T_{n}}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})\geq K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}-t_{i}+\max\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{t_{i}-1}{t_{i}}\right\}.

(8.41)

Proof.

Since
F_{i0}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant,
it follows that

tang(\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{i0})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}F_{i0}+F_{i0}^{2}=1.

Hence there is a unique singularity
q_{0}
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
F_{i0}
, which must be non-reduced by
Claim 8.9
.
Let
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S_{0}
be the blowing-up centered at
q_{0}
.
We claim first that the exceptional curve
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant;
indeed, if it is not the case, then according to the proof of
Lemma 4.14
,

tang(\mathcal{F}_{1},\overline{F}_{i0})=K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\cdot\overline{F}_{i0}+\overline{F}_{i0}^{2}=(\sigma_{1}^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-E_{1})(\sigma_{1}^{*}F_{i0}-E_{1})+(\sigma_{1}^{*}F_{i0}-E_{1})^{2}=-1,

where we denote also by
\overline{F}_{i0}
the strict transform of
F_{i0}
in
S_{1}
by abuse of notation.
This gives a contradiction.
Hence
E_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant. Moreover, it follows from
Lemma 4.14
that
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1})=2
and that
the intersection
q=\overline{C}\cap E_{1}
is not a singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

Suppose that there are two points
q_{1},q_{2}
on
E_{1}
with

Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{1})=Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},q_{2})=1.

Then we are in case (i) by
Lemma 4.9
.
It follows that the singularity
q_{0}
is already a non-degenerate singularity of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
. This is case (i) in our lemma.
Moreover, in this case,
\lambda_{q_{0}}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\neq 1
;
otherwise, the exceptional curve
E_{1}
would be not
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant, which is impossible as argued above, where
\sigma_{1}:\,S_{1}\to S_{0}
is the blowing-up centered at
q_{0}
with
E_{1}
being the exceptional curve and
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Hence
\beta_{q_{0}}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\geq-\frac{1}{2}
by the definition of
\beta
-invariant in (
2.7
).

Suppose next that there is exactly one point
p_{1}
on
E_{1}
with
Z(\mathcal{F}_{1},E_{1},p_{1})=2
.
The resolution of such a singularity
p_{1}
is exhibited in
Corollary 4.13
.
We only remark that
E_{1}
is the curve
C
in
Corollary 4.13
.
The cases (i) and (ii) in
Corollary 4.13
are respectively the cases (ii) and (iii) in this lemma.
It remains to prove (
8.39
) and (
8.40
), and to prove that the case (iii) in
Corollary 4.13
can not happen.

We prove first (
8.39
).
According to the resolution exhibited in
Corollary 4.13
,

K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{j=2}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{j}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{j=2}^{n}(j-1)\overline{E}_{j},\qquad\pi^{*}(F_{i0})=\mathcal{E}_{1}+\overline{F}_{i0}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{E}_{j}+\overline{F}_{i0},

where
\mathcal{E}_{j}=\sum\limits_{s=j}^{n}\overline{E}_{s}
is the total inverse image of the exceptional curve of
\sigma_{j}
as in
Corollary 4.13
.
Remark again that the indices here different from that in
Corollary 4.13
,
because
E_{1}
here is the curve
C
in
Corollary 4.13
.
Thus the first equation in (
8.39
) follows immediately.
Moreover,
D:=\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{n-1}\overline{E}_{j}
is contained in the support of the negative part
N_{n}
since
D
is actually a Hirzebruch-Jung chain with
K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}\cdot\overline{E}_{1}=-1
and
K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}\cdot\overline{E}_{j}=0
for
2\leq j\leq n-1
.
In particular,
q_{j}\in T_{n}
for
1\leq j\leq n-1
.
Note also that the contraction
\psi
factorizes through
\pi
as

\psi:\,S\overset{\rho}{\longrightarrow}S_{n}\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}S_{0}.

Hence

\displaystyle\rho_{*}(M)+\rho_{*}(C)+\rho_{*}(Z_{v})=\rho_{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}})

(8.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=\pi^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}-\sum_{j=2}^{n}(j-1)\overline{E}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(M_{0})+\pi^{*}(C_{0})+\pi^{*}\Big(\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}t_{i^{\prime}}F_{i^{\prime}0}\Big)+\pi^{*}(t_{i}F_{i0})-\sum_{j=2}^{n}(j-1)\overline{E}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(M_{0})+\pi^{*}(C_{0})+\pi^{*}\Big(\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}t_{i^{\prime}}F_{i^{\prime}0}\Big)+t_{i}\overline{F}_{i0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(t_{i}-j+1)\overline{E}_{j}.

In particular,
t_{i}-j+1\geq 0
for any
1\leq j\leq n
, i.e.,
t_{i}\geq n-1
.
If both
q_{n}
and
q_{n+1}
are reduced, then

\sum_{q_{j}\in T_{n}}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=\frac{n-1}{n}>(n-1)-t_{i}+\frac{t_{i}-1}{t_{i}}.

We explain a little about the equality
\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n-1}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=\frac{n-1}{n}
above.
Since
\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{n-1}\overline{E}_{j}
is a chain of rational curves with
\overline{E}_{j}^{2}=-2
for any
1\leq j\leq n-1
.
Thus by (
2.3
) and (
2.14
),

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{2},q_{1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{1},q_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2
,
and hence

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{3},q_{2})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{2},q_{2})=\overline{E}_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{2},q_{1})=-\frac{3}{2}.

By induction, one proves that for any
2\leq j\leq n-1
,

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j+1},q_{j})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j},q_{j})=\overline{E}_{j}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j},q_{j-1})=-\frac{j+1}{j}.

Hence

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{j(j+1)}=\frac{n-1}{n}.

(8.43)

We assume next that at least one of
\{q_{n},q_{n+1}\}
is a non-reduced.
Then by a similar proof as
Claim 8.9
, one has
t_{i}-j+1>0
for any
1\leq j\leq n
according to (
8.42
).
In particular, taking
j=n
, we get
t_{i}\geq n
.
Note also that

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},E_{n},q_{n})+\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},E_{n},q_{n+1})=E_{n}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},E_{n},q_{n-1})=-1+\frac{n-1}{n}=-\frac{1}{n}.

Hence
\beta_{q_{n}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})+\beta_{q_{n+1}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})>-1
.
Together with (
8.43
) it follows that

\sum_{q_{j}\in T_{n}}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})\geq-1+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=-1+\frac{n-1}{n}\geq(n-1)-t_{i}+\frac{t_{i}-1}{t_{i}}.

This proves (
8.39
).

One can proves similarly (
8.40
) in case (iii),
and in fact easier than (
8.39
).
Indeed, the first equality in (
8.40
) follows directly from (
4.11
).
Moreover, by (
2.3
) and (
2.14
), if
n\geq 4
, then

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},E_{n},q_{n-1})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{n-1},q_{n-1})=\overline{E}_{n-1}^{2}=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j+1},q_{j})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j},q_{j})=\overline{E}_{j}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j},q_{j-1})=-\frac{j+1}{j},\quad\forall\,2\leq j\leq n-3,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},E_{n},q_{n-2})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{n-2},q_{n-2})=\overline{E}_{n-2}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{n-2},q_{n-3})=-\frac{2n-1}{n-1},\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},E_{n},q_{n})=E_{n}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},E_{n},q_{n-1})-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},E_{n},q_{n-2})=-\frac{1}{2(2n-1)}.\end{aligned}\right.

Moreover, similar as above, one shows that
D:=\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{n-2}\overline{E}_{j}
and
\overline{E}_{n-1}
are contained in the support of the negative part
N_{n}
.
In particular,
q_{j}\in T_{n}
for
1\leq j\leq n-1
.
Note that
q_{n}
is reduced, and hence
q_{n}\not\in T_{n}
.

\sum_{q_{j}\in T_{n}}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{(2n-1)(n-1)}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-3}\frac{1}{j(j+1)}=\frac{1}{(2n-1)(n-1)}+\frac{3n-8}{2(n-2)}.

If
n=3
, then

\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{1},q_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-3,\quad\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{2},q_{2})=\overline{E}_{2}^{2}=-2,\quad\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\overline{E}_{3},q_{3})=\overline{E}_{3}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{3}=-\frac{1}{6}.

Hence

\sum_{q_{j}\in T_{3}}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{3})=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\beta_{q_{j}}(\mathcal{F}_{3})=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{3}=\frac{5}{6}.

The inequality
t_{i}\geq n-2
can be proved using a similar argument as the proof of
t_{i}\geq n-1
in case (ii) above, cf. (
8.42
).

Finally, we prove that the case (iii) in
Corollary 4.13
can not happen.
This follows from the formulas (
2.3
) and (
2.14
).
In fact, if the case (iii) in
Corollary 4.13
happens,
then one obtains a chain of rational curves
\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{n-1}\overline{E}_{j}

with
\overline{E}_{j}^{2}=-2
for any
1\leq j\leq n-1
.

q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
\overline{F}_{i0}
\overline{E}_{1}
\overline{E}_{3}
\overline{E}_{2}
\overline{E}_{4}
\overline{E}_{n-1}
E_{n}=\overline{E}_{n}
q_{n-2}

By (
2.3
) and (
2.14
),

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{1},q_{1})=\overline{E}_{1}^{2}=-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j+1},q_{j})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j},q_{j})=\overline{E}_{j}^{2}-\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{j},q_{j-1})=-\frac{j+1}{j},\quad\forall\,2\leq j\leq n-2,\\
&\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{n-2},q_{n-2})^{-1}=\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{n-1},q_{n-2})=\overline{E}_{n-1}^{2}=-2.\end{aligned}\right.

This gives a contradiction by computing
the invariant
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{n-2},q_{n-2})
.
Indeed, taking
j=n-3
in the second equality above,
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{n-2},q_{n-2})=-\frac{n-2}{n-3}
;
while
\mathrm{CS}(\mathcal{F}_{n},\overline{E}_{n-2},q_{n-2})=-\frac{1}{2}
by the last equality above.
∎

We now come back to the proof of (
8.6
).
Let
\pi:\,S^{\prime}\to S_{0}
be a sequence of blowing-ups resolving
the singularities (including the infinitely close ones) over
\text{Supp}(Z_{v0})
, such that all the singularities of

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{0})
on
S^{\prime}
are non-degenerate.
Since there is exactly one singularity
p_{0}
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
C_{0}
,
it follows that there is at most one fiber
F_{i0}\subseteq\text{Supp}(Z_{v0})
which is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant.
Let
T
be the set of singularities of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
,
which are either non-reduced or on
\text{Supp}(N^{\prime})
,
where
N^{\prime}
is the negative part in the Zariski decomposition
K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=P^{\prime}+N^{\prime}
.
Then
\pi^{-1}(p_{0})\in T
by the construction with (
8.31
).
Moreover,

1=Z(\mathcal{F}_{0},C_{0})=2+K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\cdot C_{0}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1+C_{0}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i},\quad\Longrightarrow\quad e:=-C_{0}^{2}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}.

By
Proposition 2.3
, the eigenvalue
\lambda_{q_{0}}=-e
.
Hence
\beta_{\pi^{-1}(p_{0})}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})=\beta_{p_{0}}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=\frac{1}{e}
.
Note also that

K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}=(M_{0}+C_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}F_{i0})=\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1\big)-1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}=(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}.

We consider first the case when none of
F_{i0}\subseteq\text{Supp}(Z_{v0})
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant.
Then by (
5.6
) and (
8.41
),

\displaystyle\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq

\displaystyle\beta_{\pi^{-1}(p_{0})}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}+\sum_{q\in T\setminus\{\pi^{-1}(p_{0})\}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{e}+K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(-t_{i}+\max\Big\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{t_{i}-1}{t_{i}}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{e}+\big(p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-2\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\max\Big\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{t_{i}-1}{t_{i}}\Big\}\geq\left\{\begin{aligned} &p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1,&&\text{if~}k\geq 2\\
&p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+2},&&\text{if~}k=1.\end{aligned}\right.

We consider next the case when one of
F_{i0}\subseteq\text{Supp}(Z_{v0})
, saying
F_{10}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant, and the rest
F_{i0}
’s (might be empty) are not
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant.
Then by (
5.6
), (
8.38
) and (
8.41
),

\displaystyle\vol(\mathcal{F})\geq

\displaystyle\beta_{\pi^{-1}(p_{0})}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+K_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}+\sum_{q\in T\setminus\{\pi^{-1}(p_{0})\}}\beta_{q}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{e}+K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}^{2}-1+\frac{e^{2}-e+1}{e(2e-1)}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}\left(-t_{i}+\max\Big\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{t_{i}-1}{t_{i}}\Big\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+t_{1}-\frac{5}{2}+\frac{3}{2(2e-1)}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}\max\Big\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{t_{i}-1}{t_{i}}\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-1,&&\text{if~}k\geq 2\\
&p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{3}{2\big(2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1\big)},&&\text{if~}k=1.\end{aligned}\right.

If the equality holds, then
k=1
and
t_{1}=1
.
This completes the proof of (
8.6
).
∎

9.
Examples

In this section, we will construct several examples.

Example 9.1
,
Example 9.2
and
Example 9.4
show that the three Noether type inequalities in
Theorem 1.9
are all sharp;
while
Example 9.5
illustrates that the Chern numbers can not be bounded from above by the geometric genus
p_{g}(\mathcal{F})
.

Example 9.1
.

For any integer
n\geq 1
, we construct a sequence of reduced foliated surfaces
(S_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
of general type,
such that the volume
\vol(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n
and the geometric genus
p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n+2
,
and hence the following equality holds

\vol(\mathcal{F}_{n})=p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})-2.

(9.1)

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be a foliation of degree two on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with reduced singularities.
Such a foliation exists, cf.
[
LS20
, Proposition 3.2]
. In fact, any foliation of degree
d
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
can be generated by a vector of the form (
[
GO89
,
Bru04
]
)

v=\big(P(x,y)+xR(x,y)\big)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\big(Q(x,y)+yR(x,y)\big)\frac{\partial}{\partial y},

where
(x,y)
is an affine coordinate of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
P(x,y),Q(x,y)
are polynomials of degree
\leq d
,
and
R(x,y)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
d
(plus some nondegeneracy conditions).
Let

v_{\alpha,\gamma}=x\Big(-\alpha^{2}+x^{2}+y^{2}\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+y\Big(2\alpha\gamma+(2\alpha+\gamma)y+x^{2}+y^{2}\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial y},

(9.2)

where
\alpha,\gamma\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
are general.
The foliation
\mathcal{F}_{0}
defined by
v_{\alpha,\gamma}
admits
7
singularities:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &(0,0),\quad(0,-2\alpha),\quad(0,-\gamma),\quad(\alpha,0),\quad(-\alpha,0),\\
&\Big(~\frac{\alpha\sqrt{3(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}}{2\alpha+\gamma},~\frac{-\alpha(\alpha+2\gamma)}{2\alpha+\gamma}~\Big),\quad\Big(~\frac{-\alpha\sqrt{3(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}}{2\alpha+\gamma},~\frac{-\alpha(\alpha+2\gamma)}{2\alpha+\gamma}~\Big)\end{aligned}\right\}.

The eigenvalues at these
7
singularities are respectively equal to

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\lambda_{1}=\frac{-\alpha}{2\gamma},\quad\lambda_{2}=\frac{4\alpha-2\gamma}{3\alpha},\quad\lambda_{3}=\frac{\gamma(\gamma-2\alpha)}{\gamma^{2}-\alpha^{2}},\quad\lambda_{4}=\lambda_{5}=\frac{\alpha+2\gamma}{2\alpha},\\
&\lambda_{6}=\lambda_{7}=\frac{(\alpha-2\gamma)(2\alpha-\gamma)-\sqrt{(\alpha-2\gamma)^{2}(2\alpha-\gamma)^{2}-24\alpha(\alpha+2\gamma)(\gamma^{2}-\alpha^{2})\,}~}{(\alpha-2\gamma)(2\alpha-\gamma)+\sqrt{(\alpha-2\gamma)^{2}(2\alpha-\gamma)^{2}-24\alpha(\alpha+2\gamma)(\gamma^{2}-\alpha^{2})\,}~}\end{aligned}\right\}.

If
\alpha,\gamma
are sufficiently general (for instance if
\{\alpha,\gamma\}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
are algebraically independent over
\mathbb{Q}
), then the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
for
1\leq i\leq 7
, and hence these are all reduced singularities.
Moreover, the two lines
L_{0}:=\{x=0\}
and
L_{\infty}:=\{y=0\}
are both
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant in view of (
9.2
).
Let
\sigma:\,S_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the blowing-up centered at
(0,0)
,
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
be the induced foliation on
S_{1}
.
Then
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is still reduced, and

K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}=\sigma^{*}K_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=\sigma^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(1)\sim C_{0}+F,

where ’
\sim
’ stands for the linear equivalence,
C_{0}=\mathcal{E}\subseteq S_{1}
is the unique section (also the exceptional curve of
\sigma
) with
C_{0}^{2}=-1
,
and
F_{1}
is a general fiber of the induced ruling
f_{1}:\,S_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Hence

\vol(\mathcal{F}_{1})=\vol(\mathcal{F}_{0})=1,\qquad p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{1})=p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=3.

It follows that the foliations
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
(they are birational to each other) satisfy (
9.1
).
By construction,
S_{1}
is isomorphic to the Hirzebruch surface

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)\big)
.
Let
F_{0},F_{\infty}
be the strict transforms of the two lines
L_{0},L_{\infty}
respectively.
As both
L_{0}
and
L_{\infty}
are
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-invariant,
it follows that both
F_{0}
and
F_{\infty}
are
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant.
Let
\pi_{n}:\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the cyclic cover of degree
n\geq 2
branched over
f_{1}(F_{0})
and
f_{1}(F_{\infty})
, and
S_{n}=S_{1}\times_{\pi_{n}}\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the fiber product as follows.

\textstyle{S_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}}

It is clear that
S_{n}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(n)\big)
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
be the induced foliation on
S_{n}
.
Then
\mathcal{F}_{n}
is a reduced foliation since the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
at each of the
7
singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.
Since both
F_{0}
and
F_{\infty}
are
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-invariant,
one obtains that (cf.
[
Bru04
, § 2.3(4)]
),

K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=\Pi_{n}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})=\Pi_{n}^{*}(C_{0}+F_{1})=\Pi_{n}^{-1}(C_{0})+nF_{n},

where
F_{n}
is a general fiber of
f_{n}
, and
\Pi_{n}^{-1}(C_{0})
is the strict transform of
C_{0}
in
S_{n}
satisfying
\Pi_{n}^{-1}(C_{0})^{2}=-n
.
Hence

\vol(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n,\qquad p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n+2.

Therefore, the equality (
9.1
) holds for the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{n}
.
This completes the construction.

Example 9.2
.

For any integer
n\geq 2
, we construct a sequence of reduced foliated surfaces
(S_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
of general type,
such that the volume
\vol(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n-2+\frac{1}{n}
and the geometric genus
p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n
, and hence the following equality holds

\vol(\mathcal{F}_{n})=p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})-2+\frac{1}{p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})}.

(9.3)

Let
n\geq 2
and
S_{n}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(n)\big)

be the Hirzebruch surface admitting a unique section
C_{0}
with
C_{0}^{2}=-n<0
.
Let
f:\,S_{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the geometrical ruling on
S_{n}
, which makes
S_{n}
as a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Note that any
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over an affine space is necessarily trivial.
One can obtain the Hirzebruch surface
S_{n}
by gluing the two trivial
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
by

\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},

\qquad\big(x,\,[Y_{0},Y_{1}]\big)\quad\mapsto\quad\left(\frac{1}{x},\,[Y_{0},x^{n}Y_{1}]\right).

Equivalently, one may obtain
S_{n}
by gluing four affine spaces as follows.
Let
(x_{i},y_{i})
be the affine coordinate on
U_{i}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}=\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
for
1\leq i\leq 4
.
The transition functions on their overlaps are given by

\left(x_{1},\,y_{1}\right)=\left(x_{2},\,\frac{1}{y_{2}}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{x_{3}},\,x_{3}^{n}y_{3}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{x_{4}},\,\frac{x_{4}^{n}}{y_{4}}\right).

Moreover,
C_{0}\cap U_{1}=\{y_{1}=0\}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
be the foliation on
S_{n}
defined by
\big\{(U_{i},v_{i})\big\}_{i=1}^{4}
, where

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{1}=h(x_{1},y_{1})\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+y_{1}^{2}g(x_{1},y_{1})\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}},\\
&v_{2}=y_{2}h(x_{2},1/y_{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}-y_{2}g(x_{2},1/y_{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{2}},\\
&v_{3}=-x_{3}h(1/x_{3},x_{3}^{n}y_{3})\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{3}}+\Big(ny_{3}h(1/x_{3},x_{3}^{n}y_{3})+x_{3}^{n-1}y_{3}^{2}g(1/x_{3},x_{3}^{n}y_{3})\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{3}},\\
&v_{4}=-x_{4}y_{4}h(1/x_{4},x_{4}^{n}/y_{4})\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{4}}-\Big(ny_{4}^{2}h(1/x_{4},x_{4}^{n}/y_{4})+x_{4}^{n-1}y_{4}g(1/x_{4},x_{4}^{n}/y_{4})\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{4}},\end{aligned}\right.

where

h(x_{1},y_{1})=a_{0}+y_{1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{a}_{i}x_{1}^{i};\qquad g(x_{1},y_{1})=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}b_{j}x_{1}^{j}+y_{1}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\tilde{b}_{j}x_{1}^{j}.

Here
a_{i},\tilde{a}_{i},b_{j},\tilde{b}_{j}
are some complex numbers satisfying certain non-degeneracy conditions to insure these
v_{i}
’s contain no one-dimensional zeros.
Then one checks easily by (
2.1
) that

K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=C_{0}+(n-1)F,

where
F
is a general fiber of the ruling
f:\,S_{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
It follows that the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}
is the following.

K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=P+N,

where
P=(n-1)F+\frac{n-1}{n}C_{0}
is the nef part,
and
N=\frac{1}{n}C_{0}
is the negative part.
Hence

\vol(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n-2+\frac{1}{n},\qquad p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n,

from which the equality (
9.3
) follows immediately.
To complete the construction, we should ensure that the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{n}
is reduced.
A sufficient condition to ensure a singularity
p\in U_{i}
of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
to be reduced is that
both of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of
(Dv_{i})(p)
are non-zero and the quotient
\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}
is not a positive rational number.
This should be satisfied for a general choice of these complex numbers
\{a_{i},\tilde{a}_{i},b_{j},\tilde{b}_{j}\}
, similar to the situation on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
,

[
LS20
, Proposition 3.2]
.
For instance, one can take

h(x_{1},y_{1})=1+y_{1}x_{1}^{n},\qquad\qquad g(x_{1},y_{1})=\alpha x_{1}^{n-1}+y_{1},\quad\text{where~}\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Q}.

The number of singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
is

\#Sing(\mathcal{F})=2n+2.

There are
2n-1
singularities in
U_{1}
:

Sing(\mathcal{F})\cap U_{1}=\{p_{i}=(\xi_{i},-\alpha\xi_{i}^{n-1}),\,~i=1,\cdots,2n-1\},\qquad\text{where~}\xi_{i}^{2n-1}=\frac{1}{\alpha}.

By direct computation, the two eigenvalues of
(Dv_{1})(p_{i})
are

\frac{(\alpha-n)\pm\sqrt{(\alpha+n)^{2}+4(n-1)\alpha^{2}\xi_{i}^{2n-2}\,}\,}{2\xi_{i}}.

Hence these
p_{i}
’s are reduced singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
.
The rest three singularities of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
are all on the fiber

F_{\infty}=\{x_{3}=0\}\cup\{x_{4}=0\}.

To be explicit, the rest three singularities are

\Big\{(0,0),\Big(0,\frac{-n}{n+\alpha}\Big)\Big\}\subseteq U_{3},\quad\text{~and~}\quad\big\{(0,0)\big\}\subseteq U_{4}.

The eigenvalues of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
at these three singularities are respectively
equal to
-n
,
\frac{n(n+\alpha)}{\alpha}
and
n+\alpha
.
Hence the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{n}
is reduced as required.

Remark 9.3
.

By (
1.8
), the foliations in the above example are all transcendental.
Moreover, it holds that
|K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}|=|(n-1)F|+C_{0}
.
Hence the moving part of
|K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}|
is
M=(n-1)F
and the positive part in its Zariski decomposition is
P=(n-1)F+\frac{n-1}{n}C_{0}
. It follows that

\vol(\mathcal{F})=n-2+\frac{1}{n}=P\cdot M<K_{\mathcal{F}}\cdot M.

Example 9.4
.

For any integer
g\geq 2
, we construct an algebraically integral foliated surfaces
(S,\mathcal{F})
of general type which is induced by a fibration of genus
g
,
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} p_{g}(\mathcal{F})&\,=g,\\
\vol(\mathcal{F})&\,=\frac{2g(g-1)}{2g+1}=p_{g}(\mathcal{F})-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{3}{2\big(2p_{g}(\mathcal{F})+1\big)}.\end{aligned}\right.

(9.4)

Let
g\geq 2
and
D_{0}=\{x\big(1-y^{2g}\big)+y^{2g+1}=0\}\subseteq Y_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
where
x,y
are respectively the affine coordinates of the first and the second factor
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of
Y_{0}
.
Let
pr_{1}:\,Y_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the projection to the first factor.
Then the restricted map

pr_{1}\big|_{D_{0}}:~D_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

has
2g+1
ramified points:
p_{0}=(0,0)
and
p_{i}=\Big(\frac{(2g+1)\xi_{i}}{2g},\xi_{i}\Big)
, where
\xi_{i}
’s are mutually different and
\xi_{i}^{2g}=2g+1
for
1\leq i\leq 2g
.
Moreover, the ramification indices are respectively
r_{0}=2g
and
r_{i}=1
for
1\leq i\leq 2g
.
Let
\Gamma_{0}=\{x=0\}
and
C_{0}=\{y=0\}
.
Then
R_{0}=D_{0}+\Gamma_{0}+C_{0}
is two-divisible.
Let
\pi_{0}:\,S_{0}\to Y
be the double cover ramified exactly over
R_{0}
,
and
f_{0}=pr_{1}\circ\pi_{0}:\,S\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Let
\rho:\,S\to S_{0}
be the desingularization and
f=f_{0}\circ\rho
.

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{pr_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}}

Since
R_{0}
has a unique singularity
p_{0}=(0,0)
,

S_{0}
is singular exactly over
p_{0}
.
Such a singularity can be resolved canonically as follows, cf.
[
BHPV04
, § V.22]
.

\Gamma_{0}
p_{0}
C_{0}
D_{0}
\sigma_{1}
\big(1,-(2g+1)\big)
\overline{\Gamma}_{0}
\big((2g+1),-1\big)
\overline{D}_{0}
\big(2g,-2\big)
\big(2,-2\big)~
~\big(1,-2\big)
\overline{C}_{0}
\sigma_{2}
\big(1,-(2g+2)\big)
\overline{\Gamma}_{0}
\big((2g+2),-1\big)
\big((2g+1),-4\big)
\big((4g+1),-1\big)
\big((2g+1),-1\big)
\overline{D}_{0}
\big(2,-4\big)~
\big(3,-1\big)~
~\big(1,-4\big)
\big(1,-1\big)
\overline{C}_{0}
\pi
\big(2,-(g+1)\big)
\overline{\Gamma}_{0}
\big((2g+2),-2\big)
E_{4g+3}
\big((4g+2),-2\big)
\quad\qquad E_{4g+2}
\big((4g+1),-2\big)
E_{4g+1}
\big((2g+1),-2\big)
E_{4g+4}
\big(4,-2\big)~
E_{4}
\big(3,-2\big)~
E_{3}
~\big(2,-2\big)
E_{2}
\big(1,-2\big)
E_{1}

In the above,
\sigma_{1}:\,Y_{1}\to Y_{0}
is the resolution of the ramification divisor
R_{0}
into normal crossing one
R_{1}
, and
\sigma_{2}:\,Y\to Y_{1}
is the further resolution to the smooth one
R_{2}
.
Except the ramification divisor
R_{0}
, we draw the rest ones using the dual graphs.
The number
(a,-b)
over a marked point stands that the corresponding curve
C
is of multiplicity
a
in the fibers and
C^{2}=-b
.
A solid point means that the corresponding curve is ramified; while a hollow point means the corresponding curve is not ramified.
Note that the local intersection
I_{p_{0}}(\Gamma_{0},D_{0})=2g+1
, there are
2g+1
blowing-ups contained in
\sigma_{1}
.
By abuse of notations, we always use
\overline{\Gamma}_{0}
(res.
\overline{C}_{0}
and
\overline{D}_{0}
)
to denote the strict transform of
\Gamma_{0}
(resp.
C_{0}
and
D_{0}
).
Remark that the curves
\overline{C}_{0}
and
\overline{D}_{0}
are not contained in the fibers.
Moreover, in the last step, we only draw the curves contained in the fiber
F_{q}:=f^{-1}(q)
, where
q=pr_{1}(p_{0})\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1}}
\textstyle{S_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{2}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}

Let
\Pi=\pi_{0}\circ\rho=\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}\circ\pi
.
By the formulas of double covers (cf.
[
BHPV04
, § V.22]
),

K_{S/\mathbb{P}^{1}}=\Pi^{*}\Big(K_{Y_{0}/\mathbb{P}^{1}}+\frac{R_{0}}{2}\Big)=\Pi^{*}\big(\Gamma_{0}+(g-1)C_{0}\big).

By construction,
f
is relatively minimal,
and all the fibers of
f
are normal crossing (in fact, except the fiber
F_{q}=f^{-1}(q)
, all the other fibers of
f
are semi-stable).
Let
\mathcal{F}
be the induced foliation. Then
\mathcal{F}
is relatively minimal, and by (
4.2
),

K_{\mathcal{F}}=K_{S/\mathbb{P}^{1}}-\big(F_{q}-F_{q,red}\big)=\Pi^{*}\big((g-1)C_{0}\big)+F_{q,red},

where
F_{q,red}
is the reduced part of
F_{q}=\Pi^{*}(\Gamma_{0})
.
Thus the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
is
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N
, where

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=\Pi^{*}\big((g-1)C_{0}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{4g+1}\frac{iE_{i}}{4g+2}+E_{4g+2}+\frac{(2g-1)\overline{\Gamma}_{0}}{2g+1}+\frac{2gE_{4g+3}}{2g+1}+\frac{E_{4g+4}}{2},

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{4g+1}\frac{(4g+2-i)E_{i}}{4g+2}+\frac{2\,\overline{\Gamma}_{0}}{2g+1}+\frac{E_{4g+3}}{2g+1}+\frac{E_{4g+4}}{2}.

Indeed, one checks directly that

\text{Supp}(N)=F_{q,red}-E_{4g+2}
, and hence the intersection matrix of
\text{Supp}(N)
is negative definite.
Moreover,

P\cdot\Pi^{*}(C_{0})=\frac{(2g-1)\overline{\Gamma}_{0}}{2g+1}\cdot\Pi^{*}(C_{0})=\frac{2g-1}{2g+1};\qquad P\cdot E_{4g+2}=\frac{g-1}{2g+1};\qquad P\cdot E_{i}=0,\quad\forall\,i\neq 4g+2.

Hence
K_{\mathcal{F}}=P+N
is the Zariski decomposition of
K_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Therefore,

\vol(\mathcal{F})=P^{2}=P\cdot\Big(\Pi^{*}\big((g-1)C_{0}\big)+E_{4g+2}\Big)=\frac{(2g-1)(g-1)}{2g+1}+\frac{g-1}{2g+1}=\frac{2g(g-1)}{2g+1}.

(9.5)

In particular,
\mathcal{F}
is of general type.
Note that any irreducible curve in the support of
N
must be contained in the fixed part of
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|
,
i.e.,
\text{Supp}(N)=F_{q,red}-E_{4g+2}
is contained in the fixed part of
|K_{\mathcal{F}}|
.
Hence

\displaystyle|K_{\mathcal{F}}|

\displaystyle=\Big|K_{\mathcal{F}}-\big(F_{q,red}-E_{4g+2}\big)\Big|+\big(F_{q,red}-E_{4g+2}\big)

\displaystyle=\Big|\Pi^{*}\big((g-1)C_{0}\big)+E_{4g+2}\Big|+\big(F_{q,red}-E_{4g+2}\big)

\displaystyle=\Big|\Pi^{*}\big((g-1)C_{0}\big)\Big|+E_{4g+2}+\big(F_{q,red}-E_{4g+2}\big)=\Big|\Pi^{*}\big((g-1)C_{0}\big)\Big|+F_{q,red}.

In particular,

p_{g}(\mathcal{F})=h^{0}(S,K_{\mathcal{F}})=h^{0}\Big(S,\Pi^{*}\big((g-1)C_{0}\big)\Big)=h^{0}\big(Y,(g-1)C_{0}\big)=g.

Combining this with (
9.5
),
we obtain a foliation
\mathcal{F}
satisfying (
9.4
) as required.

Example 9.5
.

In this example, we construct a sequence of foliated surfaces
(S_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
such that their geometric genera are fixed, while the three Chern numbers
\chi(\mathcal{F}_{n})
,
c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{n})
and
c_{2}(\mathcal{F}_{n})
go to the infinity when
n
goes to the infinity.

Let
f_{1}:\,S_{1}\to B_{1}
be a semi-stable fibration of genus
g\geq 2
with

K_{f_{1}}=K_{S_{1}/B_{1}}^{2}>0,\qquad\chi_{f}=\deg\big((f_{1})_{*}\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(K_{S_{1}/B_{1}})\big)>0,\qquad e_{f_{1}}>0.

Such a fibration clearly exists, cf.
[
CH88
,
Xia87
]
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}
be the induced foliation. Then by (
1.2
),

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{1})=\kappa(f_{1})=K_{f_{1}}^{2},\qquad\chi(\mathcal{F}_{1})=\lambda(f_{1})=\chi_{f_{1}},\qquad c_{2}(\mathcal{F}_{1})=\delta(f_{1})=e_{f_{1}}.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in\text{{\rm Pic\,}}(B_{1})
be a sufficiently ample divisor such that

\dim H^{0}\big(S_{1},K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\otimes f_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{-1})\big)=\dim H^{0}\big(B_{1},(f_{1})_{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})\big)\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-1}\big)=0.

Clearly such an ample divisor exists.
Moreover, for such a divisor
\mathcal{L}
and any integer
k\geq 1
,

\dim H^{0}\big(S_{1},K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\otimes f_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{-k})\big)=\dim H^{0}\big(B_{1},(f_{1})_{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})\big)\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-k}\big)=0.

For any number
n\geq 2
, let
D_{n}\in\big|\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}\big|
be a general element, such that

D_{n}
is reduced and that any fiber over
D_{n}
is smooth.
Then there is a cyclic cover
\pi_{n}:\,B_{n}\to B_{1}
defined by the relation
D_{n}\sim\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}
. Let
f_{n}:\,S_{n}\to B_{n}
be the fiber-product.

\textstyle{S_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{B_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\textstyle{B_{1}}

By construction,
f_{n}
is semi-stable and
\Pi
is a cyclic cover defined by the relation

\Lambda_{n}=f_{1}^{-1}(D_{n})\sim f_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{L})^{\otimes n}.

Hence
(\Pi_{n})_{*}\mathcal{O}_{S_{n}}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{L}^{-i}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
be the induced foliation on
S_{n}
.
Then

c_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=nc_{1}^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{1}),\qquad\chi(\mathcal{F}_{n})=n\chi(\mathcal{F}_{1}),\qquad c_{2}(\mathcal{F}_{n})=nc_{2}(\mathcal{F}_{1}).

Moreover,
K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}=K_{S_{n}/B_{n}}=\Pi_{n}^{*}(K_{S_{1}/B_{1}})=\Pi_{n}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})
, and hence

\displaystyle p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{n})

\displaystyle=\dim H^{0}\big(S_{n},K_{\mathcal{F}_{n}}\big)=\dim H^{0}\big(S_{n},\Pi_{n}^{*}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})\big)

\displaystyle=\dim H^{0}\big(S_{1},\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})\otimes(\Pi_{n})_{*}\mathcal{O}_{S_{n}}\big)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\dim H^{0}\big(S_{1},\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-i}\big)

\displaystyle=\dim H^{0}\big(S_{1},\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}(K_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})\big)=p_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{1}).

This completes the construction.
